PREFACE
In an essay entitled "Wittgenstein's Remarks on the Foundation
of Mathematics", in the British Journal for the Philosophy of
Science, IX, No. 34 (August, 1958), G. Kreisel reviewed the book
which I shall examine in this essay. He closed this review by
commenting: "now it seems to me to be a surprisingly insignificant
product of a sparkling mind". Although one can sympathize with
this comment, it is not entirely accurate. It is not surprising that
the book as a whole seems insignificant because it is a posthumous
selection of remarks from his note-books. These remarks had
been written over a period of at least seven years, 1937 to 1944.
In the text they are organized according to their date of com-
position, rather than according to a connecting theme, though I
must confess that in my essay I treat his work as a treatise. Fur-
thermore, many of the remarks are very obscure. They are like
the expressions of an insight which one jots down in a note-book
and then needs several pages to unpack it. Unfortunately, Witt-
genstein did not unpack his insights. I submit that my theory of
strict finitism which is presented in Chapters II and III is an
unpacking of several of his remarks, and of those remarks which
express his deepest insight on mathematics. There is no doubt
that if one thought that the only significant remarks on the foun-
dations of mathematics occurred in mathematical journals, such
as the Journal of Symbolic Logic, then Wittgenstein's Remarks
would seem surprisingly insignificant. There is no discussion of
the technical problems of axiom systems except to say that they
are philosophically unimportant. Only an elementary knowledge
of symbolic logic is required to read his attacks on formal proofs


4
Preface
in mathematics and the attempt to show that mathematics is logic
in Principia Mathematica. Although Wittgenstein's Remarks on
the Foundations of Mathematics is insignificant as a mathematical
or metamathematical treatise, it is not fair to expect it to be one.
A brief glance through it immediately shows that it is not at all
like the technical articles on that branch of mathematics called
"the foundations of mathematics". I hope that my essay is no
more technical than Wittgenstein's Remarks.
However, many of Wittgenstein's remarks have surprising phi-
losophical significance. In my opinion the most significant points
are the following three. Wittgenstein accounts for the necessary
truth of simple geometry and the arithmetic of small numbers
without any trace of platonism; this is what I call his strict finitism.
He finds the proper place for self-evidence; in other words, he
recognizes the importance of not being able to conceive of the
denial of a proposition without using inconceivability of the denial
as a criterion for or definition of necessary truth. Thirdly, he
shows, by non-mathematical means, the mathematical and phil-
osophic irrelevance of attempts such as Principia Mathematica,
to reduce mathematics to logic. I do believe that these Remarks
contain a conclusive refutation of Logicism.
This essay discusses the same topic as my dissertation: An
Examination of Ludwig Wittgenstein's Remarks on the Founda-
tions of Mathematics, viz., Wittgenstein's Remarks. The only
similarity between the two, however, lies in the presentation of
strict finitism. In my dissertation, I tried to uncover an implicit
metaphysics in Wittgenstein's strict finitistic philosophy of mathe-
matics and then tried to refute Wittgenstein by refuting this meta-
physics which I attributed to him. In this essay my aim is not to
criticize Wittgenstein. My aim has been primarily to develop some
lines of thought suggested in Wittgenstein's Remarks in hope that
such developments may make reading these remarks of Wittgen-
stein's easier and more profitable.
Throughout my essay I abbreviate Wittgenstein's Remarks on
the Foundation of Mathematics as RFM. RFM is broken up into
five books: I, II, III, IV, and V. Each book is divided into re-
marks numbered by arabic numerals. Thus I shall refer to specific


Preface
5
passages, such as remark 22 in Book II by citing book number
and remark number, eg., 11-22.1 abbreviate Wittgenstein's Philo-
sophical Investigations as PI. Most of the papers on RFM which
I consider are reprinted in: Philosophy of Mathematics, Selected
Readings, edited by Paul Benacerraf and Hilary Putnam (Prentice
Hall, 1964). I abbreviate this book of papers as BP. I hope that
the absence of footnotes facilitates reading.

I
PHILOSOPHICAL PROBLEMS AT THE FOUNDATIONS
OF MATHEMATICS
I have not yet made the role of miscalculating clear.
The role of the proposition: "I must have miscalcu-
lated". It is really the key to an understanding of the
'foundations' of mathematics. (11-90)
1. It is not obvious that the role of an admission of miscalculating
is the key to an understanding of the foundations of mathematics.
What is the role of a proposition? What is meant by "foundations
of mathematics"? How could an understanding of such an ap-
parently minute point, namely an admission of miscalculation, be
so helpful in such an apparently large topic, namely the founda-
tions of mathematics? Yet, Wittgenstein is basically correct. To
show that he is correct, I shall first describe what I believe Witt-
genstein meant in this passage by "foundations of mathematics".
Then, I shall explain how understanding something which you
could call the role of "I must have miscalculated" provides an-
swers to some of the crucial questions about the foundations of
mathematics. However, my main goal in this chapter is not to
explain this passage about miscalculating. Rather, I hope, while
explaining this passage about miscalculating, to reach the main
goal of this chapter - to explain what I consider to be an in-
vestigation into the foundations of mathematics.
In this essay, by "foundations of mathematics" I mean, and I
believe that in RFM Wittgenstein meant, philosophic foundations
of mathematics as opposed to mathematical foundations of math-
ematics. In IV-52 Wittgenstein wrote:


14
Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
The philosopher must twist and turn about so as to pass by the mathe-
matical problems, and not run up against one, - which would have to
be solved before he could go further.
His labour in philosophy is as it were an idleness in mathematics.
In V-13 he wrote:
The mathematical problems of what is called foundations are no more
the foundations of mathematics for us than the painted rock is the
support of a painted tower.
But what is the philosophy of mathematics or an investigation
into the philosophic foundations of mathematics? And what, if
anything, are mathematical foundations of mathematics? In my
opinion, someone is investigating the philosophic foundations of
mathematics when he tries to answer one or more of the following
questions. (I have obtained the idea of the philosophy of mathe-
matics being based on questions like these from the Introduction
of Stephen Korner's The Philosophy of Mathematics (London,
1960).
Group I: Questions on the ontology of non-applied mathematics.
(i) When we do non-applied mathematics are we talking about
some objects?
(ii) When we do non-applied mathematics are we talking about
non-ordinary objects?
(iii) If we are talking about non-ordinary objects when we do
non-applied mathematics, what are they like and how do we
get knowledge of them?
Group II: Questions on the epistemology of non-applied mathe-
matics.
(i) Are there truths in non-applied mathematics?
(ii) If there are truths in non-applied mathematics, are they nec-
essary truths?
(iii) If the truths of non-applied mathematics are necessary
truths, what does it mean to say that they are necessarily
true in addition to their being merely true and how do we
come to know that they must be true?
Group III: Questions designed to check whether or not answers
to the preceding questions on the ontology and


Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
15
epistemology of non-applied mathematics enable the
philosopher to account for applied mathematics.
(i) Does a bit of non-applied mathematics become applied
mathematics by making it be about objects if it were about
no objects as non-applied mathematics? Or, if it were about
objects when non-applied, does it become applied by making
it be about a different kind of object? If non-applied mathe-
matics is about the same objects as applied mathematics,
what is the difference between applied and non-applied
mathematics?
(ii) Does a proposition of non-applied mathematics become ap-
plied by making it be a truth about something if it were not
true when non-applied? Are propositions of applied mathe-
matics necessarily true? If propositions of applied mathe-
matics and non-applied mathematics are both necessarily
true, what differentiates applied from non-applied mathe-
matics?
2. Before I state what I mean by an investigation into the
mathematical foundations of mathematics, I shall comment ex-
tensively on these questions leading to a philosophy of mathe-
matics. What is meant by "applied" and "non-applied"? I cannot
now say precisely what is the difference between non-applied and
applied mathematics. To state precisely the difference between
non-applied and applied mathematics is, as is clear from Group
III, one of the problems of the philosophy of mathematics. How-
ever, the following distinction will suffice for our present purpose
of illustrating these groups of questions. A person has done ap-
plied mathematics if he makes or accepts a non-mathematical
statement and he has obtained it by doing mathematics. The
mathematics that he used to conclude his non-mathematical pro-
position is applied mathematics.
For instance, a person may say "This box of soap weighs three
pounds." The statement that a box of soap weighs three pounds
is not a mathematical statement. Assume that we ask this person
how he arrived at this conclusion because we know that he did
not weigh the box. If he answers that he figured it out because
he knew that this box and a smaller one, which weighs one third


16
Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
what it weighs, together weigh four pounds, he has done applied
mathematics.
Of course, when doing his applied mathematics, the person
gives mathematical expression to facts about his environment. In
our example, the man would have expressed the relevant facts in
the system of equations: bx + b2 = 4 and b2 = bt/3. In this sys-
tem bx stands for the weight of big box and b2 for the weight of
the smaller box. Similarly, conclusions about the pressures of
gases, accelerations of objects towards one another, and the
amount of interest earned in four months are drawn by having a
mathematical expression for some facts of nature and then com-
puting the non-mathematical conclusion from them. (I include
statements of initial conditions such as T = 17°C as mathematical
expressions of natural facts.) Such conclusions about pressures,
etc., we would normally say are obtained as a result of doing
applied mathematics. According to my characterization of applied
mathematics, the drawing of such conclusions would be typical
applications of mathematics. So I take my characterization of ap-
plied mathematics as justified. Note, however, that I do not con-
sider giving mathematical expression to natural facts applied
mathematics, although it may be called applying concepts used
in mathematics. In this I also agree with common sense because
to state laws of nature mathematically is not considered to be a
function of mathematics; it is considered to be a function of
natural science.
An advantage of characterizing applied mathematics as the
mathematics that a person uses to arrive at a non-mathematical
conclusion is that I do not beg the philosophic question of whether
the subject matter and modality of mathematical propositions are
the same or different in applied and non-applied mathematics.
(By modality of a proposition I mean whether it is necessarily
true (or false) or contingently true (or false). Very likely the
person in our example concluded that the big box weighed three
pounds by solving the system of equations: bx + b2 = 4 and
b2 = bj/3. His solving of this system was doing applied mathe-
matics. However, in solving this system we do not know whether
or not he has done the same as he would have done if he were


Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
17
solving this system as a problem in non-applied mathematics. For
instance, when he solved this system he may have been doing the
same as he did as a student in an algebra class or he may have
done something quite different.
3. A person has done non-applied mathematics if he has done
mathematics but has not done it for the purpose of drawing a
non-mathematical conclusion. By doing mathematics I have in
mind proving a theorem of mathematics or computing the value of
a function. Since computing the value of a function, eg., computing
that the cube root of 27 is 3, can be regarded as proving a low
level theorem, eg., 3]/27 = 3; I would say that doing non-applied
mathematics is proving mathematical theorems. What, then, is
mathematics? Mathematics is what is considered to be mathe-
matics by university departments of mathematics and editors of
mathematical journals.
4. My characterization of mathematics requires comment on
two points. The first is that I admit that what we ordinarily call
computing or calculating can be non-applied (pure) mathematics
as much as proving the fundamental theorem of arithmetic is
doing pure mathematics. (Establishing that 15 X 13 = 195 is a
typical example of what we ordinarily call computing or calcu-
lating.) In this I agree with Wittgenstein. The opening quotation
of this chapter hints that he regarded calculating as an important
part of non-applied mathematics. Also, a reading of RFM will
show that he is trying to gain a philosophic understanding of
mathematics, to answer the three groups of questions listed above,
primarily by asking these questions about that level of mathe-
matics we call calculating. Indeed in his "Mathematics and the
'Language Game'" (BP 481-490), Alan R. Anderson went so far
as to criticize Wittgenstein for identifying mathematics with cal-
culating procedures. But Wittgenstein did not make the factual
error of identifying mathematics with calculating procedures. For
instance, in IV-46 he admitted: "Mathematics' is not a sharply
delimited concept." Thus he would not believe that he could
identify mathematics, which he cannot clearly identify, with cal-
culating. Also in 11-46 he explicitly claimed: "mathematics is a
MOTLEY of techniques of proof'.


18
Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
5. If Wittgenstein made any kind of error of identifying mathe-
matics with calculating, it was the error of holding that a phil-
osophic understanding of calculating both applied and non-
applied, gives a philosophic understanding of mathematics on any
level. I do believe that Wittgenstein thought that a philosophic
understanding of calculating would provide a philosophic under-
standing of all of mathematics. To support this, I again refer to
the opening quotation and his practice in RFM. I would also call
attention to IV-16 in which he wrote:
Strangely, it can be said that there is so to speak a solid core to all
these glistening concept-formations. And I should like to say that that
is what makes them into mathematical productions.
In our development of his philosophy, we shall see that Wittgen-
stein regards mathematics as a way of forming concepts. How-
ever, an important point to make here is that the assertion of
there being this solid core should not be read as contradicting the
claim that there is a motley of mathematical techniques of proof.
It should, I submit, be taken as a suggestion that there is one
facet of all mathematical thinking such that, if properly under-
stood in any one of the manifold branches of mathematics, this
understanding would provide a philosophic understanding of all
other branches of mathematics. Now it is a philosophic error to
hold that a philosophic understanding of calculating suffices for
a philosophic understanding of mathematics only if there is no
such solid core. So whether or not Wittgenstein made a philo-
sophic error by focusing his attention primarily on calculating
can be judged only after we have his philosophic examination of
calculating.
6. The second point of comment on my characterization of
non-applied mathematics is the loose characterization of mathe-
matics as that which mathematicians call mathematics. Such a
characterization is certainly compatible with Wittgenstein's ad-
mission that "mathematics" is a vague, - not sharply delimited -,
concept. I interpret "vague" or "not sharply delimited "as meaning
that there are no necessary conditions for applying "mathematics".


Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
19
Also in V-26 Wittgenstein wrote:
Mathematics is, then, a family; but that is not to say that we shall not
mind what is incorporated into it.
This use of "family" indicates that Wittgenstein held that "mathe-
matics" is a family concept in the way in which he showed that
the concept "game" is a family concept in 65-77 of his Philo-
sophical Investigations. I would say that a concept, C, is a family
concept if we cannot state any one feature necessary for every-
thing that we call a C to be a C but yet there are limits on what
can be called a C.
7. But is not an admission that "mathematics" is a family
concept incompatible with the claim that there is a solid core to
mathematics? There is no incompatibility because what people
now call mathematics may be so called without there being any
necessary condition that they use for calling it all mathematics.
Nonetheless there may be one common feature of everything
which is now called mathematics such that, if properly under-
stood, it would provide a philosophic understanding of everything
which is NOW called mathematics. However, there is no claim
that this common feature is a necessary condition for something
to be mathematics now. Nor is there any claim that this common
feature is something we can start to use as a necessary condition
for being mathematics. We cannot say how "mathematics" will
be used in the future. Five hundred years from now people may
label activities as "mathematics" which do not have the common
feature we found. In such a case, we would need a new philosophy
of mathematics; but not a new definition of "mathematics".
The importance for this essay of using my loose characteriza-
tion of mathematics is that by using it we shall follow Wittgenstein
in our attempts to gain philosophic understanding of mathematics.
We shall try to gain philosophic understanding of what is now
called mathematics without trying to foreclose on what can be
called mathematics. Or, as Wittgenstein put it in IV-52:
It is not that a new building has to be erected, or that a new bridge
has to be built, but that the geography, as it now is, has to be judged.


20
Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
And a bit later in the same passage be wrote:
Even 500 years ago a philosophy of mathematics was possible, a
philosophy of what mathematics was then.
(For a similar statement see 11-81.) So in Wittgenstein's RFM we
should expect no definitions of "mathematics". Similarly, we
should expect no altering of mathematics as it is actually done,
eg., re-doing mathematics in symbolic logic. He wanted to under-
stand mathematics as it is actually done now but he does not want
to say that what is done now is or is not REALLY mathematics. He
wants only to understand whatever it is that mathematicians are
now doing. If they do something different in the future, it will be
up to philosophers of that age to understand what it is that their
mathematicians are doing.
8. I agree with Wittgenstein that what we want to understand
is mathematics as it now is. Thus I agree with Wittgenstein that
in the philosophy of mathematics we should leave it as it is. If we
change it, we will be studying something else. Wittgenstein stated
this position very well in PI-124 where he wrote:
Philosophy may in no way interfere with the actual use of language;
it can in the end only describe it.
For it cannot give it any foundation either.
It leaves everything as it is.
It also leaves mathematics as it is, and no mathematical discovery
can advance it. A "leading problem of mathematical logic" is for us
a problem of mathematics like any other.
Of course, when he claimed that no mathematical discovery can
advance the philosophy of mathematics, he was claiming that a
new discovery such as the discovery that the predicate calculus is
recursively undecidable will not help us understand mathematics
as it is now done since this new discovery is only part of the
current mathematics to be understood. However, he did not mean
that mathematical advances are totally irrelevant for philosophy
because the advances may give new mathematics to philosophize
about. Thus Michael Dummett is a bit inaccurate when he criti-
cizes Wittgenstein for sharply segregating philosophy from mathe-


Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
21
matics on p. 492 of BP in his article "Wittgenstein's Philosophy
of Mathematics" which is reprinted on pp. 491-509 of BP.
9. But now that I have said so much about gaining a philo-
sophic understanding of what mathematicians are doing, I should
return to complete my discussion of what it is to try to gain a
philosophic understanding of mathematics. This requires that I
return to commenting on my three groups of questions. What is
question (Ii) supposed to bring out? It is supposed to bring out
whether or not a philosopher believes that an essential step in
coming to a philosophic understanding of a mathematical propo-
sition is to describe the truth conditions for it. In his "Wittgen-
stein's Philosophy of Mathematics", Michael Dummett suggests
that the fundamental division in the philosophy of mathematics
stems from whether or not a philosopher holds that a description
of truth conditions rather than a description of assertibility con-
ditions is the proper way to explain the meaning of a mathe-
matical proposition. Although I disagree with Dummett's view
that Wittgenstein holds a conventionalistic interpretation of neces-
sity, Dummett is correct in holding that these two ways of ex-
plaining the meaning of mathematical propositions lead to the
fundamental philosophic division between those who hold that
mathematical truths tell us the order and connexion of some ob-
jects and those who either hold that there are no truths in mathe-
matics or hold that mathematical truths do not tell us of the order
and connexion of some objects. For this reason, I think that an
investigation into the philosophical foundations of mathematics
should begin with question (Ii). Of course, a person's initial answer
to (Ii) may need to be revised if it gives him difficulty on the other
questions or has repugnant philosophic consequences later on.
But what does it mean to explain the meaning of a sentence?
The answer is in Russell's Introduction to Wittgenstein's Tractatus
Logico-Philosophicus. Russell claims that Wittgenstein in the
Tractatus is concerned with the question: "what relation must one
fact (such as a sentence) have to another in order to be capable
of being a symbol for that other?" I believe that this question of
the Tractatus is a special case of a more general question which
we can call, misleadingly as we shall see, explaining the meaning


22
Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
of a sentence. The more general question is: How does a sentence
do what people use it to do? Why worry about how a sentence
can be used to do what it is used to do? A philosopher has such
a worry if he characterizes some use of a sentence in a particular
way, has a preconceived idea on how this use as he characterized
it is carried out, but yet the conditions he believes must be met
for it to be so used do not obtain. A fine example of such a
problem was Russell's worry about "The present King of France
is bald." He could imagine characterizing a use of this sentence
as fact stating. According to his preconceived idea of what must
obtain for this sentence to be used to state a fact the term "The
present King of France" should denote some individual. But it
does not. Thus Russell had the problem of how "The present
King of France is bald" could be used as a fact stating sentence.
Ethics provides another example of this kind of problem. Some-
one might characterize uses of sentences such as "Physical health
is good" as fact stating but also believe that "good" should stand
for some observable property if the sentence is used to state a
fact. Consequently, such a philosopher would ask how ethical
sentences can do the job, viz., state facts, which he believes that
they do. Another philosopher may characterize all uses of sen-
tences as expressions of thoughts but, because of preconceived
ideas of what thoughts are like, be unable to understand how
audible or visible sentences can carry thoughts. I believe that with
ingenuity one could restate most, if not all, philosophical problems
as wonder about how a kind of sentence can do the job it is be-
lieved to do, given a philosopher's preconceptions on how that
job can be done.
Our main concern here, however, is with problems about
mathematical objects and necessity, in other words, problems
about the ontology and epistemology of mathematics. Such prob-
lems can be formulated as doubts about how sentences can do
jobs which they have been said to do. For instance, mathematical
sentences may be characterized as primarily devices for stating
facts. If one has the preconceived idea that a sentence can be
used to state a fact only if some of the terms in it are used to
refer to observable individuals and properties which are arranged


Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
23
in a way in which they do not have to be arranged, he will wonder
how mathematical sentences can state facts, since if they are about
anything they are about how unobservable individuals and proper-
ties must be arranged. Actually, I have designed my three groups
of questions as attempts to determine in what way, i^ any, mathe-
matical sentences can be used to state facts, granted that it is ex-
tremely doubtful that they say how observable objects happen to
be arranged. (Of course, once one has the problem of how a sen-
tence can do the job it allegedly does, he should also be prepared to
ask whether the sentence really is used to do the job it is said to be
used for or to modify the preconceived ideas on what must hold
for this job to be done.) They are two ways for a philosopher to
handle problems due to his characterization of the role a sentence
plays and his preconceived idea of what must obtain for that role
to be played. The first way corresponds to explaining meaning in
terms of truth conditions while the second corresponds to ex-
plaining meaning by describing assertibility conditions.
10. Explaining the meaning of a sentence in terms of its truth
conditions is to explain how the sentence can be used to state a
fact when it is doubtful that it can state a fact, given preconceived
ideas of what it is to state a fact. For instance, it is doubtful that
the kind of conditions which entitle us to say "Peter is blond"
exist when we assert "7 is prime". So, a philosopher who holds
that "7 is prime" states a fact will wonder how it can do so. But,
let us say that he is not willing to give up the belief that "7 is
prime" states a fact. Nor, is he willing to change radically his
conception of what there must be for a sentence to state a fact.
Let us say that our philosopher holds that for a sentence to state
a fact the non-logical signs must stand for observable individuals
or properties and the logical signs must be interpreted so that the
sentence takes on truth values depending upon how the objects
referred to are arranged. Thus, he holds that "Peter is blond"
states a fact only if "Peter" is used to stand for a human male,
"blond" is used to stand for the property of having yellowish hair,
and the logical word "is" is interpreted as saying that the man
Peter and the property blond happen to be arranged so that the
hair of Peter exemplifies the property of being blond. Now he


24
Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
cannot, let us assume, say that "7" stands for an observable in-
dividual and that "prime" stands for an observable property, and
he cannot say that it merely happens to be the case that 7 is
prime. (He cannot say these things because he wants to agree
with ordinary usage.) Still he wants to say that "7 is prime" states
a fact only if "7" refers to some individual, "prime" to some
property, and "is" says that some kind of exemplification occurs.
So this philosopher has a phenomenon to explain. The phenome-
non he has to explain is that "7 is prime" is capable of stating a
fact. Very likely he would account for this alleged fact by devel-
oping a theory which has non-observable objects necessarily con-
nected, i.e., a species of platonism. The important point for this
essay, however, is that he has explained meaning, - explained
how a sentence can be used to state a fact -, by developing a
theory about how some objects are connected in order to provide
truth conditions for fact stating uses of the sentence.
11. There is a second way in which a philosopher can handle
this kind of problem, which arises when his characterization of
the role a sentence plays and his preconceived ideas on how that
role is played, clash with the way in which the sentence is actually
used. He can take the position that as a philosopher he cannot
refer to something beyond language to account for how language
operates. He would hold that it is the task of empirical science
to discover what and how things beyond language enable language
to function as it does. All that a philosopher can do to remove
his doubts about how language functions is to become clear on
how it does function. To accomplish this he shall have to drop
his original characterizations of use and preconceived ideas on
how language can be used as he had characterized it. He must
simply be clear about what goes on when people make assertions.
(At this point, it is extremely important to emphasize that de-
scribing what goes on when people make assertions is to do far
more than merely describe what is said and to point out what
forms of words people would regard as bizarre. In RFM, we shall
find that describing what goes on when people make mathematical
assertions consists primarily of calling attention to non-linguistic
facts, viz., physical, psychological, and sociological facts.) After


Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
25
he is clear about what goes on when people make assertions,
questions on why and how this happens will be questions for
empirical science. So in effect his philosophic work presents data
for scientific explanation. However, the purpose of the philosophic
description of what goes on when people use language is not to
present data for science. Its purpose is to answer the philosophic
question on how a sentence can do what it is used to do. But the
philosophic question is answered by removing or trying to remove
the question rather than by developing a theory to answer it.
As a result of becoming clear about how language is used we,
it is hoped, will no longer ask for non-scientific explanations of
how it works. Even if we continue to ask, as we probably shall,
how language can do what it does, we shall not answer such
questions because they lead us into non-empirical science. In-
stead, we shall continue to try to understand better what is being
done in the particular use in question. We shall try to make the
question disappear; we shall not try to build on the question in
the sense that we shall not try to build a theory about some objects
in order to answer it. (That is, we shall do these things if we adopt
the second way of handling these problems.) Similarly, we do not
try to build on a disease by developing ways of life to foster it; we
try to remove it. As Wittgenstein said on occasion, for instance in
PI-255: "The philosopher's treatment of a question is like the treat-
ment of an illness." This means that the philosopher is supposed
to try to make his question disappear without traces, i.e., explicit
answers. Of course, the presupposition of this way of handling
philosophical problems is as Wittgenstein put it so well in PI-126:
"Since everything", what is said and the conditions for saying it,
"lies open to view there is nothing to explain. For what is hidden,
for example, is of no interest to us."
12. How did Wittgenstein explain the meaning of sentences?
In RFM he used the second way. But it was not always so with
him. Russell notes in his Introduction to the Tractatus, and as
Wittgenstein himself admitted in P/-114, Wittgenstein of the
Tractatus characterized the role of sentences a£ fact stating. (Thus
we get the restricted question quoted from Russell in section 9.)
As he continued his philosophical investigations, Wittgenstein re-


26
Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
jected the assumption that the essential role of a sentence is to
state facts. This rejection is brought out in PI 18-24. But I think
that a more significant alteration of his philosophical views oc-
curred when he came to the conclusion that the "how" in "How
does a sentence do what people use it to do?" asks for a descrip-
tion rather than for an explanation in terms of some theory. (It
makes no difference if the philosopher calls his theory meaning-
less as Wittgenstein did at the end of his Tractatus; he still has
the theory.) On page 18 of his Blue Book Wittgenstein wrote:
Philosophers constantly see the method of science before their eyes,
and are irresistably tempted to ask and answer questions in the way
science does. This tendency is the real source of metaphysics and leads
the philosopher into complete darkness. I want to say here that it can
never be our job to reduce anything to anything, or to explain any-
thing. Philosophy really is 'purely descriptive'.
This alteration is more significant than his rejection of the mistake
that sentences are essentially fact stating devices because as a
result of these observations he completely changed his way of
doing philosophy. He changed from developing theories about
some objects, which make language work as it does, to describing
how language actually works. The point of this brief sketch of
Wittgenstein's philosophical development is to account for his
philosophizing by describing language and conditions for its use.
He changed in order to avoid the kind of theory building in his
Tractatus, since such theorizing only produces more puzzles when
we try to make the theory clear.
Unfortunately, the method of describing assertibility conditions
has a philosophic point only if one is trying to remove some
preconceived ideas on how language is used and some theories
on how it can be used this way. In other words, the kind of
philosophy Wittgenstein did later in his career makes sense only
as an antidote for his earlier philosophy or other metaphysical
schemes. If usage is not described for the sake of removing the
questions which give rise to metaphysical theory building, the
description becomes the mere, rather casual, compilation of data
for scientific explanation. So, unfortunately RFM will be of value
only to someone who has developed or is tempted to develop a


Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
27
metaphysical scheme to explain how we can have the mathematics
that we actually have. It will not help an unprejudiced mind.
13. Let us return to ask again exactly what question (Ii) is
supposed to bring out. It is supposed to bring out whether or not
the philosopher has enough of a theory of language, - non-em-
pirical most likely -, to try to explain the meaning of mathe-
matical sentences in terms of truth conditions. It should be clear
now, though, that to explain meaning does not require one to talk
about something called meaning; one is only trying to understand
how a sentence can be used to do what it is believed to do.
If one answer (Ii) negatively, he can skip to (Hi). His negative
answer has determined that he will not answer questions from
Group II by means of discussing truth conditions. However, if he
answers (Ii) affirmatively, he has not yet forced himself to hold
that there are some special objects constituting truth conditions
for mathematical propositions. But let us follow up the results
of affirmatively answering (Ii), viz., admitting that mathematical
statements tell us how it is with some objects. It is well to follow
up the results of holding that mathematical sentences have truth
conditions because only when we see what else we may have to
hold when we hold that mathematical sentences have truth con-
ditions can we appreciate why anyone would claim that they do
not. Remember, though, that when I talk about truth conditions
I mean some objects, i.e., individuals and properties, referred to
in the sentence such that if they are arranged in a certain way the
statement made with the sentence is true; otherwise not. I do not
mean by "truth conditions" simply any conditions which entitle
a person to apply "true" to a statement or proposition. For in
this very general sense of "truth conditions" any sentence which
can be used to make a statement or to express a proposition,
which people call true or false, has truth conditions. If one admits
that mathematical sentences have truth conditions, he must also
answer (Iii). Are the objects that we are talking about in non-
applied mathematics ordinary objects? Admittedly, there is no
sharp distinction between ordinary and non-ordinary objects. In
most localities, a man with black teeth is out of the ordinary.
But for purposes of this essay, what do I mean by "non-


28
Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
ordinary objects"? If someone claims that there are some indi-
viduals and relations whose order and connection can be dis-
covered only by doing mathematics, he has claimed that there
are, for purposes of this essay, non-ordinary objects. I have in
mind here someone claiming that the objects which they talk about
in mathematics cannot be dealt with by common sense observa-
tion because they lack the secondary qualities of color, odor,
visual size, shape, and feel while adding that they cannot be dealt
with by the method of physical science because they do not have
properties definable in terms of length, mass, and time. Of course,
a person who claims that there are mathematical objects, as
above, would also claim that they cannot be studied by the social
sciences since these alleged mathematical objects are non-temporal
individuals and relations. I do not think that it is unfair to label
atemporal, unobservable objects "non-ordinary". Nor, do I think
that it is inaccurate to label as platonistic a view of mathematics
which holds that there are truth conditions for mathematical sen-
tences consisting of such non-ordinary objects, as the term "pla-
tonistic" is presently used in a non-technical slightly abusive way.
14. I shall here add a note about the use of the term
"platonist" in this essay. In this essay, I shall follow Dummett's
explication of the term in his "Wittgenstein's Philosophy of
Mathematics". Dummett wrote, on p. 492 of BP, "According to
Platonism, mathematical objects are there and stand in certain
relations to one another, independently of us, and what we do is
to discover these objects and their relations to one another."
Dummett's statement of platonism is not too different from Ber-
nays' statement of absolute platonism in his "Sur le Platonisme
dans les Mathématiques" an English translation of which appears
in BP on pp. 274-286. According to Bernays', on p. 277 of BP,
absolute platonists postulate "the existence of a world of ideal
objects containing all the objects and relations of mathematics".
Earlier, on p. 275 of BP, Bernays noted that they view "the
objects as cut off from all links with the reflecting subject". Ex-
cept, of course, they can be known by the reflecting subject; they
simply can exist without being known and they are not affected
by being known. On p. 277 of BP, Bernays contrasts absolute


Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
29
platonism with what he calls a more restricted platonism. Accord-
ing to Bemays a restricted platonist talks about there being a
domain of individuals and properties which are studied exclusively
by mathematics but he does not claim this domain "to be more
than, so to speak, an ideal projection of thought". In effect, a
restricted platonist does not hold that mathematical objects exist
independently of human thinking. For instance, an absolute pla-
tonist would hold that the totality of integers exists independently
of anyone thinking about them. A restricted platonist would hold
that the totality of integers exists only if people think of such a
totality. However, after he has posited, - reified -, the totality of
integers, a restricted platonist would talk about particular integers
existing independently of being thought about. More generally, a
restricted platonist admits that domains of mathematical objects
are mind-dependent but that within these mind-dependent do-
mains he can talk about there being objects unknown and un-
thought of.
My purpose in talking about platonism is twofold. Wittgenstein
is charged with dogmatically rejecting platonism. To understand
this charge we must understand what Wittgenstein would have
meant by "platonism" had he used the term. Second, Bernays
maintains that the occurrence of the antinomies, e.g., Russell's
paradox involving the class of all classes which do not belong to
themselves, show that absolute platonism is untenable. If Bernays
were correct on this second point, Wittgenstein was wrong about
how to philosophize about mathematics. Recall, from section 8,
that Wittgenstein held that mathematical results are philosophi-
cally irrelevant except insofar as they provide more material to
philosophize about.
15. Wittgenstein would have found both absolute and restricted
platonism philosophically repugnant. In IV-5 he wrote:
Someone makes an addition to mathematics, gives new definitions and
discovers new theorems - and in a certain respect he can be said not
to know what he is doing. - He has a vague imagination of having
discovered something like a space (at which point he thinks of a
room), of having opened up a kingdom, and when asked about it he
would talk a great deal of nonsense


30
Problems at the Foundations
of
Mathematics
Later in IV-5, he wrote:
In other words: if someone believes in mathematical objects and their
queer properties - can't he nevertheless do mathematics? Or - isn't he
also doing mathematics.
I think that these remarks make it clear that Wittgenstein rejects
belief in mathematical objects and that he was not inclined to
accept a mathematicians account of what he does in mathematics.
His disinclination to accept mathematicians' philosophy of what
they are doing is a clue that he rejected restricted as well as
absolute platonism. (I take restricted platonism as just the kind of
position that a mathematician would advance if he thought that
he was talking about objects when he did mathematics, but was a
bit timid about advancing his position.) Restricted platonism would
have been repugnant to Wittgenstein because talk about mathe-
matical domains being created by the mind reveals the belief that
one needs a theory about some objects to account for their use
of sentences in mathematics. Also, mind-dependent domains with
mind-independent members are almost as mysterious as the do-
mains of the absolute platonists, and I think the above remarks
make it clear that Wittgenstein was loathe to talk about mysterious
entities, be they in or out of the mind. The final paragraph of
IV-5 makes it more certain that he was opposed to talking about
objects for mathematical propositions, even if it is conceded that
they exist only in the mind. In this last paragraph, he suggested
that the phrase "ideal object" is misleading. Finally, if we turn to
PI-254 where he wrote:
what a mathematician is inclined to say about the objectivity and
reality of mathematical facts, is not a philosophy of mathematics, but
something for philosophical treatment...
We have even more support for my contention that he would
consider a statement of restricted platonism as problematic as a
statement of absolute platonism. So, although there is a difference
between absolute and restricted platonism, in the rest of this essay
I shall talk about platonism as absolute platonism. I shall do this


Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
31
because Wittgenstein does not precisely distinguish between the
two. He probably would have regarded restricted platonism as
simply a hopeless attempt to patch up a bad position.
16. Why does Bernays claim that the mathematical results of
the antinomies show that the philosophical position of absolute
platonism is untenable? He is rather terse on this point. On p.
277 of BP, Bernays writes:
The essential importance of these antinomies is to bring out the im-
possibility of combining the following two things: the idea of the
totality of all mathemathical objects and the general concepts of set
and function; for the totality itself would form a domain of elements
of sets, and arguments and values for functions.
I think that Bernays' argument is as follows. If someone regards
the totality of mathematical objects as being the same kind of
thing as mathematical objects, then he accepts that the totality
of mathematical objects can be dealt with, as other mathematical
objects are dealt with. This means that the totality of mathematical
objects will be dealt with as if it can belong to a set, be an argu-
ment of a function, or be a value of a function. If someone accepts
that the domain of all mathematical objects can be dealt with as
other mathematical objects, he has the antinomies. If someone is
an absolute platonist he holds that the domain of all mathematical
objects is the same kind of thing as particular mathematical ob-
jects. Thus someone who is an absolute platonist has the anti-
nomies. But since no one can accept the antinomies no one can,
rationally, be an absolute platonist. For instance, an absolute
platonist may say that the domain of classes is the domain of
mathematical objects, where class is understood intuitively as all
and only those objects satisfying a certain condition. If they regard
the domain of classes as the same kind of thing as other classes,
they may easily slip into the view that the domain of classes is
the class of all classes and that this class can be dealt with as
other classes are dealt with. For instance, they may split the class
of all classes into two parts, viz., those that belong to themselves
and those that do not. Once they make this split, they have the
Russell paradox as soon as they realize that their belief that the


32
Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
class of all classes can be split into two parts requires them to
determine whether or not the class of all classes which do not
belong to themselves does or does not belong to itself.
Now I believe that Bernays is correct on all his premisses ex-
cept for the one saying that if someone holds that the domain of
all mathematical objects is the same kind of thing as mathematical
objects he also holds that the domain of all mathematical objects
can be treated as another mathematical object. This premisse is
unclear on exactly what it is to regard two things as being of the
same kind. In this context in which we are discussing absolute
platonism, the fair interpretation of "A is of the same kind as B"
is "A and B both exist independently of (dependent upon) being
thought about or being known." With this interpretation, there is
no reason for charging that an absolute platonist holds that the
domain of mathematical objects is itself a mathematical object
like the other mathematical objects. All that an absolute platonist
need hold is that both the domain of mathematical objects and
mathematical objects themselves exist outside and independently
of minds. Indeed, an absolute platonist can regard the antinomies
as a mathematical proof of the fact that the mind-independent
domain of mathematical objects is mathematically different from
the mathematical objects with which it is ontologically similar.
So, if I am right, the mathematical discovery of the antinomies
did not resolve the philosophic issue between absolute and re-
stricted platonism. Thus we do not here have a counter-example
to Wittgenstein's claim that mathematical results are philosophi-
cally irrelevant.
17. I believe, and I think that Wittgenstein believed, that if
we do accept that mathematical sentences have truth conditions,
i.e., give an affirmative answer to (Ii), we will be forced to pla-
tonism, i.e., giving an affirmative answer to (Iii). I believe this
because as soon as we admit that there are mathematical objects,
integers for example, we immediately encounter all the hackneyed
arguments that they cannot be ordinary objects. For instance, one
may face an argument that the number two could not be an ob-
servable individual because one can weigh an observable individual
but it makes no sense to talk about weighing the number two. I


Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
33
call these arguments hackneyed because it takes no real under-
standing of any subject to make these obvious observations about
what can be said. No insight is provided by these observations,
and it is surprising that anyone could think that an important fact
about the world could be established by such casual observations.
But note that I am not saying that such ways of arguing are trivial
if we are NOT doing metaphysics. If we are merely trying to be
clear about what is said, such facts on what can be said may be
very relevant. This way of arguing would be what Wittgenstein
in PI-127 calls assembling reminders for a particular purpose.
Such arguments are relevant if they remind us how number words
are actually used; but they do not show anything about the nature
of truth conditions for mathematical sentences.
18. It is more difficult to establish that Wittgenstein denied
that mathematical sentences have truth conditions in order to
avoid platonism. But it is easy to establish that Wittgenstein
denied that mathematical sentences have truth conditions. I mean
that it is easy to establish that Wittgenstein rejects a view that
there are mathematical facts to provide truth conditions for state-
ments made in mathematics. In V-15 he suggested:
it takes mathematics to define the CHARACTER of what we are
calling a 'fact'!
He went on to say in V-15:
It is interesting to know how many vibrations this note has! But it
took arithmetic to teach you this question. It taught you to see this
kind of fact.
Later in V-15 he challenged himself with:
To be practical, mathematics must tell us facts.
Since he had no doubt about the practicality of mathematics, he
must face the challenge. His reply was:
But do these facts have to be the mathematical facts? - But why
should not mathematics, instead of 'teaching us facts', create the form
of what we call facts?


34
Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
These remarks support, if they do not establish, that Wittgenstein
did not accept truth conditions, - mathematical facts -, for mathe-
matical sentences.
Indeed Bernays, on p. 513 of BP, in his article "Comments on
Ludwig Wittgenstein's Remarks on the Foundations of Mathe-
matics", which is reprinted on pp. 510-528 of BP, charges that
Wittgenstein uncritically adopted "a preconceived philosophical
view ..., that there can exist but one kind of factuality: that of
concrete reality". Bernays also writes in this article, on p. 513
of BP,
The fundamental division between the sphere of mathematics and the
sphere of facts appears in several passages in the book. In this con-
nection Wittgenstein often speaks with a certitude which strangely
contrast with his readiness to doubt so much of what is generally
accepted.
Bernays is, I believe, certainly correct that Wittgenstein, with un-
characteristic directness, proclaims that there are no mathematical
facts. Bernays cites such striking remarks as:
"If you know a mathematical proposition, that's not to say you
yet know anything" and "the mathematical proposition is only
supposed to supply a framework for a description" from V-2.
(One can find more support for the claim that Wittgenstein denied
that there were mathematical facts in 1-155.)
19. I do not, however, accept Bernays' complaint that Wittgen-
stein uncritically accepted the generally accepted (philosophical)
view when he made this sharp distinction between factual and
mathematical propositions. To the contrary, Wittgenstein was well
aware of the fact that many people, both philosophers and non-
philosophers, are inclined to talk about mathematical objects and,
hence, mathematical facts. He realized that our ways of speaking
in mathematics suggests that there are mathematical facts. In
III-11 he wrote:
Arithmetic as the natural history (mineralogy) of numbers. But who
talks like this about it? Our whole thinking is penetrated with this
idea.


Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
35
(Bernays also notes this passage.) In Appendix 1-4, Wittgenstein
wrote:
We are used to saying "2 times 2 is 4", and the verb "is" makes this
into a proposition, and apparently establishes a close kinship with
everything that we call a 'proposition'. Whereas it is a matter only of
a very superficial relationship.
So Wittgenstein did not unconsciously assume the popular posi-
tion on mathematical facts, although I do grant that he did not
directly argue for the position that mathematical sentences do not
have truth conditions.
However, although he did not directly argue that mathematical
statements are not statements of fact, he did show that mathe-
matical statements do not state experimental facts. And his dis-
cussion of whether or not mathematical sentences are used to
state experimental facts is not dogmatic. Over and over again in
RFM, he returned to question whether or not mathematics is an
experimental science. (His assumption was that, if mathematical
sentences are used to state facts, they state empirical facts. This
assumption was revealed in V-15.) But his answer is always the
same: Although mathematics depends upon facts, mathematical
statements are not factual.
The reason for his not being able to identify empirical facts as
mathematical truth conditions, and thus not being able to find any
mathematical truth conditions, may well have been a fundamental
position which he accepts unargued although not necessarily un-
consciously. I believe that if we ask about the role of Wittgen-
stein's anti-platonism in his philosophy of mathematics and if we
ask about the source of his anti-platonism in his philosophy, we
can uncover some of the most fundamental of Wittgenstein's
positions. I do believe that an insurmountable objection to ac-
cepting mathematical objects, in other words, an insurmountable
objection to platonism, was the determining factor in Wittgen-
stein's philosophy of mathematics. It was a determining factor in
the sense that any method of philosophizing or philosophical
position which led to platonism would be rejected. If a philosopher
had no insurmountable objection to platonism, I do not know


36
Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
why he would fight against the common belief that there are
mathematical facts, reject the suggestion of our ways of speaking
that there are mathematical facts, and develop a new method of
philosophizing in order to handle traditional problems of phi-
losophy without having to talk about truth conditions.
In this essay, I shall not criticize Wittgenstein for being anti-
platonistic. I shall direct my efforts towards trying to present and
evaluate his non-platonistic account. I suspect that most phi-
losophers would agree that if a philosopher could develop a non-
platonistic account, the non-platonistic account is to be preferred
over any platonistic one. However, at times his resolve to be
non-platonistic may seem dogmatic and uncritical in his philoso-
phy of mathematics. But this anti-platonism may have a source
outside his philosophy of mathematics.
20. So it is appropriate to digress on the source of Wittgen-
stein's anti-platonism. (This section is a digression in the sense
that I shall be talking about his philosophy in general rather than
about only his philosophy of mathematics.) His anti-platonism
did not stem from an empiricism which holds that we can think
about and get knowledge of only observable objects. In my
opinion, his anti-platonism stemmed from his belief that no fact
must be as it is. This belief in the contingency of all facts remained
unchanged since his early thought in the Tractatus. In his Trac-
tatus, Wittgenstein wrote that necessary truths and falsehoods
cannot be statements of fact. As he put it in 4.462:
Tautology and contradiction are not pictures of the reality. They
present no possible state of affairs,
In 5.43 he wrote:
But all propositions of logic say the same thing. That is nothing.
And in 5.1362, in a passage strikingly similar to the ones we cited
from V-2 he wrote:
("A knows that p is the case" is senseless if p is a tautology.)
(It should be noted that in the Tractatus Wittgenstein held that
there is only logical necessity in 6.37.) In addition in 5.5303 of


Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
37
his Tractatus he maintained that statements of identity are not
statements of fact because they are necessarily true or necessarily
false. I am sure that the above passages show that in the Trac-
tatus Wittgenstein would not admit that there is a kind of fact
which provides truth conditions for necessary truths.
It seems to me that his willingness to talk about the constituents
of facts in the 2s section of his Tractatus indicates a willingness
to talk about unobservable objects while doing philosophy, al-
though I doubt that he would have admitted that these unob-
servable constituents of facts ever formed an unobservable fact.
So even at the Tractatus stage of his thought empiricism was not
a major concern of Wittgenstein. In Wittgenstein's later writings
there are, to my knowledge, no claims that there are unobservable
objects, no claims that there are no unobservable objects, and no
claims that talk about unobservable objects are meaningless. I
submit that in his later writings Wittgenstein was unconcerned
with how statements of fact are connected with their truth con-
ditions and the nature of these truth conditions. He was not con-
cerned with whether or not descriptive terms in a sentence have
observable referents. Nor was he concerned with whether or not
factual statements have observable confirming or disconfirming
conditions. I interpret Wittgenstein's famous claim in PI-43: "For
a large class of cases - though not for all-in which we employ
the word 'meaning' it can be defined thus: the meaning of a word
is its use in the language" as expressing a lack of concern for the
traditional empiricists' goals of establishing meaningfulness of
sentences by finding observable referents for descriptive terms or
finding observable confirming or disconfirming conditions. His
statement in V-13 that propositions about physical objects and
sense impressions do not need an analysis also reveals a lack of
concern with the traditional problems of empiricists. So I con-
clude that in his later writings his anti-platonism does not come
from an empiricist bias. And his indifference to empricism in his
later writings was no reaction against his earlier work; he simply
was never bothered by the problems of empiricism.
However, I must admit that Wittgenstein wrote in RFM as if
the only facts that there are are empirical facts. In V-15 he claimed


38
Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
that only experiment can uncover facts. To my knowledge he
never challenged this claim that only experiment can uncover
facts. In III-4 he wrote:
'This could be imagined otherwise' or 'We can imagine the opposite
too', ascribes the role of an empirical proposition to it.
So it seems that Wittgenstein identified being a contingent fact
with being an empirical fact and being an empirical fact with
being a fact. Thus 1 conclude that in RFM, as well as in his
Tractatus, Wittgenstein believed that there were no necessary
facts, i.e., no facts provided truth conditions for necessary truths.
But how does a belief that there are no necessary facts lead to
anti-platonism? If there were timeless objects, as the mathemati-
cal objects of platonism would be, they would stand in unchanging
arrangements. But objects, i.e., individuals and properties, in un-
changing arrangements would constitute necessary facts. So a
rejection of necessary facts requires a rejection of the mathe-
matical objects of platonism.
My purpose in going into this digression on the source of
Wittgenstein's anti-platonism in his philosophy of mathematics is
to show that it was not based on a dogmatic, sophomoric rejection
of abstract objects simply because they are abstract, or as Bernays
said, outside concrete reality. I would like to suggest that his
anti-platonism sprung from a Weltanschauung expressed in 6.41
of his Tractatus. In 6.41 he wrote:
The sense of the world must lie outside the world. In the world every-
thing is as it is and happens as it does happen. In it there is no value
- and if there were, it would be of no value.
If there is a value which is of value, it must lie outside all happening
and being-so. For all happening and being-so is accidental.
What makes it non-accidental cannot lie in the world, for otherwise
this would again be accidental.
I submit that in his RFM, as in his Tractatus, Wittgenstein held
that the world is the totality of facts, only natural science can give
us knowledge of these facts, these facts are all that can be known,
and none of them has to be as they are. The major difference
between his early and later philosophy is that in his later phi-


Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
39
losophy he had a new method of philosophizing which prevented
him from doing the metaphysics behind his pessimistic Welt-
anschauung but which enabled him to provide a far more accurate
account of the role of mathematical and other non-scientific lan-
guage although the non-scientific language could never, strictly
speaking, be used to express knowledge.
21. Let us return from this digression to a discussion of the
three groups of questions on the philosophy of mathematics. Of
course, if we give a non-platonistic answer to (Iii), we can skip
down to the second group of questions on the modality of mathe-
matical propositions. On the other hand if we give a platonistic
answer, we face (Iiii). What (Iiii) asks is straightforward. Actually
(Iiii) can be fully answered only by answering the questions in
Group II. Also the great difficulties a philosopher faces in answer-
ing (Iiii) may well lead him to recant his platonistic position and
hence his view that mathematical sentences have truth conditions.
This recantation will bring him directly to the Second Group of
questions. So without further comment, let us turn to (Hi).
Question (Iii) is designed to make those who have denied that
mathematical sentences have truth conditions commit themselves
on whether or not it is legitimate to talk about there being any
mathematical truths or falsehoods. Of course, (Iii) is a genuinely
open question for a philosopher who has denied that mathematical
sentences have truth conditions, viz., who has denied that mathe-
matical sentences are used to state facts in non-applied mathe-
matics. One can look at the program of Logicists, such as Bertrand
Russell, according to which they would try to express all existing
mathematics as logical truths containing no unbound descriptive
signs as an attempt to show how there could be mathematical
truths, indeed necessary truths, without there being any mathe-
matical facts. However, answering (Hi) is not easy for a non-
platonist. A platonist need simply say that mathematical proposi-
tions are true if they correctly describe the mathematical facts;
otherwise they are false. But the non-platonist faces the dilemma
of either saying that mathematical statements are neither true nor
false or else explaining how they can be true but yet true of
nothing. One has to oppose ordinary usage of "true" too much


40
Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
to say that mathematical statements are neither true nor false.
(One does not, however, oppose ordinary usage if he points out
that the conditions for applying "true" to a mathematical state-
ment are radically different from the conditions for applying
"true" to an empirical statement. I should also add that to say
that the conditions for applying "true" in mathematics are dif-
ferent from those in natural science is not necessarily to say that
there are two senses of "true".) It is difficult, but a philosopher
can come to grips with the other horn of the dilemma. However,
a philosopher cannot come to grips with it by holding that mathe-
matical statements are contingently true but yet have no truth
conditions. It seems to me that for a statement to be contingently
true it must correspond to a fact which does occur but which
need not have occurred. I think that someone is misusing "true"
if he says that a proposition, or a statement he makes, is contin-
gently true because its truth is contingent upon a rule or conven-
tion saying that it is true. To say "S is true because S is called
true" is odd. I suppose that a platonist could try to maintain that
mathematical sentences are used to express contingent facts about
the mathematical domain. But I do not see how timeless objects
could stand in contingent arrangements. Thus if a philosopher
answers (Hi) affirmatively he will very likely answer (Ilii) by
saying that mathematical truths are necessarily true. That is: a
philosopher who answers (Hi) affirmatively will very likely go on
to say that mathematical truths are necessary truths unless he
holds that mathematical sentences are used to state empirical
facts. If a philosopher holds an empirical interpretation of mathe-
matical statements, he will have his difficulties with question (Ilii)
on the necessity of mathematical truths. But before we say any-
thing about (Ilii) let us try to attain an idea of how Wittgenstein
answers (Hi).
22. Wittgenstein was tempted to say that what mathematical
sentences are used to state or that which they express is neither
true nor false. To say that what they are used to state has truth
value very readily leads us to believe that they state facts. He
revealed this wish in Appendix 1-4 when he remarked that there
is only a superficial similarity between what is expressed with


Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
41
arithmetic sentences, such as 2 + 2 = 4, and everything else that
we call a proposition. But he did not oppose ordinary usage.
Throughout his book he talked of mathematical propositions. At
places he talked explicity of mathematical truth. In 11-46 he asked
himself: "When do we say that a mathematical proposition is
true?" From the context it is clear that he did not expect the
answer to be "never". In V-36 he remarked: "The proof of a
proposition shews me what I am prepared to stake on its truth."
So it is clear that Wittgenstein answered (Hi) affirmatively, i.e.,
he would talk about true and false in mathematics.
If I am right, then, in maintaining that a philosopher who does
not give an empirical interpretation of mathematics but who holds
that there are mathematical truths will answer (Ilii) by saying that
mathematical truths are necessarily true, Wittgenstein should have
held that mathematical truths are necessarily true. And this he
did. As a matter of fact, the central problem of RFM can be
viewed as the problem of explaining an affirmative answer to
(Ilii). Recall that explaining an affirmative answer to (Ilii) is to
explain the nature of mathematical necessity and to explain the
nature of mathematical necessity is to answer (Iliii). Early in
RFM, at 1-4, Wittgenstein asked himself: "But then what does
the peculiar inexorability of mathematics consist in?" In the case
of the problems of mathematical necessity we have a clear case
of philosophical problems being problems about understanding
our grammar if one, not unreasonably, regards the modality of a
sentence as one of its grammatical features. So at least in this
case, Wittgenstein was not inaccurate when he wrote in V-13:
"What mathematical propositions do stand in need of is a clarifi-
cation of their grammar." That is, he was not inaccurate if, as I
submit, one of the central problems of RFM was to help us under-
stand how mathematical sentences can be used to say what must
be so.
23. Let us now consider the third group of questions. It is
clear, I hope, that the third group of questions asks whether ap-
plied and non-applied mathematics differ, if at all, by their subject
matter or modality. The intent of asking the third group is to
provide some check on the answers to the first two groups. At


42
Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
least a necessary condition for being an acceptable philosophy of
mathematics is to be able to account for applied mathematics. I
suppose that the only completely satisfactory check on one's
philosophy of mathematics is to integrate it into a satisfactory
philosophy. Of course, the third group of questions is also de-
signed to answer philosophic doubts about how mathematics can
be both pure and applied.
24. Wittgenstein's answers to the questions about applied
mathematics are an important part of his philosophy of mathe-
matics. One of Wittgenstein's most important positions was that
mathematical sentences are meaningless without application. In
IV-2 he wrote:
one can form a mathematical proposition in a grammatically correct
way without understanding its meaning.
Now when does one understand it? - I believe when one can
apply it.
In IV-2, he suggested that it is application which makes a game
with signs into mathematics. (Presumably, mathematical sen-
tences, which Wittgenstein calls "propositions", without applica-
tion would be meaningless and hence their use would be a mere
game with symbols.) A consequence of his position that there are
no meaningful unapplied mathematical propositions is a hint that
a proper understanding of applications would remove the anti-
nomies. In V-8, Wittgenstein noted:
What Russell's '~f(f)' lacks above all is application and hence mean-
ing.
If l~f(f)' were meaningless we would not have to worry about
any of its consequences. So there is no doubt that Wittgenstein
thought that a philosopher of mathematics must pay attention to
applied mathematics.
25. Can an attempt to answer the above three groups of ques-
tions be called doing the philosophy of mathematics? Of course,
there is no doubt that dealing with the ontology, epistemology,
and difference between pure and applied mathematics is philoso-
phizing about mathematics. The only question is whether or not


Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
43
a philosophy of mathematics should concern itself with more. In
particular, should a philosophy of mathematics include analyses
of crucial mathematical concepts and an attempt to provide
mathematical foundations for mathematics? In section 27, I will
point out why Wittgenstein thought that labor on the mathematical
foundations of mathematics was philosophically irrelevant. But in
this section let us see what Wittgenstein would have said about
analyzing mathematical concepts.
In his The Nature of Mathematics (Routledge and Kegan Paul,
1933), in a chapter entitled "Tasks of a Philosophy of Mathe-
matics" Max Black wrote the following.
A philosophy of mathematics has two principal objects intimately
connected with arithmetic and the theory of functions respectively: -
(1) To elucidate and analyze the notion of 'integer' or 'natural
number',
(2) to alucidate the nature of the mathematical continuum.
For Black an elucidation or an analysis of a notion such as
'integer' is what he called a logical analysis of it. For him a logical
analysis of a notion consists in showing how the motion should
be connected with other notions so that with the analyzed notion
we can say everything we said with it before except those things
which led to contradictions or which were philosophically (logi-
cally) misleading. For instance, a logical analysis of the notion of
class or set should show how we can do all of set theory except
for those parts which led to the antinomies. In his chapter on
logical analysis, Black cited Russell's analysis of the notion of
definite descriptions as a good example of logical analysis. In his
analysis of definite descriptions, Russell showed that once we
understand how the notion of a definite description is related to
other notions such as that of existential quantification, we shall
no longer be misled to believe that there must always be some
kind of referent for a definite description. Frege's attempt to
define "integer" is also an example of logical analysis. In RFM,
one finds no logical analyses.
26. I think that Wittgenstein was unconcerned with logical
analyses because, as we noted in section 8, he was concerned with


44
Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
understanding mathematics as it is now done. A logical analysis
of current mathematics will alter the mathematics now done in
the following ways. It will improve it mathematically by removing
contradictions, alter its forms of expression by elimination of
allegedly philosophically misleading expressions, and it will alter
the structure of present mathematics by containing connections
not contained in present mathematics. For instance, the arith-
metization of analysis was an alteration of the present structure
of mathematics. For Wittgenstein such an alteration of mathe-
matics would be philosophically useless and, I suspect worse than
useless since looking at the new improved mathematics would not
be a study of mathematics as it is now done. Also it is possible
that a logical analyst could so seriously alter the ways of expres-
sion used in mathematics that the IMPROVED mathematics is no
longer mathematics. (In Chapter IV, we will find that Wittgen-
stein argued that Logicists had so seriously altered mathematics
that they no longer had mathematics after their analyses.)
But it is more important to see why Wittgenstein would regard
such analyses as philosophically useless since the efforts of mathe-
maticians may actually lead to the kind of mathematics which a
logical analyst thinks that there ought to be. In case of such a
development, the job of a philosopher would be to understand the
new IMPROVED mathematics. For one, the mere removal of con-
tradictions leaves unanswered the philosophically significant ques-
tion: Why remove contradictions? In my opinion the question:
"Why remove contradictions?" is philosophically significant be-
cause an answer to it presupposes positions on the ontology of
mathematics, the epistemology of mathematics, or positions on
the nature of applied mathematics. For instance, one philosopher
may claim that contradictions are inadmissible because contra-
dictions cannot correctly describe the mathematical domain. An-
other philosopher may hold that contradictions are disastrous
because the only way through which we can come to know most
mathematical propositions is through logical derivation from pre-
viously proved propoistions and every proposition can be derived
from a contradiction. Also some philosophers may object to
contradictions primarily because they can have no application. In


Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
45
RFM, we will find Wittgenstein trying over and over again to get
philosophers and mathematicians to ask themselves why they
abhor contradictions. A second reason why it is philosophically
useless to give a logical analysis of a mathematical notion is that
such an analysis will exhibit connections not recognized in mathe-
matics as it is presently done. But the philosophically interesting
question is: In mathematics do we discover or invent connections?
Merely exhibiting a connection is irrelevant for the philosphical
question. Finally, removal of allegedly philosophically misleading
expressions is useless and may actually be pernicious. Such elimi-
nation is useless because it is not the expressions themselves which
are defective. Their only defect is our defect of putting some in-
correct or unclear philosophical interpretation of their use upon
them. In P/-194 and in 1-125 Wittgenstein wrote:
We pay attention to the expressions we use concerning these things:
we do not understand them, however, but misinterpret them. When
we do philosophy we are like savages, primitive people, who hear the
expressions of civilized men, put a false interpretation on them, and
then draw queer conclusions from it.
The proper thing to do would be to get a clear understanding of
their use, - become civilized -, so that we no longer give the in-
correct or unclear interpretation of their use. Would it not be
foolish to give up the expressions of civilized men for new bar-
barisms? Also why should we think that some ways of expressing
ourselves are philosophically better? Should we not ask ourselves
whether or not we are assuming that some ways of speaking
present a philosophically correct picture of reality? And if so,
should we not justify such an assumption? Wittgenstein himself
believed that many logical analysts incorrectly assumed that we
can get a way of speaking, which will lead us to the right picture
of reality, if we use the notation of mathematical logic. He re-
marked in IV-48:
'Mathematical logic' has completely deformed the thinking of mathe-
maticians and of philosophers by setting up a superficial interpretation
of the forms of our everyday language as an analysis of the structure
of facts.


46
Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
(In chapter IV, section 17, there are more comments on this re-
mark from IV-48.)
27. So one should not expect analysis of mathematical notions
in RFM. But what about work in areas such as improving the
rigour of mathematical proofs and trying to establish the con-
sistency of portions of mathematics? Should not such investiga-
tions be part of a philosophy of mathematics? I would say that a
person is working on the mathematical foundations of mathe-
matics if he is investigating the cogency of proofs, the intercon-
nections of various branches of mathematics, attempting to prove
the consistency, or discussing the completeness, categoricity, or
independence of axioms for all or part of what is called mathe-
matics; and he can accomplish, or believes that he can, his ends
by means of mathematical techniques. I think that passages that
I have cited so far show that Wittgenstein was not at all interested
in what I have just called work on the mathematical foundations
of mathematics. That is to say that he is not interested in these
investigations except insofar as they provide more mathematics
to philosophize about.
So a reader of RFM will not have to struggle with problems in
mathematical logic. Of course, his refusal to become entangled in
problems of logical analysis and logic eliminates labor in coming
