to understand his position only if it is compensated by his coming
to grips with the philosophical issues in a clear way. It may well
turn out that his non-technical way of approaching the philosophic
issues is more demanding than technical problems and less satis-
fying intellectually because it does not have the clarity of mathe-
matical method. Unfortunately, Wittgenstein's approach to these
questions about the philosophic foundations of mathematics is
difficult to follow. Hence, it may be helpful if I give my inter-
pretation of his method of philosophizing about mathematics.
28. How should one try to answer these questions on the
philosophic foundations of mathematics? There are at least two
ways to approach these questions. One way is direct and rather
naive while the other is devious and more sophisticated. But be-
fore I say anything specific about either of these two approaches,
I shall discuss what they have in common. This discussion of what


Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
47
they have in common is partly an elaboration on what we have
already discussed in section 9 but I believe that further discussion
will be helpful towards placing Wittgenstein's method in per-
spective.
One is not going to approach these questions at all unless he
has some problem about, - some wonder about
the ontology,
epistemology, and applications of mathematics. The questions
arise when one has certain beliefs, or biases if you will, about the
ontology, etc., but for some reason realizes that there are diffi-
culties in accepting these beliefs. The man questions, then, or
wonders, how these beliefs can be reconciled with the difficulties.
For instance a philosopher may have the belief that mathematical
statements are about objects. Possibly, the similarity between "Bill
is the son of John and Mary" and "Five is the sum of two and
three" led him to such a belief. But nonetheless he has it. Yet,
for the kind of reason we noted in our discussion of platonism in
section 17 he realizes that mathematical objects would be non-
ordinary. However, he has some doubts about the existence of
non-ordinary objects, let us say, because of their timeless char-
acter. Hence, he has the problem of reconciling his belief, - his
expectation -, that his mathematical statements are about facts
with the apparent possibility that there are no facts for them to
be about. Another philosopher may believe that mathematical
propositions are necessarily true but wonder how this could be
so if all knowledge is gained from experience which only shows
us what is the case and never what must be the case. A third
example of having a problem about the philosophy of mathe-
matics would occur in the case of a man who believed that in
applications of mathematics we regarded ordinary objects as
mathematical objects. Such a man would wonder how mathe-
matics could be applied without distorting what we applied it to.
So in my opinion any philosopher who approaches these questions
approaches them because he has a preconceived idea of how they
should be answered; he has also, however, some doubts about the
correctness of his preconceived answers, and he approaches these
questions to reassess his preconceived answers.
29. How would a philosopher go about reassessing his pre-


48
Problems at the Foundations of
Mathematics
conceived answers? One does not have to use the method I will
suggest but I think that it is the most reasonable one and the most
frequently used method. The method I wish to present will be
more intelligible if I first illustrate it with an example. (I should
also add that I am still talking about what is common to the naive
and sophisticated approaches.)
Assume that a philosopher has the preconceived answer that
mathematical propositions are about non-ordinary objects and
that he wants to reconcile this answer with his skepticism about
the existence of non-ordinary objects. How can he proceed? First,
he would try to find a kind of proposition which he could regard
as a proposition about objects which have most, if not all, the
features which he attributes to mathematical objects. For instance,
he may pick propositions from finite arithmetic or he may pick
propositions about small finite classes. By consideration of this
kind of proposition, he would try to get a clear idea of what it is
like for there to be non-ordinary objects and to convince himself
that there are such. In his efforts to get a clear idea of what it is
like for there to be non-ordinary objects he may have to do some
metaphysics to place these non-ordinary objects amongst the
ordinary objects. To convince himself that there can be these
non-ordinary objects, he may have to revise some of the meta-
physical beliefs which originally led him to be skeptical about the
existence of non-ordinary objects. For instance, he may have to
give up a belief that only observable individuals can exist. If he
is lucky enough to discover a kind of proposition which he is
certain is about non-ordinary objects, the immediate effects of his
reassessment will be in his metaphysical beliefs. To find a place
for non-ordinary objects in his scheme of things, he will reluc-
tantly, but relentlessly, eliminate or alter beliefs which are in-
consistent with there being non-ordinary objects. Of course, if he
cannot find such a kind of proposition, if he cannot find a place
for non-ordinary objects, or if he is simply convinced of his
beliefs which are inconsistent with there being non-ordinary ob-
jects, the result of his reassessment may be either a rejection of
the original answer that there are mathematical objects or a con-
tinuation of the tension of an unsolved philosophical problem.


Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
49
But to continue our example, let us assume that our philosopher
has considered propositions about small finite classes, has ob-
tained a clear idea of the place of such classes in his scheme of
things, and has convinced himself that classes are non-ordinary
objects which exist. Despite all of this, he has only begun to
philosophize about mathematics; he has only selected his para-
digm of a mathematical proposition. His next task is to show how
all mathematical propositions can be construed as being the same
kind of proposition as his paradigm. (I shall explain in section 31
why I use the vague term "construe". In our example, this first
amounts to trying to show how all mathematical propositions can
be re-expressed as propositions about small finite classes. When
our hypothetical philosopher realizes that he cannot re-express
mathematics as propositions about small finite classes he, unless
he wants to give up, will convince himself of the metaphysical
belief that for purposes of ontology all finite classes are the same.
He probably will not have too much difficulty in admitting any
finite class. Soon our hypothetical philosopher will realize that
he needs to admit infinite classes to re-express all mathematical
propositions as propositions about classes. He must then not only
add an axiom of infinity for mathematical purposes, he must also
philosophically reconcile himself to the position that if he admits
finite classes there is no reason why he should hesitate to accept
infinite ones. After he has accepted infinite classes, the antinomies
require him to admit that his concept of what it is like for there
to be a class was not as clear as he had originally believed. As a
matter of fact until he can get a consistency proof of his class
theory revised to avoid the antinomies, he must admit that he
does not have a perfectly clear idea of what it is for there to be
an infinite class. (And if all classes are the same kind of thing,
he must even admit that he does not have a perfectly clear idea
of what it is for there to be a small finite class.) Still, once he has
construed mathematical propositions as being the same kind of
propositions as his paradigm kind, his philosophizing about mathe-
matics is not yet finished. His next task is to see whether or not
he can give adequate answers to the questions in Groups II and
III. (By construing mathematics as class theory, he has already


50
Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
answered the questions of the first group on mathematical on-
tology.)
30. My purpose in sketching this set-theoretic way of ap-
proaching these questions on the philosophical foundations of
mathematics was to illustrate what I think is the most reasonable
and most common approach to these questions - and, not inci-
dentally, the approach taken by Wittgenstein in RFM. But let me
emphasize that Wittgenstein did not try to construe mathematical
propositions as being about sets or classes, be they large or small.
The approach is for one to try to find a kind of proposition, - a
paradigm set of propositions -, such that if mathematical propo-
sitions were like this kind of proposition, then one could see how
his original answers about the ontology, epistemology, or applica-
tions of mathematics could, with or without modification, be true.
Then he would try to show that mathematical propositions are
sufficiently like the paradigm kind of propositions. If by this
method he reached a fairly satisfactory solution to one of the
problems, e.g., problems about ontology, he would then reconsider
his paradigms to see whether or not they made it clear how the
other philosophic questions about mathematics can be satisfac-
torily answered. If reconsideration of one's paradigms did not
provide satisfactory answers to the other questions, he could
either look for a new paradigm kind of proposition or lower his
standards on what is a satisfactory answer. However, in the case
of Wittgenstein (as well as in the case of those who believe that
propositions about classes are suitable paradigms), he had a fairly
good idea that his respective paradigms were paradigms for his
original answers about ontology, epistemology, and applications.
This is so because philosophers rarely restrict their attention to
one issue at a time. However, it is not misleading to regard
philosophers as working in the systematic way that I suggested.
31. Before I turn to a comparison of what I called the direct
and devious approaches, I must say something about my use of
the words "construe" and "sufficiently like". What is it to construe
a mathematical proposition as being of the same kind as a para-
digm kind of proposition? What is it to show that a mathematical
proposition is sufficiently like a paradigm proposition? For in-


Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
51
stance, what is it to show that propositions of mathematics are
sufficiently like propositions about classes or what is it to construe
them as propositions about classes? I think that it is a mistake to
lay down precise criteria for saying that one has construed mathe-
matical propositions as being identical with or sufficiently like a
paradigm kind of proposition. For one, we cannot identify mathe-
matical propositions with anything but mathematical propositions.
We should take seriously Butler's maxim: "A thing is what it is
and nothing else" with which G. E. Moore began his Principia
Ethica. Once we can no longer demand identity, the question of
what degree of similarity we should accept can be a subject of
endless dispute unless we have some specific purpose for saying
that the mathematical propositions are (are not) sufficiently like
a paradigm kind of proposition. Our purpose in saying that a
mathematical proposition is like a paradigm proposition is to
transfer what we say as philosophers about the paradigm back to
the mathematical proposition. So the construing will occur as part
of a philosophical argument. Hence, before we can start to deter-
mine whether or not mathematical propositions are like a phi-
losopher's paradigm we should consider his paradigm, what re-
marks about his paradigm he wants to transfer to mathematics,
and his justification, if any, for transferring the remarks. I think
that trying to lay down criteria on what is a satisfactory analysis
of mathematical propositions, where "analysis" means construe
them as another kind of proposition, would be tantamount to
criticizing arguments before they are presented. In general, and
in philosophy in particular, it is always best to look at the argu-
ments rather than some general considerations on what can and
cannot be done. Still, I think that the following two generaliza-
tions can be made. To construe proposition S as being of the
same kind as proposition Q is to regard the subject matter of S,
the ways of coming to know S, and the uses of S as being of the
same kind as those of Q. If proposition S is sufficiently like prop-
osition Q, then we could have originally done with S what we
now do with Q without ever having been familiar with Q.
32. We can now turn to contrasting the direct and the devious
approaches. But the effort spent in discussing what they have in


52
Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
common was not wasted since it shows how much Wittgenstein's
approach to the philosophy of mathematics has in common with
more standard approaches. A direct and naive approach to these
questions involves developing an ontology and epistemology.
What I call the devious and sophisticated approach involves
trying to answer these questions without developing an ontology
or an epistemology. To illustrate the difference let us return to
the example, in section 29, of a philosopher who tries to develop
a philosophy of mathematics by talking about set-theory. Recall
that at one stage this philosopher had to develop a clear idea of
what it is like for there can be classes and to convince himself
that classes exist. This would be doing ontology. Later on he
would have to become clear about how humans can attain knowl-
edge of necessary truths about classes. This would, of course, be
doing epistemology. It is the doing of the ontology and episte-
mology which can be done directly and naively or deviously and
sophisticatedly. It is done directly and naively if one develops a
theory about the kinds of objects that there are and a theory on
how they can be known. This theory would be such that it would
enable the philosopher to deduce that if mathematical proposi-
tions are sufficiently like the paradigm propositions, then answers
quite a bit like his original answers to the questions would be
correct; he could, at the same time, deduce that his doubts about
the correctness of his original answers could, with minor conces-
sions to them, be dismissed. In our example, a philosopher may
develop a theory on the place of classes amongst the things in
the universe which would enable him to deduce his original
answer that mathematical propositions are about some non-
ordinary objects necessarily connected with one another, while
deducing the incorrectness of his original doubt that we can have
knowledge only about observable objects. In effect, a philosopher
who takes the direct and naive approach treats philosophical
problems as scientific problems on how to explain some phe-
nomenon; he tries to remove wonder by showing that what was
wondered about is exactly what is to be expected according to
the correct theory. (Recall the quotation from Wittgenstein's Blue
Book, in section 12, where he blamed use of the method of


Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
53
science in philosophy as the source of metaphysics.) I call such
an approach direct because such an approach is what seems to be
called for by the questions on mathematical ontology and episte-
mology. I call it naive because it is assumed that we can develop
metaphysical theories. Even if the philosopher calls his theory
meaningless, it is at least assumed that a presentation of it and
use of it will solve the philosophical problems.
However, a sophisticated philosopher, - sophisticated about
the difficulties of metaphysics
who is convinced of the im-
possibility of metaphysics may try to accomplish the same goal
as the naive philosopher. The common goal is the removal of
wonder. But he will try to attain it in a devious manner. He may
do everything the same as the naive philosopher except for the
stating of the metaphysics, i.e., stating his ontology and episte-
mology. For instance, a philosopher taking the devious approach
may use set theory in the following way. He would take a standard
set-theory, such as von Neumann's, and then by talking about it
would try to suppress any wonder about mathematical objects,
epistemology, and applications. But he would take care to sup-
press wonder without stating any metaphysical theory. Most likely
he would answer questions about the existence, nature, and con-
nections of mathematical objects by discussing examples of classes
and by pointing out how classes are talked about in set-theory.
For instance, he may convince someone of the existence of classes
by convincing him that a pair of shoes is different from each shoe.
But he would stop after convincing the person that a pair of shoes
is not a shoe; he would not state that classes exist. He may
convince someone that infinite classes exist by pointing out that
in set theory an infinite set is said to exist; but he himself will not
explicitly assert that an infinite set exists. I am not suggesting that
any philosopher who took the devious approach would try to
philosophize about mathematics by talking about set-theory; I am
sure that he would not. I simply want to show that an essential
feature of the devious approach is not talking about ordinary
language as opposed to a formal language. As I see it, the essen-
tial difference between the direct and devious approaches is that
the first uses philosophical propositions while the second will not


54
Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
utter them. The direct approach removes wonder by explaining
why the thing wondered at occurs. The devious approach tries to
describe, - gets us to look at
the thing wondered about from
many points of view until it no longer seems something to be
wondered about.
33. There is no doubt that in his Blue Book, PI, and RFM
Wittgenstein took the devious approach. This is why he wrote in
PI-133:
The real discovery is the one that makes me capable of stopping doing
philosophy when I want to. - The one that gives philosophy peace, so
that it is no longer tormented by questions which bring itself in ques-
tion. - Instead, we now demonstrate a method, by examples, and the
series of examples can be broken off. - Problems are solved (diffi-
culties eliminated, not a single problem.
There is not a philosophical method, though there are indeed
methods, like different therapies.
I take him to mean that once a philosopher has removed wonder
about one issue he should be able to stop philosophizing about
that issue. He can stop if the removal of wonder did not require
the development of a metaphysical theory which itself requires
justification. If his removal of wonder required a metaphysical
theory, his removal of wonder is contingent upon how well his
metaphysical theory solves other problems since his metaphysical
theory will at least connect several problems, if it does not unite
all philosophical problems into the single problem of finding the
correct metaphyhics. But worst of all: If the removal of his wonder
requires the development of a metaphysical theory, the notorious
difficulties in metaphysics may lead the philosopher to despair of
ever removing the wonder. So the gist of what Wittgenstein meant
by saying that a philosopher should be able to stop philosophizing
is that after a philosopher solves a particular problem he should
not have to worry about the ramifications of a metaphysical theory
devised to solve the problem. I do not, despite the fact that he
calls philosophy a therapy, believe that Wittgenstein thought that
the goal of philosophy was to stop men from philosophizing,
where by "philosophizing" I mean the removal of wonder not
removable by the explanations of natural science.


Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
55
34. What is the correct approach? The direct approach is the
natural approach. It is natural to answer direct questions directly.
In particular, it is natural to answer ontological and epistemolog-
ical questions by saying something such as: "There are not these
kinds of objects because there can only be these other kinds" or
"This kind of thing can be known because there are such and
such objects and the mind has such and such capabilities." I
think that the only reason that one has for taking the devious
approach is despair over the possibility of metaphysics. But I
think that it is a mistake to try to determine which approach is
correct. A philisopher's approach is part of his argument and I
believe it is best not to evaluate an argument until it is completely
laid out. Without developing any metaphysics, Wittgenstein may
remove our wonder about how it can seem so certain that mathe-
matical truths are truths about objects in necessary connections
while at the same time it can seem so reasonable to deny pla-
tonism. But we cannot tell until we see his arguments. Even if we
do discover a metaphysical theory behind Wittgenstein's philoso-
phyzing about mathematics, this does not mean that his solutions
are defective. It means only that his declared method of philoso-
phizing is not adequate for solving philosophical problems. It
may even turn out that his implicit metaphysics, if he had one,
when developed is the correct metaphysics. I think the fact, if it
is a fact, that Wittgenstein's approach is inadequate is unimpor-
tant. The important thing is whether or not his solutions to
particular philosophical problems remove wonder about one area
without producing wonder about another. So, since the important
things to study are Wittgenstein's actual philosophizing, we shall
turn to his paradigms which exhibit his original answers to ques-
tions about mathematical objects and necessity. But before we
consider his paradigms in Chapter II, let us return to a con-
sideration of the role of "I must have miscalculated."
35. The role of a sentence is its function, its implications, and
criteria for correct use. Questions about the ontology, episte-
mology, and even applications of mathematics arise when we ask
about the function, implications, and criteria of "I must have
miscalculated." For instance, assume that one tries to solve: x + y


56
Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
= 13 and x = 3y. But somehow he concludes that y = 2.25 and
x = 10.75. (Very likely his error would be due to careless writing
which made a 3 look like a 2.) However, it might have occurred,
a check of his result shows that 3 x (2.25) is not 10.75. He may
then say "I must have miscalculated." What is the function of, or
purpose in, admitting that he miscalculated? Although, one is
saying that his answer is unacceptable, he is clearly saying more
than that. A simple claim that it is unacceptable would suffice to
say that it is unacceptable. The admission of miscalculation, when
an unacceptable result is obtained, reflects a belief of our culture
that not everything anyone happens to calculate can be considered
correct. It also reveals the belief that the correct mathematical
answers can be arrived at by correct thinking, or at least the belief
that incorrect ones are arrived at by incorrect thinking. Once we
try to understand the function of "I must have miscalculated",
we are trying to understand why we believe that there is a
mathematical truth to be known by some correct method. We are
also trying to understand what this truth is and what this way of
knowing is. Such an investigation will surely involve asking the
questions in Groups I and II. If our miscalculation occurred in
applied mathematics, we could go on to consider whether or not
our mistake was about the facts to which the mathematics was
applied or in the mathematics itself. Such a line of thought may
lead us to try to find the difference between applied and non-
applied mathematics. And this is the intent of the third Group of
questions. An implication of "I must have miscalculated" is that
at some stage in one's work one did something called miscalcu-
lating. To try to understand what miscalculating is will certainly
involve asking the questions about mathematical ontology and
epistemology. For instance, what is one thinking incorrectly about
when he miscalculates? Or, how can one even think when he
miscalculates since if mathematical truths are necessary truths
mathematical falsehoods would seem to be unthinkable absurdi-
ties. Another implication is that once the miscalculation occurred
further mistakes necessarily followed. A question about this im-
plication would be: What is mathematical thinking like so that
further results are determined by a given result? Of course, if we


Problems at the Foundations of Mathematics
57
ask about the criteria for correctly using "I must have miscalcu-
lated" we are immediately concerned with mathematical episte-
mology. So since an attempt to understand the role of "I must
have miscalculated" leads us to ask the questions which provide
a philosophic foundation of mathematics, it is not rash to call
such an attempt a key to the foundations, - philosophic founda-
tions -, of mathematics.
36. This may have been the key that Wittgenstein actually
used. He frequently reminded himself that calculations are not
experiments on what results we or others happen to get. This
shows that he was trying to understand the belief that mathe-
matical truth is objective but yet not descriptive of behavior. We
just saw that a function of "I must have miscalculated" is to
express this belief. Over and over again, he tried to become clear
about what it meant to say that one step in a calculation proceeds
necessarily from another. As a matter of fact RFM begins with
such a problem. We have just seen that such an issue arises when
we try to understand one of the implications of "I must have
miscalculated." In passages such as 1-112 and 1-116 Wittgenstein
showed that he was very concerned with understanding how we
can think a mathematical falsehood. In IV-28, he questioned the
use of reductio ad absurdum proofs because use of them requires
us to assume a mathematical falsehood. In V-19, he went so far
as to claim that his main difficulty with Godel's Incompleteness
Proof lay in trying to understand how we could assume a mathe-
matical falsehood. We have just seen that we would have to under-
stand how we can think mathematical falsehoods if we are to
understand the role of "I must have miscalculated." And as the
cited passages show, Wittgenstein was concerned with this issue
of how we can think what is mathematically false. So, Wittgen-
stein may have actually used this key in his philosophy of mathe-
matics although I must admit that his remarks do not contain any
explicit study of "I must have miscalculated."


n
STRICT FINITISM
The proposition 'a=a', 'p D p', "The word 'Bismarck'
has 8 letters", "There is no such thing as reddish-
green", are all obvious and are propositions about es-
sence: what have they in common? They are evidently
each of a different kind and differently used. The last
but one is most like an empirical proposition. And it
can understandably be called a synthetic a priori prop-
osition.
It can be said: unless you put the series of numbers
and the series of letters side by side, you cannot know
how many letters the word has.
(HI-39)
1. In the preceding Chapter, I suggested that a philosopher who
believed that mathematical sentences were used to express neces-
sary truths but could not be used in non-applied mathematics to
state facts, may begin to answer the questions about the philoso-
phic foundations of mathematics in the following way. Very likely
he would be motivated to ask the questions because he realizes
that someone can make a good case for platonism. In order to
vindicate, and possibly modify, his non-platonistic position, he
would select a kind of sentence with the following properties. He
could say about a typical sentence of this kind that it is used to
state a necessary truth which is not a statement of fact. (The kind
of sentence which he picks is his paradigm kind of sentence while
the particular sentence which he refers to is his paradigm sen-
tence.) Under the assumption that mathematical propositions are
expressed with sentences of his paradigm kind, he will try to give
satisfactory non-platonistic answers to the three groups of ques-
tions on the philosophic foundations of mathematics. "Satisfac-


Strict Finitism
59
tory" means that he can give clear answers to all of the questions
and his answers do not have undesirable philosophic conse-
quences. Of course, the paradigm kind of sentence should be
sufficiently like the sentences used to make mathematical state-
ments so that the philosophy developed by talking about the
paradigm kind of sentence can be regarded as a philosophy of
mathematics.
2. In this Chapter, my primary aim is to develop a philosophy
of mathematics which originates from an attempt to preserve the
belief that mathematical statements assert necessary truths but are
not statements of fact. (I should repeat that I mean by "statement
of fact" a statement with truth conditions.) This philosophy will
be developed by taking "The word 'Bismarck' has 8 letters" as a
paradigm of its paradigm kind of sentence. I shall call this phi-
losophy of mathematics: strict finitism. (I follow Kreisel who
called Wittgenstein's philosophy of mathematics strict finitism in
his "Wittgenstein's Remarks on the Foundations of Mathematics"
in the August, 1958 issue of the British Journal of the Philosophy
of Science. And I believe that I am developing Wittgenstein's
philosophy of mathematics in this chapter. On p. 505 of BP,
Dummett reports that Hao Wang suggested calling this philosophy
"anthropologism". I prefer "strict finitism".) However, in this
Chapter, my primary aim will not be to argue that Wittgenstein
held the philosophy developed here, although I shall cite passages
which suggest that I am developing insights of Wittgenstein. In
Chapter III, I shall be primarily concerned with supporting my
interpretation of Wittgenstein as a strict finitist. I should also
mention that, in this Chapter, I shall not discuss the legitimacy of
assuming that mathematical sentences are sufficiently like the
paradigms of strict finitists so that strict finitism can be regarded
as a philosophy of all of mathematics. We shall discuss the issue
of the comprehensiveness of strict finitism in the last chapter
when we criticize Wittgenstein's philosophy. But here our aim is
to understand strict finitism. To gain this understanding we shall
consider "The word 'Bismarck' has 8 letters" and other sentences
of this kind.
3. Let us first rewrite "The word 'Bismarck' has 8 letters" as


60
Strict Finitism
"The number of letters in 'Bismarck' is 8." Let us call the re-
written version B. I rewrite the sentence because in the rewritten
version it sounds more like a mathematical statement expressing
the value of a function, and I do want to suggest that B is very
much like a mathematical sentence. Also in the rewritten version
the "is" is naturally read as the "is" of identity. In the original
version one could easily read the "is" as attributing the property
of having 8 letters to the word "Bismarck". I want the paradigm
kind of sentence to contain the "is" of identity, i.e., be equations,
because if the "is" of identity is not the only copula in mathe-
matics it is certainly the most frequent.
After we have rewritten our original sentence as B, we shall
convince ourselves that B is necessarily true. Or, I could just as
well have said that we shall prove B. In this essay, by "proving a
sentence P" I mean the process of establishing that what P is used
to assert must be true. Then we shall analyze what we have gone
through when we proved B. But the steps of the analysis will be
illustrated by reference to a proof of another strict finitist paradigm
considered by Wittgenstein in 111-50 and 51. The second para-
digm is: The reverse of the letters in OVER is REVO. After this
analysis, we shall develop the philosophy of strict finitism by
answering the three groups of questions. The answers will be
illustrated by reference to yet another paradigm sentence. The
third strict finitistic paradigm is: The sum of two and three is
five. The third paradigm will show that strict finitism can at least
construe some arithmetic sentences as being of their paradigm
kind. Let us now turn to proving B.
4. Is it true that the number of letters in "Bismarck" is 8 and
is it necessarily true? Of course, it is true. Still, taking a close
look at how we establish that it is true is helpful in establishing
that it is necessarily true. How do we establish that B is true?
Again the answer is easy: We count the letters in a token of the
type "Bismarck". (I follow what I believe is standard philosophical
usage in calling an instance of a word actually written down a
TOKEN of the word and the word which we say the instance is a
token of the TYPE.) Let us take a closer look at this counting. One
way of doing this counting is to do it in three stages. See Picture 1.


Strict Finitism
61
Stage 1: Bismarck
Stage 2:
B i s m a r c k
I II I M i l
Stage 3:
B i s m a r c k
I II I M i l
1 2 3 4 5 6 7 8
The first stage is the production of a typical token of "Bismarck".
The second stage is an application of the operation: taking the
number of, to the typical token of "Bismarck". In the third stage,
we apply a numerical concept to the result of having applied the
operation: taking the number of. (In effect, all counting of ele-
ments in configurations is like this; only rarely so simple-minded.
If we would have counted aloud, we would have visualized a
token of "Bismarck" or looked at an actual token, made eight
sounds while correlating each sound with a letter and a number
word. For instance, saying the word "four" is correlating the
sound we make when we say "four" with the number word
"four".) We could say that what was done in Stages 1 through 3
was an experiment to determine how many letters the word "Bis-
marck" contains. If we call it an experiment, we can call Picture
1, and especially Stage 3, a picture of how such an experiment
would almost always come out. As a result of this experiment we
possess as a contingent empirical truth: "The number of letters
in 'Bismarck' is 8."
5. But we have not yet proved B. Or better, we have not yet
shown that we can transform the empirical "The number of letters
in 'Bismarck' is 8" into the non-empirical "The number of letters
in 'Bismarck' MUST BE 8." In 11-30 Wittgenstein wrote: "A proof
leads me to say: this MUST be like this." In reading Wittgenstein
it is helpful to interpret "proving" and "calculating" as naming
processes by which we determine that what a sentence says must
be true in addition to merely being true. His "calculate" does not
refer to the performance of arithmetric manipulations. I would
go so far as to say that in KFM he uses "prove" and "calculate"
synonymously. Of course, the experiment itself cannot be the


62
Strict Finitism
proof or calculation since it tells us only what is the case. How-
ever, the experiment will play an essential role in the proof or
calculation. In V-15 he remarked: "A calculation - it might be
said - is perhaps part of the technique of an experiment, but it is
by itself not an experiment." (That calculating is not experimenting
is one of the few positions that Wittgenstein argues for in what
could be called the standard way philosophers argue. For instance,
see 11-65 to 11-76 and V-15.) So the problem of transforming the
experimentally justified B into a proved proposition remains.
6. Picture 1 already is part of the proof; it is the proof pattern.
In V-15 Wittgenstein remarked: "the calculation is the picture of
an experiment as it practically always turns out". We just noted
that Picture 1 pictures how an experiment to determine the num-
ber of words in "Bismarck" would almost always turn out. How-
ever, we cannot really say that Picture 1 by itself is the proof or
calculation. By itself it is only a picture of the technique which
we used to establish B experimentally. We must make use of or
look at this pattern in a certain way in order to have a proof of B.
In 11-11, Wittgenstein explicitly claimed: "the pattern is not the
proof". And in 11-22 he wrote:
The proof (the pattern of the proof) shews us the result of a procedure
(the construction); and we are convinced that a procedure regulated
in this way always leads to this configuration.
In 11-24 he added: "The proof must be our model, our picture,
of how these operations have a result." I should point out that
in 11-22 and 11-24, Wittgenstein was writing about proving meta-
linguistic propositions of the form: This sequence of formulae
from axiom system A is a formal proof of formula F. We shall
see in sections 92 and 118 it is not inappropriate to take these
remarks about meta-mathematical proofs as applying to any
strict
finitistic
proof.
So, although Wittgenstein
sometimes
wrote as if Picture 1 itself could be a proof, I submit that he
really meant that pictures like Picture 1 were patterns for proofs.
To get a proof from a pattern certain mental conditions must
obtain. For instance, people must be convinced that the picture
of what happens when an operation is applied to a figure is


Strict Finitism
63
suitable as a model of what it is like to apply this operation to
such figures. Let us now go on to develop a proof of B from
Picture 1.
7. Call the typical token of "Bismarck" in Picture 1 X. (If
one wants to be pedantic about the use of "token", call the token
of "Bismarck" in stage 3 X. However, I intended the stages of
Picture 1 to represent various stages of dealing with a single
token of "Bismarck". Stage 3 shows us that it is empirically true
that the number of letters in X is 8. But inspection of Picture 1
establishes more than this empirical truth. From Stage 3 of Picture
1 we can read-off that the number of letters in X must be 8. (I
use read-off because Wittgenstein used "read-off" in this way.
See RFM 11-24.) Why do I claim that we can read-off that X
must have 8 letters? It is inconceivable that X could be the token
that it is and have more or less than 8 letters. If I am wrong
about the inconceivability of X being a "Bismarck" token and
having more or less than 8 letters, the simplest way to refute me
is to draw another number of strokes, say 7 or 9, and picture
how they can be put in one-to-one correspondence with the letters
of X. Although you may not put it this way, you would agree
with Wittgenstein's remark in 1-45: "A demon has cast a spell
around this position", e.g., having 9 letters, "and excluded it
from our space."
At first glance, before one counts the letters in X and before
one considers whether or not there can be more or less letters in
X, one may think that X need not have 8 letters. Similarly, one
may think that X need not be printed in the color in which it is
printed. However, the facts are that we can think of X being a
"Bismarck" token even if it is another color while we cannot think
of it being one with more letters or less letters than 8. Similarly,
at first glance one may not think that a red cube must have 12
edges to be a cube. Yet reflection established that it is incon-
ceivable that this particular red cube be a cube without its 12
edges; although we can easily think of it being blue. In the case
of the cube, reflection did not show us only that it is inconceiv-
able that a cube have 12 edges and not have 12 edges. Reflection
showed us that the particular cube could not be the figure that it


64
Strict
Finitism
is without 12 edges. Similarly, I am not merely claiming that it
is inconceivable that X has 8 letters and X does not have 8 letters.
I am making the much stronger claim that it is inconceivable
that X has any amount of letters other than 8. (I shall argue in
section 10 that "We cannot conveive of a token of 'Bismarck'
having more or less than 8 letters" is not equivalent to "We can-
not conceive of a token of 'Bismarck' having both 8 letters and
simultaneously not having 8 letters.")
However, from observation of what is the case with X, we can
read-off a general necessary truth in addition to the singular
necessary truth that X must have 8 letters. Since X is a typical
token of "Bismarck", observation of and reflection on Stage 3
enables us to read-off, or intuitively induce, as a necessary truth
the general statement: For any X if X is a token of "Bismarck",
X has 8 letters. Or, since this general statement is equivalent to
B, we can say that observation of and reflection on Stage 3 has
resulted in a proof of B.
8. We can look at what we have done in establishing B as
necessarily true in another, and I think illuminating, way. We
could say that on the basis of what we see in Stage 3, we establish
an implicatory link between the concepts "being a 'Bismarck'
configuration" and "containing 8 letters". In suggesting this I
follow Wittgenstein's suggestion in V-46 where he wrote: "The
mathematical Must is only another expression of the fact that
mathematics forms concepts." In V-49 he specified what he
meant by forming a new concept when he wrote:
"To give a new concept" can only mean to introduce a new employ-
ment of a concept, a new practice.
We could say that in the proof of B we legitimatized the practice
of using "having 8 letters" as a necessary condition for applying
the concept "Bismarck - configuration". Thus following Wittgen-
stein's specification of what it is to introduce a new concept, we
could say that in the proof of B we establish a new concept of
"Bismarck".
I would not object to a claim that in mathematical proofs we
establish new conceptual connections, and hence new concepts,


Strict Finitism
65
and that mathematical sentences are used to state what connec-
tions we have established. However, Wittgenstein hesitated to
make such a claim. In V-33 he mused:
Is a new conceptual connexion a new concept? And does mathematics-
create conceptual connexions?
The word "concept" is by far too vague.
Mathematics teaches us to operate with concepts in a new way.
And hence it can be said to change the way we work with concepts.
In V-49, he again commented that "concept" is a vague concept.
I attribute Wittgenstein's hesitation about making such a claim to
his approach to philosophy. Recall from our discussion in Chap-
ter I, section 32, that he did not want to burden himself with
a philosophical theory in order to understand a non-philosophical
practice. In this case, he did not want to burden himself with a
theory of concepts in order to understand proving. In this case,
he is probably justified in avoiding such theorizing, since it is as
he said, viz., "concept" is vague.
9. Let us follow Wittgenstein's suggestion in the opening
quotation of this Chapter. Let us call necessary truths such as
those discussed above: essential truths or statements of essence.
The singular ones will state essential features of individuals and
the general ones, such as B AFTER IT IS PROVED, state essential
features of kinds of things. I suggest that we regard them as
statements of essence for the following reasons. First, I think that
if any statements could be called statements of essence, these
can. The singular ones tell us of a feature an individual must have
to be of the kind that it is. Since, if an individual were not of the
kind that it is, it would not be the individual that it is; these
singular essential truths tell us of a feature that an individual must
have to be what it is. The general ones tell us of a feature that any
member of a kind must have to be of that kind. Secondly, by
regarding them as statements of essence, we can better under-
stand remarks such as "The mathematian creates essence" from
1-32. It will also help us understand two passages from his Phil-
osophical Investigations. These passages are: "Essence is ex-
pressed by grammar" from PI-371 and "Grammar tells us what
kind of object anything is. (Theology as grammar.)" After the


66
Strict Finitism
proof of B, there is an implicatory link between the concepts
"being a Bismarck" and "having 8 letters", this link can be called
a grammatical link, and knowledge of this link tells us in part
what a "Bismarck" is. So it is not eccentric to say that a proof
of B results in a new bit of grammar and that knowledge of this
bit of grammar is knowledge of what a "Bismarck" is. But I am
not suggesting that one could fix the grammar of "God" in the
same way as we can fix the grammar of "Bismarck-configura-
tion".
10. Let us also convince ourselves of, or at least try to ap-
preciate, Wittgenstein's claim in the opening quotation that B as
A PROVED PROPOSITION is synthetic a priori. As it stands B is not
analytic. It does not have the form of a logical truth any more
than "The number of Tom's children is 8" has the form of a
logical truth. Of course, a person who is convinced that the only
necessity is logical necessity will not be impressed by the fact
that, as it stands, B does not have the form of a logical truth.
Such an opponent of the synthetic a priori may claim that B can
never be proved, i.e., B can never be more than an extremely well
justified contingent empirical truth about words. Or else, he may
argue that when properly analyzed B would be rewritten in a
form which is the form of a logical truth. In face of the incon-
ceivability of the denial of B, it is sheer dogmaticism to claim
that B cannot be proved. (Recall that I am using "proved" to
mean established as necessarily true.) So our opponent of the
synthetic a priori could honestly argue only that when properly
analyzed B would become analytic. In the proposed proper anal-
ysis "Bismarck-configuration" would be defined so that B would
be transformed into an analytic sentence which may read like the
following. "The number of letters in a word which has amongst
other features 8 letters is 8." Such an analysis can be given but it
will not show that B is really analytic. For one: there is no defini-
tion of "Bismarck-configuration." So one shall have to be made
up. In order to make up such a definition of "Bismarck-configu-
ration", we have to recognize essential features of "Bismarck" in
order to know what to put into the definition. Recognition of
essential features requires recognition of the very kind of neces-


Strict Finitism
67
sary truths which the definition is supposed to show are eliminable.
For instance, how would one know that a definition of "Bismarck-
configuration" should be such that it states or implies that the
number of letters in any token of "Bismarck" is 8? I believe that
a proof such as that which we developed from Picture 1 would
be necessary to determine that a definition of "Bismarck-con-
figuration" must state or imply that "Bismarck" has 8 letters.
Even if one accepted the conventions for spelling "Bismarck" as
a definition of "Bismarck-configuration", one would still not be
able to avoid appeal to essential truths in order to transform B
into an analytic sentence. The conventional rules for spelling
"Bismarck" do not contain an explicit provision that we write
down 8 letters. To know that these conventions do imply that
"Bismarck" has 8 letters, we would have to follow these conven-
tions to construct a token of "Bismarck", and then do as we did
in Picture 1, viz., read-off B as an essential truth. In general, any
configuration of letters or simple structure can have the term
standing for it defined only after we have determined what is
essential to the structure. So use of definitions will not enable
one to show that sentences like B are really analytic.
This brings us to another reason for saying that B is not really
analytic. B can also be used to state an experimentally justified
claim about the number of letters in any token of "Bismarck".
If someone did not try to think of B being false, he would regard
B as nothing more than an experimentally justified truth about a
word. If B were really analytic, one would have to hold that B is
never really suitable for being used to make experimental state-
ments. But it can be used to state the result of an experiment;
thus B is not really or implicitly analytic. A third reason for
saying that B, when looked at as necessarily true, is synthetic
a priori is that our way of establishing its necessity seems very
much like the way Kant says that we would establish a synthetic
a priori judgment. (In Section 2 of his Prolegomena to Any Future
Metaphysics, Kant claimed that to establish 7 +5 = 12 as syn-
thetic a priori we have to inspect empirical situations for applying
"five added to seven".) I think that my arguments about definition
show that an analysis of the concept of being a "Bismarck" will


68
Strict Finitism
not show us that B must be true. However, if we represent to
visible intuition, i.e., sight, a situation for applying "Bismarck-
configuration" and "having 8 letters" we can judge that these two
concepts are connected because of what our sensuous intuition,
- again sight -, shows us in Picture 1.
I am not, of course, claiming that a strict finitist will also hold
the Kantian positions that space and time are forms of sensuous
intuition. The Kantian positions on space and time are philo-
sophical theories to account for and justify what is done in a strict
finitistic proof. A strict finitist can avoid these positions by either
advancing different philosophical theories or by trying to phi-
losophize without the development of any philosophical theory.
In other words, I am not suggesting that the Kantian and the
strict-finitist would give the same answer to: How is pure mathe-
matics possible? I am only suggesting that they would agree on
how a kind of judgment, called synthetic a priori by Kant, is
established. But agreement on this last point vindicates calling
propositions proved by a strict finitists "synthetic a priori". So, I
hope that I have shown the appropriateness of Wittgenstein's
suggestion that after a proof B is synthetic a priori.
11. Let us now turn to an analysis of what we did when we
gave a strict finitist proof of B. Or as we have just seen, we could
say that we want to analyze what we did when we established
that having 8 letters was of the essence of "Bismarck" as well as
saying that we want to analyze what we did when we made the
synthetic a priori judgment that B. We shall go through the
analysis by analyzing what we do when we give a strict finitistic
proof of "The reverse of OVER is REVO" rather than analyzing
our proof of B. In this way we get a better idea of the range of
sentences in a strict finitist's paradigm kind of sentence. This is
helpful since there is no precise characterization of what kind of
subject matter strict finitistic paradigm sentences are about. Al-
though I suspect that all, or most, strict finitistic sentences have,
or can be given, the form: The X of Y is Z. Also the necessity of
"The reverse of OVER is REVO" is even more obvious than
that of B. So its proof is even more suitable for an analysis of
how we establish necessity.


Strict Finitism
69
12. The opening sentence of the preceding paragraph shows
that I am using "strict finitist" ambiguously. In some cases, I use
"strict finitism" to stand for a philosophy of mathematics. In
other cases, I use it to stand for a way of doing mathematics. The
way of doing mathematics that I call "Strict finitistic" is that
portion of mathematics of which a strict finitist as a philosopher
can give an account. I believe that throughout this essay it will
be clear from the context whether I am talking about a kind of
mathematics or a philosophy of mathematics.
13. Let us now turn to an analysis of a proof of "The reverse
of OVER is REVO". (I shall abbreviate this sentence as RO.)
The proof pattern of RO is in Picture 2 below.
14. The first stage in a strict finitistic proof of a proposition
of the form: "The X of Y is Z" is to construct, or represent to
our senses or imagination, a typical instance of the operand or
Y term. So at Stage 1 of Picture 2 we construct a typical OVER.
But not just any OVER will do.
The typical instance of the operand, - in our case the typical
token of OVER -, must be perspicuous. To be perspicuous a
figure must meet certain requirements of size, simplicity, and
state of motion. If a figure is perspicuous, it is not so large that
we must use inductive reasoning to identify it as being an instance
of the operand term; nor is it so large that we would have to use
inductive reasoning to determine that we have actually applied to
Stage 1:
OVER
Stage 2:
OVER
Stage 3:


70
Strict Finitism
it the operation which we intended to apply to it. For instance,
if an OVER had each letter as large as a sky-scraper, it would
not be perspicuous unless we viewed it from a great distance.
(But if we viewed it from a great distance, we would have a
reasonably small OVER before us.) If we were to stand close to
one of the sky-scraper sized letters, we could not see all the
letters together as a group; thus we would have to use inductive
reasoning to determine that this huge letter is in an OVER. Cer-
tainly, with letters of this size, we could use only inductive
reasoning to determine whether a rearrangement of them was a
reversal of them. Similarly, a perspicuous figure is not so small
that we need to use inductive reasoning to know what kind of
figure it is or to know whether some manipulation of it is the
manipulation we intended to perform or have performed. Closely
connected with the requirements on its size is the requirement
that a perspicuous figure not be so complex that we cannot, with-
out induction, identify it or the operations we apply to it. This
last requirement is connected with size because often an enlarge-
ment will produce the required simplicity. (Of course, the enlarged
figure is a new figure for the first stage of the proof; it is not the
complicated small one.) What I mean by saying that a certain
state of motion is required for perspicuity is that the figure must
not be undergoing physical changes so that we need to use induc-
tive reasoning to identify it or operations we apply to it. For
instance, if an OVER were whirling around in a circle, we could
not directly, - with a single look -, identify it as an OVER.
It may help to cite some sentences in which the operand is not
perspicuous for being of a kind and for the operation talked about.
Wittgenstein's 11-16 suggests two examples, although he does not
use these examples as I use them here. The examples are: "The
number of peas in this vessel is the number of his shillings" and
"The number of letters in the Song of Songs is the number of his
shillings." We cannot tell with absolute certainty that we have
counted all the peas in a vessel. We cannot tell without induction
that any given printing of the Song of Songs has every letter in it
that should be in it. Again, we can never be absolutely certain
that we have counted every letter when we try to count the letters


Strict Finitism
71
in a copy of the Song of Songs. So the peas in a vessel are not
perspicuous for the operation of counting them, unless there are
only a few. And also, no copy of the Song of Songs is perspicuous
for being of the kind: copy of the Song of Songs. (Wittgenstein
cites these examples to remind us that although referring to num-
bers in these ways is worthless for mathematical purposes, i.e.,
giving proofs, they are legitimate expressions of empirical data.)
A lump of ground beef provides another example of an object
that is not perspicuous for being one of a kind and for a certain
operation. Consider the sentence: "The number of golf-ball size
meatballs formable from a pound of hamburger is 8." Certainly,
one cannot tell without induction that a lump of ground-beaf is a
pound of hamburger. Nor can one be absolutely certain that when
he forms meatballs he is forming golf-ball size ones. Another
value of citing these examples is that it shows us that not all sen-
tences can be made into necessary truths by strict finitistic proofs.
I should note that my main textual clue in developing this
concept of "perspicuity", as well as those of "memorability", and
"surveyability" came from the last paragraph of 11-39 where he
wrote:
it must not be necessary to make a physical investigation of the proof-
configuration in order to shew us what has been proved.
I also followed a similar clue from 111-41.
Why does a strict finitist need perspicuity? A strict finitist, - as
a mathematician
wants to establish a claim of the form: "The
X of Y MUST be Z." And he wants to establish this by reading it
off from a picture showing that the X of a particular Y is a Z.
If he is not certain that he has a Y in his picture he, of course,
will not see from that picture what the X of Y must be. For
instance, looking at something you are not sure is an OVER will
not give good evidence what the reverse of an OVER is, let alone
show you what it MUST be. Similarly, if you could doubt that a
procedure you pictured as taking the X of Y really was taking
the X of Y, the picture would not convince you of much about
the X of Ys.
To me it seems appropriate to compare perspicuous figures


72
Strict Finitism
with sense data. They are like sense data insofar as their prop-
erties which interest us are concerned. Their seeming to have
them is sufficient for their having them. And their seeming not
to have them is sufficient for saying that they do not have them.
Thus a very large OVER does not seem at a glance to be an
OVER, so as a perspicuous figure it is not one. Also if a figure
seemed, to all who looked at it, to be an OVER, it would suffice
as an OVER for a proof. However, perspicuous figures are not
like sense data insofar as they are not objects posited to solve
epistemological problems.
15. Wittgenstein's criterion for perspicuity was ease of repro-
duction. In II-1, regarding his claim that a mathematical proof
must be perspicuous, he commented:
'A mathematical proof must be perspicuous.' Only a structure whose
reproduction is an easy task is called a "proof." It must be possible
to decide with certainty whether we really have the same proof twice
over, or not. The proof must be a configuration whose exact repro-
duction can be certain.
If we can be certain that a figure F-2 is a reproduction of another
figure F-l, we must be certain about what F-l was and certain
about what F-2 is. If we are certain about what the figures are,
we have found this out without induction. Now, knowing what
these figures are cannot, in this context, be knowing every feature
of these figures or even very many of their properties. For in-
stance, we cannot tell without induction the exact color of an O,
its exact size, or its curvature although we can be certain that it is
an O. Wittgenstein realized this because he continued II-1:
Or again: we must be sure we can exactly reproduce what is essential
to the proof. It may for example be written in two different hand-
writings or colours. What goes to make the reproduction of a proof
is not anything like an exact reproduction of a shade of colour or a
hand-writing.
For instance, both a red OVER and a blue OVER are repro-
ductions of what we have at Stage 1 of Picture 2, as long as we


Strict Finitism
73
can be certain that we still have OVERs and can be certain that
the operation we call their reverse really is their reverse, because
these are the features essential to a proof of RO. So I submit that
knowing what figures are, when we are talking about a proof,
is knowing that they are figures of a certain kind and being able
to identify with certainty that an operation we do apply to them
is the operation referred to in the sentence to be proved. Hence,
I conclude that Wittgentsein's criterion for perspicuous figure is
the same as mine.
In V-17 there is a discussion of perspicuous figures which is
very much like mine. But I must admit that in V-17 as well as in
II-1 he was talking about the entire proof pattern being perspi-
cuous instead of applying "perspicuous" only to the paradigm of
the operand. However, in section 20 we will see that this differ-
ence with Wittgenstein is not serious.
16. The second stage of a proof of RO is experimental. We
experiment to find out what does happen if we put the last letter
of OVER in first place, the second to the last in second place,
etc. (In Picture 2, I picture the experimental stage as Stage 2. In
actual practice Stage 2 and Stage 3 would be indiscernible since
when you experimented as to what results when you put last first
etc., you would actually write down the letters in reverse order.
Still there is a difference between Stage 2 and Stage 3. The differ-
ence is that Stage 2 pictures getting the result experimentally
while Stage 3 pictures describing the experimentally obtained
result.) Stage 2 is experimental in the sense that the immediate
consequence of this stage is only that we see that a certain result
does follow rearranging the letters. We could stay at this empirical
stage and then, after we described our experimentally obtained
result, settle for RO as a mere empirical truth.
17. At this stage it is appropriate to discuss the concept of
memorability, since a proof pattern gets the feature of memora-
bility from the kind of operation performed at the experimental
stage. It is the picture that we draw of our experimenting that
must be memorable. A picture we draw of our experimenting, on
what happens if we apply a certain operation, is memorable if
we can tell without induction that what we have pictured is the


74
Strict
Finitism
operation we intended to picture. For instance, Stages 2 and 3 of
Picture 2 are memorable pictures of reversing letters because once
we write the lines down to represent reversal there can be no doubt
that they show reversal. But |
s
~| is not a memorable
picture of what we get if we apply an operation such as: The result
of drawing a ? through the center of
is
(This rectangle example comes from 1-70.) We cannot be certain
that the wavy line we draw through the rectangle is the same kind
of wavy line as the one we intended to draw in order to apply the
operation: The result of drawing ? etc. We will not be certain
because we cannot remember exactly what the wavy line looks
like. So if we do this experiment again, viz., draw ? through a
rectangle, we need induction to know whether or not we have
really performed the same experiment again. Hence, here, as in
the case of perspicuity, to say that we do not need induction to
tell whether two pictures are the same is to take ease of repro-
duction as a criterion of memorability. This example with the
wavy line also shows that it is not odd to call this feature of proof
patterns "memorability".
A strict finitist, as a mathematician, needs memorability be-
cause if he cannot be certain that he ever pictures the X of
, no picture which is only possibly a picture of the X
of a Y will show him what the X of Ys MUST be.
18. At the third stage we establish two empirical truths. Be-
cause of what we see we judge that the reversal of the OVER in
Picture 2 is REVO. Then, since we have taken the OVER there
as a typical OVER, we also make the general statement, on the
basis of this experiment, that the reversal of OVER is REVO.
It must be emphasized, though, that RO is not used here to ex-
press a necessary truth; at this stage it is used only to express an
experimentally justified belief. I represent this third stage of es-
tablishing the truth of "The reversal of this OVER is REVO" as
filling in the blanks from Stage 2; I was trying to picture seeing
that as a matter of fact REVO came out. I did not picture
generalizing the experimental result. Still, notice that there is a
difference between the singular statement describing the result of
the experiment and RO as an empirical generalization. The sin-


Strict Finitism
75
gular statement is based on the sensory evidence in Picture 2
while RO, as an empirical generalization, is inductively grounded
in the singular statement plus statements that the OVER in
Picture 2 is a typical OVER. The point is that in the empirical
realm general statements are based on singular ones. We will see
in section 23 that there is not such a relationship between the
mathematically general and the mathematically singular. How-
ever, our main interest is not in the empirical statements, - the
is statements
which are justified by the experiments that we
picture. I call attention to the empirical statements which are
justified by the experiments pictured in strict finitistic proofs so
that I can show that strict finitists make a transition from is to
MUST. Study of this transition may provide a clue on how to make
the transition from is to OUGHT. (For a discussion of is to MUST
and is to OUGHT transitions see my: "Deduction and Intuitive
Induction" in the March 1966 Journal of Philosophy and Phe-
nomenological Research.) In this essay, however, our main interest
in the third stage is in the picture which is drawn there. This
picture is the completed proof pattern and it must be survey-
able.
19. A picture of an operation being performed is survey able
if it shows us the result being part of applying the operation or
getting the result being the same as applying the operation. (There
is no significant difference between part of applying an operation
and being applying the operation; it all depends upon the descrip-
tion of the operation. For instance, having an R before an E is
only part of applying the reversal of OVER while having REVO
is applying the reversal of OVER. But we could easily describe
an operation on OVER such that having R before E would be
applying the operation. So in the future I shall talk only of having
the result being the same as applying an operation.) I believe that
I am using "surveyable" as Wittgenstein used it. In 11-55 he
wrote:
"Proof must be surveyable": this aims at drawing our attention to the
difference between the concepts of 'repeating a proof' and 'repeating
an experiment'. To repeat a proof means, not to reproduce the con-


76
Strict Finitism
ditions under which a particular result was once obtained, but to
repeat every step and the result.
Is it not true that if someone drew another picture of reversing
the letters of an OVER he would draw what is in Stage 3 of
Picture 2? And Stage 3 of Picture 2 contains the result of the
reversal as well as a picture of the reversing. So isn't Stage 3 of
Picture 2 surveyable in Wittgenstein's sense? Doesn't it also show
having REVO as identical with reversing the letters? So isn't it
surveyable in my sense?
Of course a strict finitist wants surveyability because he wants
to see what must result from applying a certain operation. If he
can see that a certain result is one and the same as applying the
operation, he will see that the operation must have that result,
viz., he will have a proof. I think that we can now see why Witt-
genstein, if he was a strict finitist, still held in RFM 111-36 as he
did in 6.2 of his Tractatus that mathematical statements are a
kind of statement of identity. They tell us that certain processes
are the same as certain results. A strict finitist also wants survey-
ability because as Wittgenstein put it in 11-22: "Proof, one might
say, does not merely show that it is like this, but: how it is like
this. It shows how 13 + 14 yield 27." Why does a strict finitist
want a proof to show him how an operation gives a result? As-
sume that one does not know how an operation gives a result.
Then whatever happens after he starts applying the operation is a
possible result. The consequence most frequently following may
be what one picks as the result of applying the operation; call it Z.
But he will have no criterion for saying that the operation is such
that it gives this result. As long as one cannot definitely identify
what "The X of Y" denotes, he does not really understand the
phrase. If you do not know how taking the X of Y gives Z, you
cannot definitely identify Z as the result of taking the X of Y, i.e.,
you cannot definitely identify the X of Y. Now knowing how an
operation gives a result Z is seeing that getting Z by taking the
X of Y is what the X of Y is. (Isn't it true that Stage 3 of Picture
2, by showing HOW reversing the letters of an OVER gives a
REVO, shows that the reversal of the letters of an OVER is a
REVO?) Hence, a strict finitist wants to know how an operation


Strict Finitism
77
gives a result so that he can know what the operation is. In effect,
for the strict finitist the proof defines the operation or gives a
model or paradigm for what it is to apply the operation. He would
agree with Wittgenstein's way of putting it in 11-24:
The proof is our model for a particular result's being yielded, which
serves as an object of comparison (yardstick) for real changes.
And surveyability is necessary to see how an operation gives a
result and hence necessary for a particular picture to be a model
of what it is like to apply an operation.
In light of this discussion of surveyability, we can understand
a remark made by Wittgenstein in both his Tractatus, at 6.1261,
and in RFM, at 1-82. The remark is that in mathematics process
and result are equivalent. This means that knowing what a mathe-
matical operation - the X of Y - is knowing the result of taking
the X of Y. So this remark expresses the position that we have a
mathematical operation only when we can identify it with its
result. At least a sufficient condition for identifying an operation
with its result is that we have a paradigm of how that operation
gives its result; and this we have if we have a surveyable picture
of an application of the operation.
20. Before I begin a discussion of the next two stages, which
turn a strict fínitistic proof pattern into a proof, it may be helpful
if I discuss the relationships between the concepts of perspicuity,
memorability, surveyability, and then compare my use of them
with Wittgenstein's.
If we have a picture of an operation, - the X of Y -, being
applied to a figure of kind Y we have the following results, (a) If
the figure is perspicuous as a Y and perspicuous for applications
of taking the X of Y, then the picture is a memorable picture of
taking the X of Y. We have (a) because if a particular figure is
perspicuous for application of an operation we can tell without
induction that we have applied the operation to it; and this is the
criterion for memorability, (b) If the picture is a memorable pic-
ture of an application of the operation, - the X of Y -, the figure
in the picture to which it is applied is perspicuous as a Y and
perspicuous for applications of this operation. The argument for


78
Strict Finitism
(b) is obvious. If we can tell without induction that we have a
picture of applying the X of Y, we must be able to tell without
induction that we have a Y and that we have taken the X of it.
(c) If the picture of the operation is surveyable, then the figure to
which it is applied is perspicuous and the picture of the operation
is memorable. The justification of (c) is also brief. If the picture
shows us that the X of Y is identical with Z we must be certain
that, i.e., know without induction, that we have a Y and have
pictured taking the X of it. We cannot identify one thing, - Z -,
with something which we cannot definitely identify. (Incidently,
if the picture is surveyable, the Z pictured will be perspicuous as
a Z.) (d) If the figure in the picture is perspicuous as a Y and
perspicuous for applications of the X of Y, the picture of taking
the X of this Y is memorable and, if the Z pictured is perspicuous
as a Z, the picture is surveyable. The argument for (d) is a bit
more complex. If we are absolutely certain that we have a Y, a
X and absolutely certain that our picture of getting Z as a result
of taking the X of the pictured Y is a picture of taking the X of
that Y, then we can see taking the X of that Y as being identical
with having Z. If we do not see getting Z as identical with ap-
plying the operation, we admit that Z did not have to be produced
by application of the operation. But the admission that applying
the operation did not have to produce Z is inconsistent with the
assumption of memorability. I.e., it is inconsistent with the as-
sumption that we were absolutely certain that the picture of get-
ting a Z by taking the X of the pictured Y was a picture of taking
the X of that Y. There is an inconsistency because if we are not
certain that a Z had to be produced when we took the X of the
pictured Y, the presence of a Z is only some evidence that we
took the X of the Y.
A consequence of (a) through (d) is that perspicuity, memo-
rability, and surveyability are equivalent concepts when applied
to a picture which shows a Z being produced by taking the X of
a Y, if the pictured Z is perspicuous as a Z. In strict finitistic
proofs, the Z, - the pictured result -, will always be perspicuous
as a Z. So whenever there is occasion to use these concepts they
are equivalent concepts. This is why in RFM we will find no


Strict Finitism
79
sharp distinction between these concepts. Nonetheless, I believe
that a detailed investigation of these concepts helps in under-
standing what Wittgenstein was trying to say with them.
21. Let us now analyze how we go from the perspicious, mem-
orable, and surveyable picture of Stage 3 to obtain necessary
truths. Recall that at Stage 3 the only truths that we have are:
The singular "The reversal of the letters of the OVER pictured
in Picture 2 is the REVO pictured there" and RO as an empirical
generalization about OVERs. Of course we also possess a survey-
able picture of our grounds for the above two empirical, or is-
statements. It remains, though, to transform these is-statements
into must-statements. The transformations will take two more
stages.
22. On the fourth stage of a strict finitistic proof the prover
makes the move from the singular "is" to the singular "must".
The fourth stage of our example is the movement from "The
reversal of the letters of the OVER pictured in Picture 2 is the
REVO pictured there" to "The reversal of the letters of the
OVER pictured in Picture 2 MUST be a REVO." The prover
makes the move from the singular "is" to the singular "must"
because he can survey Stage 3 of Picture 2 and see that getting
REVO is what it is to reverse the letters of that OVER. As
Wittgenstein put it in 11-39: "This must come out - then this is
not for reasons which we do not see." Of course, what we see
when we see what MUST be is what we see after appreciating the
survey ability of Stage 3.
I grant that the product of the fourth stage is so uninteresting
for non-philosophical purposes that its existence is unknown.
However, calling attention to the fourth stage is philosophically
interesting because it shows the vital role of diagrams in proofs.
The diagrams, proof patterns are not rough sketches of some-
thing else; in the proof we determine what must be true of the
diagrams themselves. A strict finitist treats the diagram as a
platonic form, if you will, of the kind of figure talked about. What
is of the essence of it holds true for all members of the kind. But
before we can determine what must hold true for all members of
the kind we must determine what holds true for the form of the


80
Strict Finitism
kind, and the form of the kind is a typical member of the kind in
the way OVER is a typical OVER.
It is important to note that Stage 4 as well as Stage 5 are parts
of a strict finitistic proof although they cannot be written down
in any way. They are, so to speak, within the person doing the
proving. For a strict finitist a proof is a production of a necessary
truth, and this production is a resultant of a proof-pattern and a
prover. This is what Wittgenstein meant in 11-42 when he wrote:
"It is not something behind the proof, but the proof that proves."
The proof is not some description of evidence such that if we
looked at the evidence behind the statements in the proof we
would see that the conclusion is warranted. No, the proof is the
activity we perform in the presence of the proof-pattern. Or to
say it in another way: proving is a response to that bit of reality
which is the proof-pattern. A strict finitist responds to the proof-
pattern by identifying a certain result with an operation. One
could even say that his response is the production of a new
standard for measuring whether or not a particular operation has
been applied. As Wittgenstein wrote in 11-27:
I am trying to say something like this: even if the proved mathematical
proposition seems to point to a reality outside itself, still it is only the
expression of acceptance of a new measure (of reality).
The fact that a strict finitistic proof requires conditions on the
part of the prover seems unfortunate since it leaves a strict finitist
open to the question Wittgenstein asked himself in 11-42: "Are
you really saying anything but: a proof is taken as proof}" Clearly,
for a strict finitist, being taken for a proof, - here "proof' refers
to proof-pattern - , is necessary for there to be a proof. For in-
stance, a prover must be convinced by Stage 3 that REVO must
come out for Stage 3 to be a proof-pattern for RO. It sounds
even more unfortunate, but for a strict finitist being taken for a
proof is sufficient for being a proof. A strict finitist would concur
with Wittgenstein's remark in 11-39:
What convinces us - that is the proof: a configuration that does not
convince us is not the proof, even when it can be shewn to exemplify
the proved proposition.


Strict Finitism
81
If proving is producing a necessary truth in the face of a survey-
able pattern, being convinced, i.e., appreciating the surveyability
of the pattern, produces the necessary truth. It sounds bad to say
that being convinced is necessary and sufficient for proof because
this seems to imply that any proposition can be proved, i.e., legiti-
mately proclaimed as a necessary truth, and that there can be no
mathematical mistakes in the sense that there cannot be an in-
correct proof. The restricted supply of surveyable pictures pre-
vents the first consequence. For instance, one simply cannot get
a pattern to prove: The number of golf-ball size meatballs form-
able from a pound of hamburger is 8. In Chapter III, section 24
we will see how a strict finitist accepts the consequence that there
can be no incorrect mathematical proof.
23. In a proof, a strict finitist engages in an activity of pro-
ducing necessary truths. However, we have not yet analyzed this
production. We shall do so now when we discuss the fifth and
final stage of a strict finitistic proof, viz., the generalization stage.
What entitles us to go from the singular to the general? In this
case, we clearly cannot do as we do in the empirical realm; we
cannot use the singular statement as inductive grounds for the
general statement. We cannot do this because inductive grounds
are, of course, irrelevant for necessary truths. It is the perspicuity
of the instance of the operand which permits us to generalize. So
since perspicuity is a feature of a singular thing, - the instance of
the operand -, general necessary truths are also grounded in
truths about individuals. Let us see how they are grounded by
considering Picture 2. The OVER in Picture 2 is perspicuous, i.e.,
we can be absolutely certain that it is an OVER. Hence, we are
absolutely certain that there is before us what we would identify
whenever we identified an OVER. Consequently, when we see
