this single perspicuous OVER we see something general or uni-
versal, because what we would identify when we identify any
OVER is general or universal in that it must be common to all
OVERs. Wittgenstein's reproducibility criterion for perspicuity
shows that he also believed, - implicitly -, that what we see in a
perspicuous figure is universal because what we reproduce is more
instances of an universal. Nonetheless, even if what is perspicuous


82
Strict Finitism
in a figure is a universal, it is in the figure. (Or to try to avoid
the metaphorical "in", we could say that the universal is the
perspicuous figure from a point of view in which it is looked at
solely as a member of a kind Y. In our example, the point of
view from which the OVER in Picture 2 is an universal is that
point of view in which we look at it as an OVER and nothing
else.) So if the universal is in the singular, or is thought about by
thinking abstractly about the singular, we must talk about the
singular object. That is the function of Stage 4. At Stage 4 we
establish a necessary truth about the universal as it is in the sin-
gular. But since the universal, although in the singular, is still an
universal what we establish MUST be true of it somewhere is true
of it anywhere. So the generalization from "The reversal of the
OVER in Picture 2 must be a REVO" to RO with a "must" in
it, i.e., "The reverse of OVER must be REVO" is grounded in
the proof-pattern because the OVER which we see there is any
OVER. Really there is no change in subject matter when we
switch from the singular to the general statement. In both state-
ments we are talking about the same universal; only in the general
statement we ignore the fact that the universal is in a single ob-
ject. In effect there is only a verbal difference between the sin-
gular and the general in the mathematical language of a strict
finitist, but the general statements are the ones he uses.
I must emphasize that the preceding speculation about perspi-
cuity as seeing an universal in the particular is not Wittgenstein's
doctrine. I think that it is compatible with his positions, if not
with his approach to philosophy. But to my knowledge, he did not
explicitly hold it.
24. Now the question is: For what are the general necessary
truths used? I believe that Wittgenstein gave the answer when he
wrote in 11-30:
I go through the proof and say: "Yes, this is how it has to be; I must
fix the use of my language in this way.
in 111-29 when he wrote:
What is the transition that I make from "It will be like this" to "it


Strict Finitism
83
must be like this"? I form a different concept. One involving some-
thing that was not there before.
and in 11-26 when he wrote:
Let us remember that in mathematics we are convinced of gramma-
tical propositions; so the expression, the result, of our being convinced
is that we accept a rule.
Let us interpret these quotations by reference to our proof about
the reverse of OVER. As a result of this proof, we set up an
implicatory connection between the concepts "being the reversal
of the letters of OVER" and "being a REVO". If one does not
like talk about concepts he could say that the person who proves
sets up an identity between the two terms "the reversal of the
letters of OVER" and "a REVO". This conceptual connection
or identity of terms is not put into language because the referents
of these concepts or terms are connected as they are said to be
connected in the proved proposition. This is not a denial that
OVERs and REVOs are so connected that if an OVER is re-
versed a REVO is always produced. It is denied only that the
proved proposition describes some previously existent reality for
whose existence the proof supplies (conclusive) evidence. This
denial is a claim that the change from "is" to "must" is not a
change from one kind of description to another. For instance, we
do not switch from describing contingent connections to describing
necessary connections. The is-proposition adequately describes the
connections which exist outside or prior to the proof, and the
experiment which the proof-pattern pictures provides convincing,
if not inductively adequate, evidence for the existence of the con-
nections. The must-proposition expresses the prover's determina-
tion on how he will talk about the connections in the future. He
resolves to say in the future that REVO is the same as the reverse
of OVER; he forms this resolution because of the surveyability
of the proof-pattern.
If my use of "is-proposition" and "must-proposition" is con-
fusing, the confusion can be removed by considering RO. When
I talk about RO as an is-proposition, I am talking about it as an
empirical statement describing the result of an experiment. When


84
Strict
Finitism
I talk about it as a must-proposition, I am talking about it as a
proved proposition. To have a must-proposition it is not necessary
to literally change the "is" to "must be". The "must" is usually
implicit.
Of course, one could interpret to move from "is" to "must" as
a transition from one kind of description to another. He could
suggest that in the first case we describe a contingent connection
while in the second case we describe a necessary connection
which we have seen in the surveyable proof-pattern. To interpret
must-statements as descriptions of necessary connections is not
incompatible with admitting that as a result of them we create an
implicatory connection between concepts or terms. The difficulty
in interpreting must-statements as descriptions of necessary con-
nections is that one then has to develop an epistemology with a
place for intuition as a faculty for recognizing these necessary
connections, viz., truth conditions for the must-propositions.
25. Before we turn to a strict finitist's answers to the three
groups of questions on the philosophic foundations of mathe-
matics, I shall speculate on how action by one person, - the
prover -, can produce a rule in language. Such speculation may
help in interpreting the three passages I just quoted from Wittgen-
stein.
Of course, one man cannot produce a conceptual connection
or an identification of terms in language. A language is not a
man's private possession with which he can tamper at will. What
actually happens as a result of a proof is that many people grant
that what does come out must come out. As a result of many
people saying, or just thinking that it must come out, e.g., that
the reverse of OVER is REVO, they refuse to call anything the
reversal of OVER unless it is a REVO. And if asked they will
call REVO the reversal of OVER. It is this agreement on what
they appreciate in proof-patterns and the corresponding agree-
ment in usage which sets up rules of implication and conceptual
connections in language. In other words, it takes more than one
to lay down a track in language, but still there are tracks in
language because several individuals each try to lay down the
same track.


Strict
Finitism
85
In 11-65 to 11-76, I submit that Wittgenstein made the same
point about the need for agreement. But in 11-65 to 11-76 he was
primarily concerned with arguing that although we need the
anthropological phenomena of agreement in appreciating neces-
sity, agreement in use, and, as he added in V-6, agreement in an
intolerance towards people differing from our use, mathematical
propositions do not assert the presence of these agreements.
Of course, in sophisticated cultures there are specialists on ap-
preciating what must be, viz., mathematicians and logicians, and
amongst the specialists there are experts and geniuses. In such a
sophisticated culture, it would not be too inaccurate to talk about
a small group altering a part of the language for everyone else.
Also since in the sophisticated culture people are aware of the
effect of appreciating what must be, i.e., a rule is developed, they
may well not wait for the rule to evolve from agreement in use.
They may explicitly make the rule when the theorem is proved
by one of the few specialists. But in simple cultures the apprecia-
tion of necessity, - proving -, and the alteration of language is
the achievement of the entire culture. For instance our elementary
arithmetic is not the product of specialists; millions of laymen
fixed language so that a group of five can be referred to with
either "five" or "two and three". Really in a simple culture the
existing mathematics is more rational for the majority of the
people than in a sophisticated culture where the majority of the
people accept explicit mathematical rules from the specialists. In
a simple culture the people through their use of language develop
the mathematical rules of their language and their usage is based
on what they think must be the case.
The speculation, just advanced, on how individual's views on
what must be result in a change in language is not incompatible
with the following remark in III-8.
We judge identity and agreement by the results of our calculating;
that is why we cannot use agreement to explain calculating.
Calculating is proving, viz., the determination of what must be by
an individual prover. There is no need to talk about agreement
with anyone when we talk about the determination of necessity.


86
Strict Finitism
It is the proof that proves; not something behind the proof such
as agreement with others that makes the proof work. However,
when we come to discuss the usual effects of the individual's
proofs, we are talking about a natural phenomenon, viz., altera-
tion of language. So in the preceding paragraph I was doing a bit
of casual anthropology rather than philosophy. This anthropology
is compatible with the just quoted remark from III-8. It is also
consistent with Wittgenstein's objections to explanations in phi-
losophy which we noted in Chapter 1.1 was only trying to explain
the natural phenomenon of alteration of language by what Witt-
genstein in 1-35 called a great and interesting agreement in cal-
culation. Further values in even sketching such an explanation
are as follows. Pointing out the need for agreement in calculation
reminds us that language is not something an individual can make
up for himself. Pointing out that there is agreement in calculation
reminds us that worry about each individual trying to develop his
own mathematics, - and thus having none
is baseless.
26. Let us now leave anthropological speculation and return
to philosophy. How would a strict finitist answer the questions
on the philosophic foundations of mathematics? A strict finitist
would answer (Ii) by replying that statements made in pure mathe-
matics are not about any objects. So he would not have to worry
about (Iii) and Iiii); hence he has avoided platonism. I should
emphasize at the outset of this discussion of strict finitism and
mathematical objects that their denial of mathematical objects is
not inconsistent with the discussion of universals in section 23.
One can consistently say that in a proof we pay attention to uni-
versals without saying that we discover truths about connections
between universals and without saying that the order and con-
nection of universals provide truth conditions for our proved
propositions. To say that we pay attention to universals is to
admit that we talk about kinds of figures, but saying this does
not imply that we discover what must be true of a kind rather
than invent what must be true of a kind. But let us examine a bit
more closely what entitles a strict finitist to answer that mathe-
matical propositions, which are proved by strict finitistic means,
are not about any objects, viz., do not have truth conditions. To


Strict Finitism
87
do this let us look at a strict finitistic proof of: The sum of two
and three is five.
The third stage of a strict finitistic proof of 2 + 3 = 5 could be:
12
12 3
1 2
3 4 5
Let us call this proof-pattern Picture 3; it is a slight modification
of a pattern which Wittgenstein presented in 1-62. In Picture 3
we have perspicuous figures for applying "two", "three", "five",
and a memorable picture of counting two and three together. The
picture enables us to survey that the counting together of two and
three is counting five. But there is nothing perspicuous and
memorable, and nothing is surveyed except Picture 3. So the only
objects that there are are the individuals and their properties in
Picture 3. The singular is-statements tell us how it is with them. Or,
we could say that the only truth conditions that are before us are
those conditions which are truth conditions for singular statements
such as: The counting together of the two marks and the three
marks in Picture 3 gives a count of five marks. There is no need to
talk about a number two and a number three standing in some
necessary relation with the number five. All that is necessary for
the proof is that we have perspicuous figures to which we can
apply the adjectives "two", "three", and "five". If I sound dog-
matic in claiming that we do not need to look at numbers to
prove that 2 + 3 = 5, one must ask himself what he has to look
at besides a picture such as Picture 3. Then he must ask himself
what he sees in such pictures that can be called a number two
rather than a group with two members. One could look at it this
way. A strict finitist denies that there are mathematical objects
because he can establish mathematical theorems by using mathe-
matical terms as adjectives applicable to observable figures, but
he does not need to use them as nouns allegedly standing for
some objects.
A comparison of strict finitistic theorems with rules of measure-
ment and a comparison of strict finitistic views of proof with


88
Strict Finitism
Kant's and Berkeley's views on establishing necessity may clarify
strict finitism's position on mathematical objects.
27. In V-15 Wittgenstein claimed:
'There are 60 seconds to a minute.' this proposition is very like a
mathematical one.
This proposition about time is like 2 + 3 = 5 in that both are
rules on identifying terms and both require some empirical con-
ditions for being made. For instance, if nature was so chaotic
that men could not observe small regularities, they could not talk
about seconds. Similarly, if men could not visualize a group of
more than four, they would not be able to establish that 2 + 3 = 5,
and they probably would have no use for it if they introduced
it by convention. So their use and conditions for their use are the
same. They differ primarily in their origin, viz., source of their
introduction (gradual) into language. "There are 60 seconds to a
minute" is introduced by convention while 2 + 3 = 5 is proved.
We have no inclination to say that "There are 60 seconds to a
minute" is true because it corresponds with its truth conditions
and its truth justifies the convention. For our purpose, the ques-
tion is whether or not the fact that 2 + 3 = 5 is proved differen-
tiates 2 + 3 = 5 from "There are 60 seconds to a minute" enough
so that we should say that 2 + 3 = 5 is about objects. (I should
be happy to frame the question as: Although analytic truths do
not have truth conditions, do synthetic a priori truths have truth
conditions?) A strict finitist would answer that the fact that we
can present surveyable pictures of 2 + 3 = 5 but not for "There
are 60 seconds to a minute" accounts for the fact that one gets
put into our languages as a result of a proof while the other
requires explicit convention. At least we cannot get a surveyable
picture of 60 seconds being a minute before the convention is
made. I could also have said that 2 + 3 = 5 becomes a rule of
language by a convention which evolves because most people
have a proof of it while "There are 60 seconds to a minute"
requires an explicit convention to become a rule of language and
this convention is not the effect of a proof.
28. In Section 11 of Part One of his Prolegomena to Any


Strict Finitism
89
Future Metaphysics, in L. W. Beck's translation in the Liberal
Arts Press, Kant wrote:
The problem of the present section is therefore solved. Pure mathe-
matics, as synthetical cognition a priori is possible only by referring
to no other objects than those of the senses.
Kant opened Remark I of Part One by writing:
Pure mathematics, and especially pure geometry, can have objective
reality only on condition that they refer merely to objects of sense.
And he opened Remark II by writing:
Whatever is given us as object must be given us in intuition. All our
intuition, however, takes place by means of the senses only;
Given the difficulty of Kantian philosophy, it may not be too
illuminating to claim that the above quotations from Kant express
exactly what a strict finitist would say about mathematical objects.
But I shall say it because it is true and because it suggestst the
possibility of understanding Wittgenstein by comparison with
Kant or Kant by comparison with Wittgenstein. For instance, my
fumblings with the concepts of perspicuity, memorability, and
surveyability are attempts to replace Kant's doctrines on space
and time as an account of how we can determine the universal
from what we see in the singular. However, I can add a few com-
ments to show the plausibility of saying that a strict finitist would
concur with these quotations from Kant. A strict finitist would
agree that geometry can have objective reality, i.e., exist as a
subject for human thought, only by referring merely to observable
objects. He would agree because if the triangles referred to in
geometry were unobservable REAL triangles, truths about them
could not be established by looking only at the triangular shapes
in his proof-patterns. In other words, if a strict finitist is right on
how to establish necessity, geometry could not exist as a set of
non-analytic necessary truths if geometry were about special
mathematical objects rather than about the observable shapes of
observable individuals. Yet these observable shapes are what
some call only approximate triangles, approximate circles, etc.


90
Strict Finitism
But for Kant and strict finitists the triangles which we see are the
real ones.
Also nothing that I have said so far shows that a strict finitist
could not agree with Kant in holding that the only intuition is
senuous intuition. However, the problem is not whether a philo-
sopher wants to accept only senuous intuition. The problem is
whether or not he can so restrict himself. In our discussion of
strict finitistic epistemology, I shall argue that a strict finitist does
not need a special non-senuous form of intuition for intuiting
mathematical truth. But before this, let us compare strict finitism's
views on mathematical objects with Berkeley's.
29. In sections 11 to 17 of the Introduction and 188 to 133
in his Principles of Human Knowledge Berkeley advances a phi-
losophy of mathematics which for a minor exception is quite
compatible with strict finitism. For instance in section 122 Ber-
keley wrote:
those things which pass for abstract truths and theorems concerning
numbers, are in reality conversant about no objects distinct from parti-
cular numerable things; except only names and characters, which
originally came to be considered on no other account but their being
signs, or capable to represent aptly whatever particular things men had
need to compute.
(This quotation, and the other one, from Berkeley are from
Berkeley, Essay, Principles, Dialogues, ed. by Mary W. Calkins,
Charles Scribner's Sons, 1929.) A strict finitist would agree that
arithmetic theorems need no objects besides particular numerable
things. A strict finitist would agree that arithmetic theorems are
conversant about words or signs in the sense that alteration of
language is a consequence, - usually long range consequence -,
of theorems. However, he would reject any suggestion that they
are conversant about language in the sense that linguistic facts
provide truth conditions for theorems. A proof leads a strict
finitist to assert a theorem, not something outside the proof such
as usage. And the strict finitist is right in doing so; we do not
consider what people say as relevant to mathematical truth. As
Wittgenstein put it in 111-35:


Strict
Finitism
91
Mathematical propositions seem to treat neither of signs nor of
human beings, and therefore they do not.
Later in 111-35 he repeated:
Once more: we do not look at the mathematical proposition as a
proposition dealing with signs, and hence it is not that.
Indeed it can be misleading even to agree that mathematical
propositions deal with signs in the sense that they determine
connections in language. This can mislead because it suggests
that the sole function of mathematical propositions is to express
what we have proved and thereby cause an alteration in language.
However, we also use mathematical propositions to do what Witt-
genstein called predict, or what I would call compute the conse-
quences of some data. Mathematical propositions can be used to
compute predictions from empirical propositions. For instance, if
we have an OVER or two apples and three pears, we can use our
proved propositions to predict what the reversal will look like or
how many pieces of fruit we will have. "Calculate" would be a
good word to use here, but it has been pre-empted to stand for
determining necessity rather than using necessary truths. "Predict"
is suitable as long as it is not interpreted as saying that proved
propositions are empirical predictions. Wittgenstein acknowledged
that laying down a track in language is not the sole function of
mathematical propositions when he wrote in 111-18:
It is clear that mathematics as a technique for transforming signs for
the purpose of prediction has nothing to do with grammar.
30. A strict finitist and Berkeley would, however, be in close
agreement on how we determine necessity. At least they would
agree that we can determine what holds of all figures of a certain
kind by looking at a particular figure of that kind. A strict finitist
would find nothing to quarrel with in Berkeley's statement in
section 15 of Berkeley's Introduction to his Principles of Human
Knowledge. It reads:
Thus, when I demonstrate any proposition concerning traingles, it is


92
Strict
Finitism
supposed that I have in view the universal idea of a triangle: which
ought not to be understood as if I could frame an idea of a triangle
which was neither equilateral, nor scalenon, nor equicrural; but only
that the particular triangle I consider, whether of this or that sort it
matters not, doth equally stand for and represent all rectilinear trian-
gles whatsoever, and is in that sense universal.
A strict finitíst would also go on to agree with Berkeley that to
use a particular figure as a representative of a kind we must think
abstractly about the particular figure without having any abstract
image or figure before us. However, unless one can prove that
developing philosophical theories is always pernicious, one should
also try to give an account of what it is for an instance of an
universal to represent any instance of that universal. The discus-
sions of memorability, surveyability, and especially the discus-
sion of perspicuity in section 23 were attempts to give such an
account.
31. There are several ways of characterizing what we do in
non-applied mathematics. As individuals, we determine what must
be the result of applying certain operations to certain ordinary
objects. For us, as individuals, our determination of what must
be the result of applying these operations alters our concept of
what these operations are. Or, in many cases, you could say that
we have formed our concept of what it is to have applied the
operation. Before the proof we have a concept of applying the
operation while after the proof we have a concept of what it is to
have applied it. But after the proof the concept of having applied
the operation replaces the concept of applying the operation be-
cause in the proof-pattern we see the applying of the operation
as being identical with having applied the operation. You could
even say that as a result of the proof we change our concept of
the operation from a temporal to a non-temporal concept. For
instance, we changed our temporal concept of reversing the letters
of OVER to the non-temporal concept of the reversal of the
letters of OVER. Evidence that we have changed our concept for
the temporal "reversing the letters of OVER" to the non-temporal
"the reversal of the letters of OVER" is provided by the fact that
we use the reversal for the criterion of whether or not we have


Strict Finitism
93
applied the operation. Mention of concepts of non-temporal con-
nections suggests that we can characterize strict finitistic proofs
as establishing the existence of internal relations. As a matter of
fact Wittgenstein concluded RFM by talking about internal rela-
tions. The last paragraph of RFM reads:
we can say that proof must shew the existence of an internal relation.
For the internal relation is the operation producing one structure from
another, seen as equivalent to the picture of the transition itself - so
that now the transition according to this series of configurations is
eo ipso a transition according to those rules for operating.
In 1-99 to 1-104, Wittgenstein indicated that an internal relation
is asserted to exist when we speak non-temporally about the rela-
tion, i.e., speak as if relating the terms as the relation does is
what it is to be the relation. For instance, being the reverse of is
regarded as an internal relation between OVER and REVO when
we identify being the reverse of OVER with REVO. And we
assert that the relation is internal when we assert "The reverse of
OVER is REVO" where the "is" is the non-temporal "is" of
identity.
The concluding paragraph of RFM deserves a bit more com-
ment. In this comment I shall talk about the proof of "The reverse
of OVER is REVO" rather than the proof 2 + 3 = 5 because I
think that this sentence about OVER and its proof are the best
examples to use whenever a difficult point in strict finitism must
be discussed. First think about reversing the letters of OVER as
a temporal transition. Think about it as the physical process or
transition of producing another structure, usually a REVO, from
OVER structures according to the rule: put the last letter first,
the second to last second, etc. What is the criterion of whether
or not someone has carried out this transition according to the
rules? Stage 3 of Picture 2 provides the criterion, but how? Stage
3 is certainly a picture of such a transition. But Stage 3 of Picture
2 is taken as more than a picture of such a transition. Because an
OVER is actually reversed in Stage 3 we take Stage 3 as being
(equivalent to) a typical transition of this type. Once we have
taken the picture of the transition as being a typical transition,


94
Strict
Finitism
we cease, for awhile, to think of the transition as a temporal
process. The picture is static; it shows us before and after at the
same time. By taking the transition out of time, we can survey it.
The survey of Stage 3 leads us to identify reversing OVER with
having a REVO. So now the series of configurations in Stage 3
of Picture 2, namely, the letters of the REVO being connected
with the letters of the OVER, provides the criterion for whether
or not one has correctly performed the temporal physical transi-
tion of having taken an OVER and rearranged its letters ac-
cording to the rule: put its last letter first, etc. Of course, the
criterion which the series of configurations provides is that the
transition is correctly performed if and only if a REVO is pro-
duced. The series of configurations, viz., Stage 3, has provided
us with this criterion because a survey of it has shown us that
getting a REVO is what this transition is: its essence. To get the
essence of the transition we had to look at it as an internal rela-
tion, i.e., a non-temporal relation or transition.
But, and this is the important point, no matter how we char-
acterize what we do in a strict finitistic proof, we are talking about
no individuals, properties, or operations other than ordinary
observable ones. Even when we characterize what we are doing
as determining and asserting the existence of internal relations,
we are still dealing only with empirical relations and terms for
these relations. To say here that the relations and terms are em-
pirical is to say that we can produce observable instances of these
relations and terms, which cannot be called mere approximations,
to real instances of these terms and relations.
32. It would not be inappropriate for a strict finitist to join
with Wittgenstein in 11-29, where he wrote: "The limit of the
empirical is concept-formation." A strict finitist would agree that
in a proof we alter our concepts of empirical operations and
objects. We make the alterations because we cannot see how
anything other than what we have proved can be. Thus as a result
of the proof we specify that certain results are not empirical
possibilities; it does not make sense to say that they occur. For
instance, it does not make sense to say that the reversal of OVER
is REOV since we cannot have an idea of what it would be like


Strict Finitism
95
to reverse an OVER and get a REOV. So in proofs, concept-
formation, we set limits on what are empirical possibilities, on
what can be a fact. We set these limits because we do not have
any clear idea of what it would be like for our proved propositions
to be false empirical propositions.
Wittgenstein recognized the tremendous importance of not being
able to conceive of a proposition being false. (See 111-29 but
especially III-4.) Actually one can look at much of RFM as an
attempt to find the proper role of this much criticized psycho-
logical criterion for necessary truth. I shall discuss this inconceiv-
ability of the denial criterion for necessary truth in sections 39,
40, and 41. Here, I shall say only that I believe that Wittgenstein's
showing us the proper place of this psychological fact is the most
valuable contribution of RFM.
In brief, then: In non-applied mathematics we determine what
we can think and what we cannot think about ordinary objects.
Our proved propositions are expressions of what we as individuals
have determined must be or cannot be. The long range linguistic
consequence of our proofs is, that what we as individual provers
have said cannot be thought and ultimately cannot be said about
ordinary objects or anything else. So at least his mathematics does
not require a strict finitist to accept anything into his ontology but
that which is necessary for there to be ordinary objects, a lan-
guage containing terms referring to ordinary objects, and people
who can determine what can and cannot be thought about ordinary
objects. But how can people determine this? This question brings
us to epistemology, or strict finitism's answers to the questions in
Group II. An epistemology is essential to the strict finitist since
his ontology is a bonus from his epistemology. As usual though,
his cutting of costs in ontology, viz., elimination of non-ordinary
objects, forces him to pay a price in epistemology, viz., finding a
place for self-evidence. But as we shall see it is not a high price.
33. The gist of strict finitism's epistemology is aptly summed
up in the remarks that a mathematician is an inventor, as Witt-
genstein said in 1-167, and that what he invents is essence, as
Wittgenstein noted in 1-32. For instance, a mathematician invents
the connection that makes the counting together of two and three


96
Strict
Finitism
THE SAME as counting five. Previous to his invention of this iden-
tity, or creation of essence for counting together of two and three,
the counting together of two and three merely almost always gave
five but it was not identical with counting five. The main diffi-
culty in strict finitistic epistemology is to provide an interpreta-
tion for "invent" or "create" so that the conventionalist view that
any sentence we wish can be made into a device for expressing
necessary truths is avoided. However, a strict finitist must not so
sharply distinguish his position from conventionalism that he drifts
into talking as if there were a kind of intuition which gave insight
into pre-existing necessary connections. I hope I explain how a
strict finitist this middle grounds when I develop his answers to
the second group of questions.
34. A strict finitist should answer (Hi) by admitting that there
are truths in non-applied mathematics, and he should go on to
admit that these truths are necessarily true. He should make these
admissions in order to avoid propounding the paradoxes that
there are no mathematical truths and that everyone else is wrong
in thinking that they must be true. However, a strict finitist should
be reluctant to make these admissions without qualification. He
should say that a mathematical proposition is true in the sense
that the same set of words which are used to express it also ex-
pressed an experimentally justified truth. He should add that such
propositions are necessarily true in the sense that we have proofs
that they are unfalsifiable. But he should go on to say that the
necessary truths of mathematics are not truths in the sense in
which their empirical variants are truths; they do not have truth
conditions. (The empirical variant of a strict finitistic mathematical
proposition is the experimentally justified proposition expressed
with the same sentence or set of words which are used to express
it. For instance, the sentence "The number of letters in 'Bismarck'
is 8" can be used to express the empirical truth justified by the
kind of experiment pictured in Stage 3 of Picture 1. This em-
pirical proposition expressed by "The number of letters of 'Bis-
marck' is 8" is the empirical variant of the proved proposition
we finally express with the same sentence.) If he said that the
mathematical truths had the same truth conditions as their em-


Strict Finitism
97
pirical variants, he, - a strict finitist
would be saying that
mathematical truths are equivalent to empirical truths. If he said
that the mathematical truths have some other truth conditions,
he most likely would end up saying that there are special mathe-
matical objects. It would not help to say that the proof itself
provides truth conditions for the mathematical truth proved by
it. If a strict finitist said that the proof itself provided truth con-
ditions for the proved proposition, he would be saying that those
empirical conditions which are necessary for us to think the proved
proposition unfalsifiable are its truth conditions. In particular, he
would be in danger of saying that the contingent psychological
fact that he cannot think of the proved proposition as being false
provides the truth conditions for the necessary truth, - the proved
proposition. (We shall see in our discussion of (Iliii) that a strict
finitist does not claim that necessary truths assert the existence of
those conditions which lead us to regard them as necessary.) In
V-15 Wittgenstein made this very point that although empirical
conditions are necessary to make the proved proposition's em-
pirical variant true and psychological conditions are necessary for
us to regard the empirical truth as unfalsifiable, the role of the
proved proposition is not to assert the presence of these necessary
conditions for its use as a necessary truth. A strict finitist would
not be unhappy with the formula that a mathematical truth is a
new use of an empirical truth, - its empirical variant -, and hence
the mathematical truth is a different proposition from the em-
pirical truth. Its truth comes from its empirical employment while
its mathematical character, - its necessity -, comes from regarding
what its empirical variant says usually happens as a result of a
process as being what the process is.
35. A strict finitist has to admit, also, that there are mathe-
matical falsehoods since people talk about statements such as
2 + 3 = 6 being false. However, a strict finitist would want to
qualify this admission. For a strict finitist we have a mathematical
proposition only when we regard an empirical truth as unfalsifi-
able on the basis of a proof. It is very unlikely that we would find
much empirical evidence for the empirical variant of what are
called mathematical falsehoods. Further, I suspect that such evi-


98
Strict
Finitism
dence as there is, for say 2 + 3 — 6, is not representable in a
surveyable picture. So a strict finitist would claim that there al-
most certainly are not any empirical falsehoods which are proved.
So for a strict finitist, talk about mathematical falsehoods cannot
mean that mathematics contains false propositions, i.e., that false
propositions are proved. To talk about mathematical falsehoods
must mean, then, to talk about a proposition which if an empirical
truth would require us to think as empirically possible what we
previously determined to be inconceivable, i.e., a proposition
which conflicts with a previously proved proposition. So in a way
mathematical falsehoods are mathematical only in a derivative
sense. They are empirical falsehoods that have never been directly
made into a mathematical proposition VIA a proof. But for every
mathematical falsehood Q there is a proposition P, whose truth
conditions we cannot imagine not occurring, but which if they
failed to occur would make the falsehood Q empirically true, and
P has been proved. Because of the way in which mathematical
rules evolve, what I have just said is that a proposition can be
regarded as a mathematical falsehood only by regarding it as
conflicting with current mathematical rules. Without the rules it
would not be mathematical. However, mathematical truths do not
need rules to be made mathematical, i.e., to be proved. The
difference between the true-false dichotomy as it applies to em-
pirical propositions and as it applies to mathematical propositions
is that there must be a mathematical truth before there can be a
mathematical falsehood, while there does not have to be an em-
pirical truth before an empirical falsehood is an empirical propo-
sition.
36. How would a strict finitist answer question (Iliii) on what
it means to say a truth is necessarily true in addition to being
merely true and how we come to know that a truth must be true?
To answer I must say that what it means to say that a proposition
P is necessarily true. To say that P is necessarily true is to say
that either one or two below holds.
One: P can be paraphrased as "The X of Yi, Ya,
, Yn, is
Z" where "is" means identity.
Two: If P is rewritten in logical notation in which it has a form


Strict Finitism
99
Q, the proposition "The truth value assigned to Q in two value
logic is true" is an identity.
If we say "White is lighter than black", "No red surface is
green", and "All OVERs give REVOs when reversed" are neces-
sarily true, we are saying that they meet condition One. We are
saying that "The relation of shades which holds between white
and black is white being lighter than black", "The number of
surfaces which are uniformly red and green is zero", and "The
reverse of OVER is REVO" are identities. I hope that these three
examples show what I mean by "paraphrase" in condition One;
we simply say the same thing except that we begin with "The X
of'. I talk about the paraphrasing simply because it enables me
to call all assertions that a proposition is necessarily true asser-
tions that a certain identity holds. A typical example of a propo-
sition being necessarily true because it meets condition Two is
provided by "This page is white or this page is not white." Since
in logical notation the form of this proposition is (P v~P), to say
that the proposition is necessarily true is to say that "The truth
value assigned to (P v~P) is true" is an identity.
Now the above explication of what it means to say that a truth
is necessarily true is not contained in RFM. However, I do believe
that this explication is compatible with his philosophy of neces-
sary truth, although maybe not compatible with his style of doing
philosophy. In 111-36 Wittgenstein wrote:
That is to say: realizing mathematical relations has a role similar to
that of realizing identity. It might almost be said to be a more com-
plicated kind of identity.
37. The value of this explication of what it means to say that
a truth is necessary is that it helps me explicate what is asserted
when a necessary truth is asserted to be necessarily true. I can
now say that if P is necessarily true, is asserted to be necessarily
true, and meets condition One, then what is asserted is that the
identity in the paraphrase holds. For instance, someone who
asserts "2 + 3 = 5", where use of "=" has the force of asserting
that 2 + 3 must be 5, asserts that the counting together of two
and three is identical with the counting of five. In this example,


100
Strict Finitism
counting together of two and three and counting five are what
could be called the topics of the assertion. If reversing the letters
of OVER is our topic, someone who asserts, as a necessary truth,
that the reverse of OVER is REVO asserts that "the reverse of
OVER" and "REVO" stand for one and the same thing. So if a
necessary truth is of type One, viz., meets condition One, to assert
that it is necessary is to assert that an identity holds. If P is of
type Two and is asserted to be necessarily true, even if P is all
that is explicitly asserted, this is equivalent to asserting the iden-
tity: "The truth value assigned to the logical form of P is true."
If there were not such an identity, then the assertion of P as a
necessary truth of type Two is not acceptable, while if there is
such an identity the assertion of P as a necessary truth of type Two
is acceptable. All that I have said is that to assert that P is a
necessary truth when P is necessarily true because it has a logically
valid form is to assert that P has a logically valid form. But the
important point is that even when we assert that an analytic truth
is necessarily true we are asserting the existence of an identity.
38. The purpose of this explication is to enable me to distin-
guish between what a necessary truth means, viz., asserts when
asserted to be necessarily true, and the grounds for it being a
necessary truth. In particular, although a necessary condition for
us to have grounds for saying that a proposition is necessarily true
will be that we cannot conceive of its denial being true, what it
means to say that a proposition is necessarily true will not be
that we cannot imagine its denial being true. To repeat: to say
that a proposition is necessarily true will be an assertion about an
identity; not a psychological assertion. If a necessary truth is
asserted but not asserted to be necessarily true, what is asserted
is whatever it is that the sentence used to make the assertion says.
So a strict finitist cannot be charged with the absurdity of trying
to reduce necessary truths to contingent psychological truths about
what we can and cannot conceive or imagine. But, still, it may
seem that a strict finitist reasons in the following poor way. I
cannot think of the X of Y not being Z. So the X of Y is identical
with Z. To see that a strict finitist does not advocate such poor
logic, we must develop a strict finitistic answer to the second part


Strict Finitism
101
of (II-iii). This part asks how we determine that a truth is neces-
sarily true.
39. One of the best passages to keep in mind when consider-
ing how a strict finitist determines what must be true is the one
from 11-42 which reads: "It is not something behind the proof,
but the proof that proves." In this context, this passage tells us
that there is not some inference rule that tells us we can go from
the strict finitist proof to the proved proposition. For a strict
finitist the proof is the grounds for determining that a truth is
necessarily true in addition to being merely true. There is no
further justification beyond the proof. In particular, a strict finitist
does not hold that a principle such as: "What we cannot imagine
cannot be" justifies his proof. Still, because we cannot imagine
a proposition being false we make it into a necessary truth. How-
ever, the "because" in the preceding sentence indicates a causal
connection and not a logical inference. (This last point will be
discussed shortly.)
If we regard proved propositions as statements on what are
good inferences, we can interpret a remark of Wittgenstein's in
V-45 as expressing a strict finitist position on what justifies estab-
lishment of a truth as necessarily true. In V-45 he wrote:
Logical inference is a transition that is justified if it follows a par-
ticular paradigm, and whose lightness is not dependent on anything
else.
Of course, it is the proof-patterns which are the paradigms which
justify logical inference. Hence, logical inference will not justify
moves from the proof-patterns. So if one does not think that a
strict finitist has properly justified a claim that a truth must be
true, the strict finitist is subject to challenge only by challenging
his proof. Talk about logical fallacies will be irrelevant since his
proofs are more fundamental than rules of logic. The best way to
challenge a strict finitistic proof is to present a picture of what it
is like for what is claimed must be true to be false.
But, although, a strict finitist does not seek a rule which per-
mits him to go from "It cannot be imagined" to "It cannot be",
doesn't he make such an unjustifiable move? The answer is: no,


102
Strict Finitism
because the psychological fact of our being unable to imagine a
proposition being false is not taken as ground for its truth or
necessary truth. As Wittgenstein remarked in III-3:
The fact, that is, that here we do not make experiments, but accept
the self-evidence, is enough to fix the employment. For we are not
so naif as to make the self-evidence count in place of expirement.
It is not our finding the proposition self-evidently true, but our
making the self-evidence count, that makes it into a mathematical
proposition.
(In the quotation, "self-evident" means cannot be imagined to be
false.) But what does "making the self-evidence count" mean? It
means that the prover responds to the self-evidence by treating
the proposition about observable objects which he cannot imagine
as falsifiable as a proposition which is not falsifiable by any ex-
perience. Part of such treatment is to assert that it must be true
and to use it as a rule of inference to compute, prior to experi-
ence, what must be the result of certain observable operations or
what must be the observable relations between observable objects.
It would not be misleading to say that for a strict finitist the
proof plays the epistemic role in the determination of what must
be while the self-evidence is the cause of the proofs epistemic
power. It could be misleading, though, to call the self-evidence
"intuition" since the term intuition suggests that the self-evidence
plays a justificatory role in the proof rather than a causal role
because "intuition" suggests that there is an insight into the order
and connection of non-ordinary objects.
40. At this point we can appreciate Wittgenstein's resolution
to avoid developing philosophical theories to solve philosophical
problems. In this case the philosophical problem is concern about
the legitimacy of strict finitistic proofs since our previous beliefs
about good reasoning tell us that a move from "is" to "must" is
not justified. But strict finitists seem to make such a move. One
way to solve this problem would be to assume that there is a
special power of intuition which can see how properties MUST be
connected from the way that they ARE connected in a perspicuous,
memorable, and surveyable picture. Wittgenstein's way is to get
us to look at strict finitistic proofs so that we will realize that it is


Strict Finitism
103
the strict finitistic proof that proves. He calls to our attention the
fact that we do make the move from "is" to "must" and then we
realize, contrary to previous beliefs, that in the presence of a
proof such a move is not unjustified. He also acknowledges the
fact that being unable to imagine the denial of the proved propo-
sition, viz., its self-evidence, is a causal factor in our making the
move. But as a philosopher he talks of self-evidence only to put
it into perspective; he does not refer to it to give a philosophical
explanation, i.e., justification, of any of our epistemic procedures.
He simply describes the fact that self-evidence is a causal factor
in our treating a proposition as unfalsifiable. So he reminds us
that justifications, here proofs, are not justified by some other
justification. We are reminded that if we doubt a justificatory
technique, we should simply go back to the justification. If it
works, our doubts vanish unless we have some irremovable pre-
conceived idea about what a justification should be like regardless
of what does or does not convince us. Of course, if we take a
close look at the justificatory procedure and its use does not
convince us of anything it is no good, and again the philosophical
problem is solved. So, here, one of the main benefits of Wittgen-
stein's style of philosophizing by eschewing philosophical expla-
nations is that we do not ignore the justificatory force of actual
proofs by devising a theory to justify the justification actually
used or on occasion to deny the justificatory power of justifica-
tions actually used.
41. When I began to develop strict finitism's answer to ques-
tion (Iliii), I mentioned that a strict finitist should try to steer a
middle course between intuitionism and conventionalism. We
have just seen how strict finitism avoids intuitionism, where "in-
tuitionism" means hypostatizing a special non-sensuous form of
perception for recognizing necessary truths. To discuss strict
finitism and conventionalism, I must say more exactly what
conventionalism is. Michael Dummett in his "Wittgenstein's Phi-
losophy of Mathematics", reprinted in BP, characterizes conven-
tionalism on p. 494 of BP.
According to conventionalism, all necessity is imposed by us not on
reality, but upon our language; a statement is necessary by vitue of


104
Strici Fimtism
our having chosen not to count anything as falsifying it. Our recog-
nition of logical necessity thus becomes a particular case of our
knowledge of our own intentions.
Dummett goes on to point out the inconsistency of a conven-
tionalistic account of necessity which holds that all necessary
truths are either our axioms, which are established by convention,
or that which follows non-conventionally from our axioms. But
not only has Quine in his "Truth by Convention", reprinted on
pp. 322-445 of BP, noted the inconsistency of such convention-
alism. In passages such asl-112tol-121, Wittgenstein revealed
that he was aware of such an inconsistency. So if Wittgenstein
and strict finitists are conventionalists, they will be consistent
about it. Dummett acknowledges on p. 495 of BP that they will
be consistent conventionalists if conventionalists at all. Dummett
writes:
Wittgenstein goes in for full-blooded conventionalism; for him the
logical necessity of any statement is always the direct expression of a
linguistic convention. That a given expression is necessary consists al-
ways in our having expressly decided to treat that very statement as
unassailable; it cannot rest on our having adopted certain other con-
ventions which are found to involve our treating it so.
What Dummett writes is true because a strict finitist admits that
there are no necessary truths until people make what a sentence
says into a necessary truth, i.e., until people treat what is said as
unassailable. Also a strict finitist agrees that there is, so to speak,
no necessary connection between necessary truths. But this pas-
sage is misleading, as is shown by the conclusions which Dummett
draws. For instance, on p. 500 of BP Dummett writes:
He appears to hold that it is up to us to decide to regard any statement
we happen to pick on as holding necessarily if we choose to do so.
On p. 501 of BP Dummett writes:
Wittgenstein's quite different idea, that one has the right simply to
lay down that the assertion of a statement of a given form is to be


Strict Finitism
105
regarded as always justified without regard to the use that has already
been given to the words contained in the statement, seems to me
mistaken.
42. What is wrong with Dummett's conclusions that a strict
finitist and Wittgenstein hold that we can make any statement
into a necessary truth? Logically any statement, even a contra-
diction, can be made into a necessary truth if we can find a proof
for it. (See 111-58, 111-59, and V-21.) But strict finitists do not
hold that we can make any statement a necessary truth merely
by SAYING it is necessarily true or by LAYING DOWN that it is
necessarily true. A proper appreciation of the role of proof in
strict finitism and in Wittgenstein's thought will show that a con-
vention is not sufficient to make a statement into a mathematical
truth just as a proper appreciation of the role of proof showed
that intuition was not epistemically necessary for a strict finitistic
proof. To make a statement into a necessary truth requires a
proof. So whether or not a strict finitist can lay down a statement
as necessarily true depends upon the contingent fact of whether
or not he can get a proof of it. If a man is not convinced that
what he says must come out will always come out, he cannot with
a clear conscience say that it must come out. More importantly,
though, if he is not convinced that it must be as he says it must,
and others are not either, one man saying it must be true will not
produce a necessary truth in language.
Of course, if many people say that a truth such as "Twelve
inches make a foot" must be true, even when they have no proof
of it, it becomes an unassailable truth. But we can still distinguish
it from truths such as "The reverse of OVER is REVO" because
we can see the proof for the statement about REVO and OVER.
We can think of changing the rule about inches and a foot, but
we cannot think of altering the rule we now have on what happens
if we reverse an OVER.
43. Here again we can appreciate Wittgenstein's descriptive
approach to philosophy. The philosophical problem to be resolved
is wonder about how it can be admitted that what is necessarily
true is determined by people without admitting the absurdity that
any statement can be made necessarily true. The wonder is dis-


106
Strict Finitism
solved by calling our attention to the fact that what people deter-
mine must be true is a truth which they are convinced must be
true. It is just a fact that people are not convinced that most
truths must be true. So it simply does not happen that any state-
ment is made into a necessary truth. And when we look at what
is required for conviction: perspicuous, memorable, and survey-
able pictures, we can rest assured that there is little danger of
people making most truths into necessary truths. We can now
understand why Wittgenstein emphasized the importance of con-
viction in proofs. For instance in 11-39 he wrote:
What convinces us - that is the proof: a configuration that does not
convince us is not the proof even when it can be shown to exemplify
the proved proposition.
For philosophic purposes the importance of conviction in proofs
is that it shows us that there are limits on what can be proved;
Wittgenstein was not suggesting that one can proceed as follows.
Look at a proof, introspect to decide whether or not you are
convinced, and then assert the proposition to be proved if you
have the proper feeling of conviction.
44. So my conclusion on conventionalism in Wittgenstein and
in strict finitism is in agreement with that of Benacerraf and Put-
nam on p. 26 of their introduction to BP where they write:
Wittgenstein's latter position is somewhat like Quine's: Quine and
Wittgenstein both have a desire to destroy "platonistic" accounts once
and for all, and at the same time, a distaste (albeit an ambivalent
one) for conventionalistic accounts.
On pp. 26-27 they go on:
What is unique to Wittgenstein is a tendency to write, at least part of
the time, in a way that suggests that "what we say" somehow deter-
mines the truths of mathematics. Interpreters who want to make
Wittgenstein a conventionalist find such passages grist for their mill;
but it would probably be more reasonable (though perhaps not very
charitable) to see this as an unfortunate way of writing produced by
Wittgenstein's extreme antipathy to universals, necessary connections
etc.


Strict Finitism
107
Of course, I do not agree with the last sentence in this quotation;
I hope that I am showing in this essay how Wittgenstein talked
about necessary connections.
45. A strict finitist can give satisfactory answers to the ques-
tions in Group III, viz., the questions about applied mathematics.
Applied mathematics is the use of the inference rules which have
developed or evolved from mathematical proofs. For instance,
after we have a proof of "The counting together of two and three
is counting five", we get 2 + 3 = 5 as a rule, and we apply this
rule when we conclude that we have five children because there
are two girls and three boys. We could put it this way: In non-
applied mathematics we determine, because of a proof, that a
proposition which originally expresses an empirical truth that the
referents of two terms are only always connected now says that
the referents are identical. In applied mathematics we make use
of the identity of referents established in pure or non-applied
mathematics. So we could say that applied mathematics is dif-
ferentiated from non-applied in that in non-applied mathematics
we make truths necessarily true but do not talk about any objects
while in applied mathematics we use these necessary truths and
hence use the referring terms in them. As a result of using the
referring terms in the necessary truths we do talk about objects;
but these objects talked about are ordinary objects. They are
ordinary objects because the terms in the necessary truth referred
to ordinary objects before the truth was made necessary and the
making of the truth into a necessary truth in no way altered the
reference of the descriptive terms. After we proved that the reverse
of OVER is REVO, "OVER" and "REVO" stood for what they
stood for before the proof. (At most, in a strict finitistic proof
the meaning of "is" is changed from the "is" of predication to the
"is" of identity.)
In IV-41 Wittgenstein wrote: "Concepts which occur in 'neces-
sary' propositions must also occur and have a meaning in non-
necessary ones." This calls our attention to the fact that in non-
applied mathematics we need a statement which is used to express
an empirical truth as our candidate for a theorem. An instance of
this statement must describe our proof-pattern. So the terms which


108
Strict Finitism
occur in the theorem, - the necessary truth -, had to be used in
a referring way before the proof.
So, if a strict finitist is to be criticized, he cannot be criticized
by charging that he cannot give satisfactory answers to the three
groups of questions on the philosophic foundations of mathe-
matics. As a matter of fact, his simple non-platonistic and non-
conventionalistic answers here are the best arguments for strict
finitism. If strict finitism is wrong, it is wrong on the assumption
that all mathematics can be understood as sufficiently like "The
number of letters in 'Bismarck' is 8" for philosophical purposes.
Or the strict finitists could be wrong because, although their
philosophy of mathematics is desirable, it is part of an objection-
able philosophy. We shall turn to a critique of strict finitism in
the last chapter. In the next chapter, I hope to support my inter-
pretation of Wittgenstein as a strict finitist by showing how several
of his problems and positions can be understood if we read him
as a strict finitist.
46. I shall close this chapter by arguing that Wittgenstein's
use of "synthetic a priori" in 111-42, where he wrote "The distri-
bution of primes would be an ideal example of what could be
called synthetic a priori, for one can say that it is at any rate not
discoverable by an analysis of the concept of a prime number" is
the same as the use of "synthetic a priori" in the opening quota-
tion. I believe that the point of this passage from 111-42 is that
we cannot tell from any general rule that a number is prime
although the definition of "prime" tells us what it means to say
that a number is prime. In particular, the definition of "prime"
does not give us a calculating procedure for determining "The
prime number after Y is Z." For a given Y, we have to have a
proof that a particular number is the requisite Z. For instance,
the definition of "prime" does not tell us that the prime after 5
is 7. To determine this we need a proof that 7 is prime and a
proof that there is no prime between 5 and 7. For very large
primes it may be hard to see how there could be strict finitistic
proofs. But there should be no difficulty in seeing how one could
make a strict finitistic proof for "The divisors of 7 are 1 and 7".
But the issue here is: How is "The prime after Y is Z" like "The


Strict Finitism
109
number of letters in 'Bismarck' is 8"? They are alike in that
neither have the form of analytic sentences, both can be proved,
- made necessary -, and if Y is sufficiently small both can be
proved by strict finitistic means.


m
WITTGENSTEIN AS A STRICT FINITIST
To resolve these philosophical problems one has to
compare things which it has never seriously occurred
to anyone to compare.
In this field one can ask all sorts of things which,
while they belong to the topic, still do not lead
through its centre.
A particular series of questions leads through the
centre and out into the open. The rest gets answered
incidentally.
It is enormously difficult to find the path through
the centre.
It goes via new examples and comparisons. The
hackneyed ones don't shew us it. (From V-12)
1. The opening quotation is from a discussion on what to say
and what to do about a calculus in which a contradiction has been
discovered. However, since the actual result of the discovery of
the antinomies in set theory has been intense investigation into
the mathematical and philosophical foundations of mathematics,
it is not perverse to interpret the above passage as a comment on
the entire philosophy of mathematics. In my opinion what Witt-
genstein thought should be compared is mathematical propositions
with propositions such as: "The number of letters in 'Bismarck'
is 8" and "The reverse of OVER is REVO" and mathematical
proofs with strict finitistic proofs of such propositions. The sort
of questions which do not lead to the center of the problem are
the sort of questions which lead us to do more mathematics while
trying to philosophize about mathematics. Such peripheral ques-
tions would be questions such as how to establish the consistency
of a calculus or how to re-write mathematics without quantifying


Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitisi
111
over predicate terms. Despite the difficulty in interpreting Witt-
genstein, I shall go out on a limb and say that the three groups of
questions asked about strict finitistic proofs after mathematical
proofs have been compared with them, - construed as them -,
is the series of questions which leads through the center and out
into the open, viz., sets one on the way towards developing a
satisfactory philosophy of mathematics. I shall not, however,
argue for the strong point that Wittgenstein was perfectly satisfied
with his strict finitism. I am arguing only that he takes it as his
starting point. He even admitted in Appendix 11-18 that he had
only indicated how a complete investigation would go. From the
perspective of my interpretation, answering the three groups of
questions by developing a metaphysical theory or by talking about
mathematics as a formal calculus is making use of hackneyed
examples and comparisons. Unfortunately for my interpretation,
Wittgenstein did not explicitly ask or answer these questions or
any questions like them. But I can explain away his not explicitly
answering these questions by pointing out that this book, - RFM
-, is a selection of notes and not an organized treatise.
In this chapter I propose to support my interpretation of Witt-
genstein as a strict finitist in the following way. I shall show that
we can understand several of his positions on mathematics and
problems which he has about mathematics if we read him as being
a strict finitist. These positions and problems are as follows. He
wondered whether or not Godel's Incompleteness Theorem, or
any reductio ad absurdum proof, makes sense. He took the posi-
tion that a mathematical proof gives meaning to the proved
proposition. He had the problem, which is related to the problem
that proofs give meaning to the proved proposition, of explaining
how one proposition could have two proofs. He held that the
law of excluded middle did not have unlimited application. He
doubted the legitimacy of Cantor's transfinite numbers and Dede-
kind's introduction of real numbers. He held the apparently out-
rageous position that there is nothing mathematically wrong with
contradictions. Finally, he continually worried about what is our
guide in the n-th term in a series.
2. Alan Ross Anderson argues that Wittgenstein's discussion


112
Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitist
of Godel's Incompleteness Theorem is incompetent. In his
"Mathematics and the Language Game", BP 481-490, Anderson
wrote of Wittgenstein's discussion of Godel's Theorem: "But we
are in point of clarity worse off after Wittgenstein's account than
before." However, I believe that Wittgenstein's discussion of
Godel's Theorem is not as unfortunate as Anderson claims. On
the contrary, Wittgenstein's discussion of Godel's Theorem is
philosophically clarifying because it calls our attention to a phil-
osophical assumption behind Godel's proof. The assumption is
that a proposition can be a mathematical proposition, viz., have
meaning as a mathematical proposition, independently of and
prior to our having a proof of it. In the next two sections we shall
consider doubts about this assumption and its role in Godel's
Theorem. But here, in fairness to Anderson, I should add that
because of his resolution to account for existing mathematics,
Wittgenstein would not conclude that Godel's Theorem is bad
mathematics. So Wittgenstein's discussion did suffer from the un-
clarity of inclusiveness.
3. In Appendix I, which is devoted to a discussion of Godel's
Incompleteness Theorem, he wrote in 15:
Whether something is rightly called the proposition "X is unprovable"
depends on how we prove this proposition. The proof alone shew what
counts as the criterion of unprovability. The proof is part of the sys-
tem of operations, of the game, in which the proposition is used, and
shews us it 'sense'.
And in 16 he wrote:
The proposition "P is unprovable" has different sense afterwards -
from before it was proved.
In IV-42 Wittgenstein wrote:
The problem of finding a mathematical decision of a theorem might
with some justice be called the problem of giving mathematical sense
to a formula.
A strict finitist also holds that the proof of a proposition changes
its sense and that proof gives a mathematical sense to a proposi-


Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitisi
113
tion. A proof of a proposition such as "The sum of two and three
is five" or "The reverse of OVER is REVO", changes the mean-
ing of the proposition by changing the meanings of the concepts
in the proposition. For instance, as a result of a proof we alter
the concepts of "The sum of two and three" and "five" by deter-
mining that there is a necessary connection between them. Before
the proof we were aware merely of an empirical connection of
constant conjunction between that to which we applied "The sum
of two and three" and that to which we applied "five". As a result
of the proof, however, we change the meaning of "The sum of
two and three" by determining that calling an aggregate the sum
of two and three is the same as to apply "five" to it. So since the
concepts of a proposition are altered, the proposition cannot have
the same sense after the proof as before. Also a strict finitist holds
that a proof gives mathematical sense to a proposition in that a
proof is what leads us to determine that an experimentally justi-
fied proposition is a necessary truth. By making it into a necessary
truth we make it into a mathematical proposition or at least make
it into the kind of proposition which can be used to compute prior
to experience what we can experience. Or we could put it this
way: As a result of a proof we change the empirical "The X of
Y is Z", where "is" is the "is" of predication to the mathematical
"The X of Y is Z", where "is" is now the "is" of equality.
So we can see that if we assume that Wittgenstein were a strict
finitist he would, as he did do, claim that proof alters the meaning
of a proposition and gives it a mathematical sense.
4. We can also, now, see why he wrote in V-19:
However, queer it sounds, my task as far as concerns Godel's proof
seems merely to consist in making clear what such a proposition as:
"Suppose this could be proved" means in mathematics.
In Godel's proof we suppose that an arithmetic proposition, P,
which says of itself under normal interpretation that it cannot be
proved by the techniques of a specific axiom system A, could be
proved by the techniques of axiom system A. (Here to say that
"P" is arithmetic and the statement "P is not provable by tech-
niques of A" is arithmetic is only to say that they can be written


114
Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitist
using signs for numbers, number variables, plus, times, successor,
equality, and quantifiers. It is not to admit that either P or the
statement "P is not provable by the techniques of A" are either
necessary truths or necessary falsehoods.) But what does "P is
provable by the techniques of A" mean and what entitles us to
call it a mathematical proposition? If we are strict finitists only a
proof will fix the meaning of the concepts in it and make it mathe-
matical; by "fix the meaning" I mean give the concepts the new
use that they will have after the proof. For a strict finitist, it would
be a proof that P is provable by the techniques of A which would
show him what it is like for P to be proved by these techniques
of axiom system A. So for a strict finitist to suppose that P is
provable by the techniques of A is to suppose that he knows
what it would be like for P to be so proved. The supposition that
he knows what it is like for P to be proved by the techniques of
A is the supposition that he knows what it is like to have a
sequence of formulae constructed according to the rules for
forming proof sequences in system A and whose end formula is P.
Now we have a dilemma. You can write down what you sup-
posed you knew or you cannot. If you can write it down, then
you exhibit a proof in A of P and Gôdel's proof could not be
given. (Incidently, such a proof sequence in A would provide a
strict finitistic proof-pattern for "P is provable in A.") If you
cannot write down what you supposed you knew, this is because
you cannot think of what it would be like for P to be proved in
A. If you cannot think of what it would be like for P to be
proved in A, you cannot think of what it is like to have a strict
finitistic proof of "P is provable by the techniques of A." To
know what it is like to have a strict finitistic proof of a proposi-
tion is to have one. If we are strict finitists, and cannot think of
what it is like to have a strict finitistic proof of "P is provable by
the techniques of A", we cannot suppose "P is provable by the
techniques of A" is a mathematical proposition. If it is not a
mathematical proposition, how can we use it in a mathematical
proof?
As can be seen from the above argument, strict finitistic wor-
ries about Gôdel's Theorem are really worries about the legiti-


Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitist
115
macy of reductio ad absurdum. I submit that Wittgenstein called
his own treatment of Godel's Theorem queer sounding because
he was discussing this highly technical result without being con-
cerned with any of the details of its techniques; he was discussing
only the legitimacy of a feature of it, viz., being a reductio, which
it shares with many unsophisticated and trivial proofs. This fact
that Wittgenstein not only questioned the legitimacy of reductio
in Godel's Theorem together with the fact that in IV-28 he ques-
tioned its use in general gives support to the claim that he can be
read as a strict finitist. A strict finitist would object to any use
of reductio ad absurdum since the reductio assumption is not
proved and hence is not a meaningful mathematical proposition.
And if it is a meaningful mathematical proposition because its
denial is proved, it is an unthinkable false mathematical proposi-
tion, and there is no need to try a reductio proof since we already
have what would be proved by the reductio.
5. Of course, if a strict finitist claims that the proof gives
meaning to the proved proposition, he faces the objection that
one would not know before he completed his proof what he wanted
to prove. In V-7 Wittgenstein considered such an objection. He
wrote:
Now how about this — ought I to say that the same sense can only
have one proof? Or that when a proof is found the sense alters?
Of course some people would oppose this and say; "Then the proof
of a proposition cannot ever be found, for, if it has been found, it is
no longer the proof of this proposition". But to say this is so far to
say nothing at all. -
A strict finitist can dismiss this objection as summarily as Witt-
genstein did. The function of a proof, for a strict finitist, is to
give a sentence, - a verbal form for expressing propositions and
making statements, - a new use. For instance, in the proof in
Chapter II, section 13 we gave the sentence "The reverse of
OVER is REVO" the new use of being a rule for computing a
priori what the reverse of an OVER is. We gave it this new use
by further fixing the meaning of, viz., giving new criteria of ap-
plication for, the concepts "the reverse of OVER" and "REVO",


116
Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitist
in addition to making the "is" into equality. So what should we
say happens as a result of a proof? A proposition P is given a
new use? Or should we say that a proposition P is changed into
a new proposition Q, - a mathematical one? It makes no differ-
ence what we say. The important thing is to understand what we
do in a proof, not how we describe what we do in terms of, the
as yet, unclear concept proposition. (Of course, I am not saying
that we should describe what we do in a proof without use of the
concept of "proposition". It is a useful concept; Wittgenstein used
it frequently in RFM. Unfortunately, however, there are no gen-
erally accepted grounds for the identity of propositions. But this
means only that we cannot answer "What is a proposition?" So,
when we use "Proposition" we should try not to use it in contexts
where it is necessary to say what one is and we should simply
try to use it as others do.) I prefer to say that we convert an
empirical proposition P into a mathematical proposition Q al-
though I cannot see how this characterization is better than one
which says that what we do is take a proposition P, which has an
empirical use, and give P a mathematical use. A strict finitist
would agree with how Wittgenstein continued V-7:
It all depends what settles the sense of a proposition, what we choose
to say settles its sense. The use of the signs must settle it; but what
do we count as the use? -
Here Wittgenstein was claiming that it really is a matter of pure
convention whether or not we say that the proposition we end up
proving is the same as the one we started out to prove. He adds
that it does not help to say that the use of a sentence will enable
us to determine whether or not it expresses the same proposition,
since there are no generally agreed upon grounds for identity of
use. We simply are not clear about which uses of a sentence
identify it as expressing the same proposition or a different one.
I take this fact that strict finitists and Wittgenstein would face a
similar problem and handle it in a similar way as further evidence
in support of my suggestion that it is helpful to read Wittgenstein
as if he were a strict finitist.
6. A strict finitist would hold that, in a sense, a proposition can


Wittgenstein
as a Strict
Finitist
117
have only one proof. A proof of "The X of Y is Z" has a proof
pattern consisting of a perspicuous instance of Y, a memorable pic-
ture of taking the X of that Y, and a picture in which we can see,
i.e., survey to realize, that the X of that Y is identical with the per-
spicuous Z in the picture. (The proof also includes our apprecia-
tion of the fact that in the pattern the X of Y is identical with Z.
But here I am concerned not with the interior aspects of proof,
only with the public proof pattern. If "proof' means individual
act of proving, a proof is never repeated.) This pattern, after the
proof, is now our model for what it is to take the X of Y. Witt-
genstein was in agreement with strict finitism when he wrote
in n-24:
The proof must be our model, our picture, of how these operations
have a result.
and
The proof is our model for a particular result's being yielded, which
serves as an object of comparison (yardstick) for real changes.
Since taking the X of Y is one thing, there is only one model of
what the operation is, although there may be many instances of
this model. In the sense that there can be only one model of what
an operation is, a strict finitist holds that a proposition can have
only one proof, i.e., one type of proof-pattern. He would agree
with 11-59 in which Wittgenstein wrote:
But this means e.g.: this proof is a mathematical entity that cannot
be replaced by any other; one can say that it can convince us of some-
thing that nothing else can, and this can be given expression by our
assigning to it a proposition that we do not assign to any other proof.
7. Of course, a strict finitist would have to admit what Wittgen-
stein admitted in 11-58: "Of course, it would be nonsense to say
that one proposition cannot have two proofs - for we do say just
that." So a strict finitist, as well as Wittgenstein, has to show how
his view of proof is compatible with the fact that we ordinarily
talk about a proposition having two proofs. There are two distinct
cases to consider. In the first case, we say that a proposition has


118
Wittgenstein
as a Strict
Finitist
two proofs although it is admitted that the two proofs are merely
variants of the same pattern. Such a case would be proving
2 + 3 = 5 with J ] f|j and a~a bbb
In the second case, we
say that the same proposition has two distinct proofs: one
in one system and another in another system. Examples of case
two are: Proofs of a geometric proposition in synthetic and in
analytic geometry, Proofs of 2 + 3 = 5 from J ] [JJ and by
construction of a derivation of it from some axioms as 1 + 0 = 1,
and Successor (a + b) = a = + Successor (b). A third case occurs
when the proposition is rewritten as a formula in a formalized
axiom system and at least two distinct proof sequences have the
formalized version of the proposition as their end formula.
How could a strict finitist handle these cases and would he
