handle them in the same way as Wittgenstein did? A strict finitist
would handle the first case by saying that two distinct proof
patterns, such as J f j~j~j and a a b b b, are two instances of
one and the same proof-pattern for a proof of "The X of Y is Z"
if and only if they are both perspicuous for X and Z, memorable
for "the X of Y", and we can survey them to see that the X of Y
is Z. If they are not instances of the same proof-pattern they
cannot be used to prove the same thing since we will not see the
same things in them. In other words, two proof-patterns are the
same if no one of them is better than the other as a model of
what the X of Y is. If two proof-patterns are not models for the
same thing, they are not proofs of the same proposition. Of
course, a strict finitist would point out that two instances of the
same proof-pattern can be called different proofs but this is not
a significant sense of "two proofs"; it really would be better, in
this case, to talk about instances of the same proof. Unfortunately
for my interpretation of Wittgenstein, I cannot find any passage
where Wittgenstein gives the same solution. However, the follow-
ing remark from 11-58 can be read as giving a strict finitist
solution to case one of multiple proofs.
"Two proofs prove the same when what they convince me of is the
same." - And when is what they convince me of the same? - How do


Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitist
119
I know that what they convince me of is the same? Not of course by
introspection.
I read the last three sentences as follows. Two proof-patterns
convince us of the same proposition if they serve equally as well
as models. Whether or not two proof-patterns serve equally well
as models is not, of course, decided by introspection; it is decided
by looking at the proof-patterns to see whether they are per-
spicuous, memorable, and surveyable in the same way. If they
are, they are the same model; otherwise not.
A strict finitist would handle the second case in the following
way. To say that the same proposition P is proved in two different
systems Si and S2, requires a proof that the two different proofs
prove the same proposition. To fulfill this requirement a strict
finitist would have to give a strict finitistic proof of a proposition
of the following form. The translation of proposition Q, proved
by proof Px in system S1; into the language of S2 is a proposition
Q* proved by proof P2 in S2. An instance of this formula is: The
translation of "Two straight lines have at most one intersection"
proved by geometric means into analytic geometry is "The system
of equations ax + by = c and dx + ey = f has at most one solu-
tion if not infinitely many" proved by algebraic means. So for a
strict finitist a proposition having proofs in different systems is
simply a bit of mathematics; a proposition has the two proofs if
and only if there is the proof that it has the two proofs. The proof
of the fact that the proposition has two proofs will show what it
means for the separate proofs to give the same meaning to the
proposition proved by both.
I submit that Wittgenstein would give the same account of
what we have when we have proofs in different systems of the
same proposition. Certainly a strict finitist would agree with
11-62 where Wittgenstein wrote:
Let us remember that it is not enough that two proofs meet in the
same propositional sign. For how do we know that this sign says the
same thing both times? That must proceed from other connexions.
For instance, although different proofs may have as their con-
clusion that non-parallel straight lines have at most one point of


120
Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitist
intersection, we do not know that this form of words says the
same thing, expresses the same conclusion, unless we have a
proof, - other connections
which show that the proofs prove
the same thing. I believe that passages such as the following two,
the first from 11-46 and the second from V-7 conclusively estab-
lish that Wittgenstein's account would be the same as the strict
finitists.
Now it is possible to image some - or all - of the proof systems of
present-day mathematics as having been co-ordinated in such a way
with one system, say that of Russell. So that all proofs could be
carried out in this system, even though in a roundabout way. So would
there then be only the single system - no longer the many? - But then
is must surely be possible to shew of the one system that it can be
resolved into the many.
I italicized "shew" to stress that to say a proof in one system
proves a proposition in another system requires proof. The next
quotation from V-7 is also cited to establish that Wittgenstein
held that it must be proved that proofs in different systems proved
the same proposition.
When two proofs prove the same proposition it is possible to imagine
circumstances in which the whole surrounding connecting these proofs
fell away, so that they stood naked and alone, and there were no cause
to say that they had a common point, proved the same proposition.
For instance, we can imagine what it would be like to have no
analytic geometry and hence for certain algebraic propositions
there would be no geometric sense.
8. Also, if we read Wittgenstein as taking the strict finitist's
approach towards accounting for proofs in different systems of
the same proposition, we can reconcile an apparent inconsistency
in his claim that proofs give meaning to proved propositions. In
V-7 he also wrote:
I once said: 'If you want to know what a mathematical proposition
says, look at what its proof proves.' Now is there not both truth and
falsehood in this? For is the sense, the point, of a mathematical pro-
position really clear as soon as we can follow the proof?
I submit that the element of falsehood is that we can have a proof
of a proposition in one system but not yet fully understand the


Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitist
121
point of the proposition. We do not fully understand its point
because we do not yet have a proof that a proof in another system
proves it; hence we do not yet understand what the other proof
will show us can be done with the proposition. For instance, simply
proving by algebraic means that the system of equations: ax + by
= s and dx + ey = f, has at most one solution if not infinitely
many does not give us the geometric point of this proposition.
9. However, my interpretation that Wittgenstein is a strict
finitist is weakened by the fact that he did not explicitly claim
that the proof that a proof in one system proves the same proposi-
tion as a proof in another system must be a strict finitistic proof.
I think that his view on establishing the equivalence of systems
was as follows. For purpose of illustration let the two systems be:
Reference to strokes such as | | | | | to calculate sums and use
of ordinary calculating rules such as 9+ 3 = 12, and 8 +5 = 13,
etc., arithmetic as taught in elementary schools. In this case, we
could say that the use of the strokes is the long way of giving a
proof; this should be obvious for calculating a sum such as: The
sum of 97 and 73 is |
|. We can call the ordinary calculating
rules the decimal system. Up to a point where the stroke proofs
get confused, the stroke proofs can justify what we say in the
decimal notation. Consequently, the stroke proofs themselves are
proofs that what is proved with the strokes is also proved in the
ordinary decimal system of addition. The stroke proofs are, of
course, strict finitistic proofs. So up to a point there is a strict
finitistic proof of the equivalence of the stroke and the decimal
system.
After awhile, however, we can no longer justify what we do
in the decimal notation by reference to stroke patterns because
the requisite stroke patterns would no longer be perspicuous,
memorable, and surveyable. When this point is reached, what we
do in the decimal system is no longer merely a different notation
for what we can do with the strokes because now, as Wittgenstein
pointed out in 11-47, only use of the decimal notation determines
truths of addition. It is as he suggested in 11-51: Calculation in
the decimal notation has a life of its own. We can now see why
Wittgenstein wrote in 11-45:


122
Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitist
But how can one technique of proof be defined by means of another?
How can one explain the essence of another? For if one is an 'abbre-
viation' of the other, it must surely be a systematic abbreviation.
Proof is surely required that I can systematically shorten the long
proofs and thus get once more a system of proofs.
Long proofs at first always go along with the short ones and as it
were tutor them. But in the end they can no longer follow the short
ones and these shew their independence.
We now have the disturbing result that we started out with a
primary system, the strokes, which we used to justify a secondary
system for doing the same kind of calculation, the decimal system.
But after awhile we cease to be able to justify what we do in the
secondary system by reference to the primary system. So how do
we justify what we do later on in the secondary system? It does
not suffice as proof simply to say that if need be we could always
return to the primary system to justify what we do in the secondary
system. We can have a vague idea of checking every addition in
the decimal notation by reference to stroke patterns just as we
can have a vague idea of doing every arithmetic calculation in
the notation of logic. Still, possession of this vague idea does not
show that we have a more ultimate method of proof at our
disposal, because if we tried to produce one of the alleged more
ultimate proofs it would be too complicated to prove anything.
In regard to saying that we can justify calculating techniques by
reference to a primary system, Wittgenstein remarked in 11-53:
"But at every step in the secondary system, we can imagine that it
could be taken in the primary one too!" - That is just it: we can
imagine that it could be done — without doing it.
Of course, imagining that we have a proof is not having one or
even being able to have one. Although a strict finitist would claim
that a proof in the imagination is a proof, this is not to claim that
imagining we can have a proof is a proof. (I should note that in
the preceding quotation the primary system is doing arithmetic in
the notation of logic. But this does not affect my point because
proofs in logical notation are just as worthless as those in stroke
notation for very large calculations.)


Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitist
123
Wittgenstein went on in 11-54 to argue, unclearly I feel, that
there cannot be a strict finitistic proof, viz., a stroke system proof,
that whatever is legitimately done in the decimal notation is
justified by a stroke proof. I think that his point was that circular-
ity would be involved in an attempt to prove that what is done
in the decimal notation can be done in the stroke system. To
prove this, we would need to prove that for every numeral there
is a distinct pattern of strokes. To prove that for every numeral
there is a distinct pattern of strokes requires mathematical in-
duction. In this inductive proof we would say something like: If a
numeral n - 1 having associated with it a pattern with exactly
n - 1 strokes implies that numeral n has associated with it a
pattern with exactly n strokes, then any numeral n has associated
with it a pattern with exactly n strokes. Now to prove in the stroke
system that what is done in the decimal system can be done in
the stroke system requires that the above statement of induction
be re-expressed as a statement about strokes. But such a re-ex-
pression requires that any statement about numerals can be re-
expressed as a statement about strokes, and that is what is to be
proved.
How, then, do we according to Wittgenstein, prove that we
have a right to replace one system with another? He answered
in V-7:
If you ask: "What right have we?" the answer is: perhaps none - What
right have you to say that the development of this system will always
run parallel with that one?
So here is where I have to admit that I cannot conclusively es-
tablish that Wittgenstein is a strict finitist. He admitted that we
can move to new systems of proof; but he did not require that
this move be justified by a strict finitistic proof. Indeed, he did
not require that the move be justified by any kind of mathematical
proof. (I do believe, however, that the first stages of a new system
would be justified by strict finitistic proofs, even according to
Wittgenstein.) Since the move from one system to another is a
significant mathematical move, I cannot say that Wittgenstein's
account of all mathematics is strict finitistic. Here it seems to be


124
Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitisi
conventionalistic. But this is not to admit that he gives a con-
ventionalist account of any mathematics done within a system.
Fortunately for his account of mathematics, here again his
descriptive approach to philosophy takes the oddity out of even
this little bit of conventionalism in his philosophy of mathematics.
He reminds us that simply because there is no extra-systematic
justification for systems, it does not follow that any system is
acceptable. 1-4 contains such a reminder.
But is this counting only a use, then isn't there also some truth corres'
ponding to this sequence? The truth is that counting has proved to pay.
But Wittgenstein did not want us to be misled to the view that
the utility of a system is a ground for saying that it is true or
consists of truths. So he continued 1-4:
"Then do you want to say that 'being true' means: being usable (or
useful)? - No, not that; but that it can't be said of the series of natural
numbers - any more than of our language - that it is true, but: that
it is used, and, above all, it is used.
A philosopher, like Wittgenstein, cites the fact that used systems
are useful to remind us that as a matter of fact despite the lack
of proof for systems as a whole, there are limits on what systems
will be used. Thus worries about all kinds of insane calculating
procedures being acceptable if we admit that convention only
justifies use of a system are shown to be baseless worries. In fact
people will not accept insane calculating procedures.
10. What would a strict finitist say about proofs in a formal
system of a propositional form, such as the proofs in the propo-
sitional calculus of the formula: (P => Q) => ((P. Q) => Q). When
we consider such formulae and such systems, it seems prepos-
terous to hold that a proposition can have only one proof. Witt-
genstein noted this apparent absurdity when he began 11-60 by
remarking:
But am I not making a crude mistake? For just this is essential to the
propositions of arithmetic and to the propositions of the Russellian
logic: various proofs lead to them. Even: infinitely many proofs lead
to any one of them.


Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitist
125
In this case, I think that Wittgenstein's way of explaining away
this apparent absurdity is the only course open to strict finitists.
If we have a formalized axiom system, A, a formula F, which is
a theorem of A, and sequences of formulae Sx and S2, each of
which is a proof of F, Wittgenstein's account would be as follows.
As long as the sequences Sx and S2 are not too complicated, they
provide strict finitistic proof-pattern for propositions in the meta-
language of A of the form: The construction of sequence S by
rules of A is a proof in A of F. Thus the many formal proof
sequences leading to the same formula F do not provide many
proofs for a proposition F. (F is, anyway, a formula, not a
proposition.) They provide only many proof-patterns for many
different metalinguistic propositions. If one wished, it would not
be odd to call these metalinguistic propositions geometric propo-
sitions since they tell what new shapes can be constructed from
some other shapes according to certain rules; they tell what new
formulae can be constructed from some origanal formulae in A
according to certain construction rules of system A.
In 11-43,11-64, and 111-14, there are remarks which show that
Wittgenstein gave the preceding account of formal proofs. How-
ever, n-38 contains both a statement of this account plus a slight
modification of it. So I shall quote from 11-38 to establish that
Wittgenstein gave the preceding account of what is proved by
formal proofs. He began 11-38 with:
Accepting a proof: one may accept it as the paradigm of the pattern
that arises when these rules are correctly applied to certain patterns.
Later on in this Remark he noted that if he held:
"The proof proves first and foremost that this formation can arise
when one operates with these signs according to these transformation
rules."
he gives a geometrical sense or point to formal proofs. He wrote:
For the proposition whose truth, as I say, is proved here, is a geome-
trical proposition - a proposition of grammar concerning signs.
As the following statements from 11-38 show, he held, for the
most part, that formal proofs have this role as proof-patterns for
geometric propositions.


126
Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitisi
Or again: when mathematics is divested of all content, it would re-
main that certain signs can be constructed from others according to
certain rules. -
The least that we have to accept would be: that these signs etc. etc.
- and accepting this is a basis for accepting anything else.
I submit that what he means here by "a basis for accepting
anything else" is that before we can maintain that a sentence is
a necessary truth because it has the form of a theorem of a certain
formal axiom system, we need the geometric, - strict finitistic -,
guarantee that the form is really a theorem of the axiom system.
However, at the end of 11-38, he modified his suggestion that
geometric certainty that a formula is a theorem of a specified
axiom system is necessary and sufficient to determine that propo-
sitions with that form are necessary truths. For instance, knowing
that the move from (x) (Fx) to Fa is sanctioned by the rules of a
system, or knowing that (Ey) ((x) (Fx) => Fy) is a theorem, is
neither necessary nor sufficient for us to know that: "If all bodies
have weight, then my body has weight" must be true. We have
to know whether the system is right. But this modification does
not alter his account of what it is for a formula to have many
proofs in a formal system. The modification shows only that strict
finitistic proofs that a formula is provable in a system have only
limited utility in establishing what is a logical truth; in particular
they cannot establish that fundamental rules of logic such as
modus ponens and universal instantiation are valid inference
forms. It is not inconsistent with strict finitism to hold that formal
proofs are not the sole proof-patterns for establishing that a
formula is the form of a logical truth. In my "Deduction and
Intuitive Induction", I tried to show how truth tables and Venn
diagrams could be used as strict finitistic proof-patterns for fun-
damental logical truths.
11. Unfortunately for my interpretation of Wittgenstein as a
strict finitist, he did not explicitly say in RFM that fundamental
rules of logic such as modus ponens and universal instantiation
are to be justified by strict finitistic means. As a matter of fact,
early in RFM-1 he wrote as if universal instantiation is a good
inference simply because people make it. (See 1-8 to 1-17.) But he


Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitisi
127
is still bothered by suggesting that anything can follow from any-
thing else if enough people draw the conclusion long enough. So
from 1-25 up through 1-107 he discussed primarily how our
determination of what must be depends upon pictures of how
experiments almost always turn out. (I developed the account of
how stict finitists would proceed as mathematicians from these
Remarks.) I interpret him as going into the discussion of what I
call strict finitistic mathematics in order to show how laws of
logic can be non-arbitrary rules of language without correspond-
ing to any facts. Unfortunately, after his discussion of strict
finitism in RFM-1 when he turns to a discussion of laws of logic,
his primary concern still seems to be to show that logical truths
are not true because they correspond with some kind of logical
fact. He does not stress that certain pictures make the laws of
logic non-arbitrary; that certain pictures force the laws of logic
on us. However, 1-131 and 1-133 give some support to my inter-
pretation that he would say that fundamental rules of logic should
be justified by strict finitistic means. In 1-133 he wrote:
The propositions of logic are 'laws of thought', 'because they bring out
the essence of human thinking' - to put it more correctly: because they
bring out or shew, the essence, the technique of thinking.
This remark supports my interpretation inasmuch as in passages
such as 1-23 he claims that it is the job of a strict finitistic
mathematician to create the essence of a thing. I think that his
view in P/-371 that essence is expressed by grammar, i.e., the
essence of a thing is given by what we must and cannot say of it,
plus his view that grammatical connections are created and not
discovered, entitles me to say that all essences are created. Since
he never talked about essences being created by means other than
strict finitistic means, I assume that he held that all essences are
created by strict finitistic means. With these assumptions, I can
conclude that the laws of logic, - the essence of human think-
ing -, are established by strict finitistic means in Wittgenstein's
opinion.
12. What does a strict finitist say about the law of excluded
middle and what did Wittgenstein say about it? This question is


128
Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitist
ambiguous because it could be taken in three ways. It could be
taken as asking what they would say about the propositional
form (P v ~ P ) where "v" and
have their standard interpre-
tation. It could be taken as asking what they would say about:
"Every declarative sentence in which there are no terms without
referents is true or false." Thirdly, it could be taken as asking
what they would say about: "Every sentence of the form (P v~P),
where there are no terms without referents in P, is true." We can
discard the second version of the question because, as Wittgen-
stein noted in his Tractatus at 6.111, a poor way to state the law
of excluded middle is to say that every proposition is true or false.
His Tractatus statement reads:
Theories which make a proposition of logic appear substantial are
always false. One could e.g. believe that the words "true" and "false"
signify two properties among other properties, and then it would
appear as a remarkable fact the every proposition possesses one of
these properties. This by no means appears self-evident, no more so
than the proposition "All roses are either yellow or red" would sound
even if it were true.
(Note that even at the Tractatus stage of his thought he wanted
logical laws to be self-evident.) So when we ask for a philosopher's
position on the law of excluded middle, we are asking what he
would say about the tautologousness of ( P v ~ P ) and what he
would say about the truth of every sentence with the (P v ~ P )
form. These are two distinct questions, as we shall see in section
14. We shall see that one can claim that (P v ~ P ) is a tautologous
form while not predicating "true" to propositions with that form.
13. A strict finitist would say that truth tables or diagrams
such as those in Tractatus 6.1203 provide strict finitistic proof-
patterns of the tautologousness of forms such as (Pv~P). Of
course, even at the Tractatus stage of his thought Wittgenstein
would give a strict finitistic proof of the tautologousness of
(P v~P). In RFM his use of the concept "tautology" in passages
such as 11-41 indicate that he did not change his concept of
tautology from the Tractatus. Nor did he change his position that
tautologousness is established by strict finitistic means, although
in RFM he did not talk about establishing that a form was tau-


Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitist
129
tologous by means of a truth table. In RFM he talked about
establishing a form's tautologousness by establishing that it is a
theorem of Russell's prepositional calculus. We have just seen in
section 11 that Wittgenstein's position was that theoremhood in
Russell's calculus is established by strict finitistic means. So Witt-
genstein and strict finitists would agree that the form (Pv~P)
is a tautology, and this is proved, not determined by convention.
14. The important question is whether or not they agree on
what the tautologousness of (P v~P) shows about the truth of
all propositions with this form, and in particular what it shows
about the truth of mathematical propositions with this form. A
strict finitist would admit that "The number of letters in 'Bis-
marck' is 8 or the number of letters in 'Bismarck' is not 8" has a
tautologous form. He would also admit that the possession of this
tautologous form shows that the sentence cannot be used to give
information about the number of letters in "Bismarck". He makes
this second admission since the rules for assigning truth to dis-
junctions would assign true to it no matter how many letters
"Bismarck" contained. So a strict finitist admits that possession
of the (P w P ) form by a disjunction of empirical propositions
makes the disjunction an empirical proposition which is always
true and hence one which says nothing. What about its status as
a mathematical proposition? For a strict finitist a proposition
requires a proof to be converted into a mathematical proposition.
So for "The number of letters in 'Bismarck' is 8 or is not 8" to
be mathematical requires a proof of it. If there could be such a
proof that would satisfy a strict finitist, it would be either the
proof that its form is a tautology or a strict finitist proof of one
of the disjuncts. The proof that its form is tautologous could not
be the proof that makes the entire disjunction mathematical be-
cause that proof makes mathematical a proposition such as: The
truth values assignable to "The number of letters in 'Bismarck'
is or is not 8" are true. (Recall the analysis in Chapter III, section
36 on what it is to say that a truth is necessarily true.) (I.e., it
changes such a proposition about truth values from a descriptive
proposition to an identity. In other words, the proof of the tau-
tologousness of a proposition P makes a metalinguistic truth


130
Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitist
about it into a mathematical proposition, but does not make P
itself mathematical. A strict finitistic proof of either of the dis-
juncts will, of course, make that disjunct a mathematical proposi-
tion. But making one disjunct mathematical will not make the
entire disjunction mathematical. On the contrary, proof of one
of the disjuncts makes the entire disjunction meaningless since a
strict finitistic proof of a proposition P is based, - causally -, on
the fact that its denial is unthinkable. For instance, the strict
finitistic proof of "The number of letters in 'Bismarck' is 8" has
made us aware that "The number of letters in 'Bismarck' is not
8" is meaningless in the sense that we cannot think of what it
would be like for it to be true. So once we prove that "Bismarck"
has 8 letters, the disjunction: "The number of letters in 'Bismarck'
is 8 or is not 8" is nonsense because the right disjunct is non-
sense. So for a strict finitist any proposition of the form (P v ~ P )
is nonsense if either P or ~ P has been proved. We can look at
it this way: A proof of one of the disjuncts turns a tautologous
disjunction of empirical propositions into nonsense.
15. There are also sentences of the ( P v ~ P ) form in which
neither P nor ~ P has been proved, and neither P nor ~ P has
use as an empirical proposition. An example of such a sentence
is: The decimal expansion of Pi is a sequence containing 777 or
it is not. In this section, let us restrict ourselves to talking about
this disjunction concerning the decimal expansion of Pi. Since
there is no proof of either disjunct, a strict finitist cannot argue
that the whole disjunction is nonsense because he has proved one
disjunct to be unthinkable. He cannot claim that the disjunction
about Pi is merely a disjunction of empirical propositions such
as: Snow is white or it is not. So why wouldn't a strict finitist
admit that it is a meaningful mathematical disjunction of two
meaningful mathematical statements, which is necessarily true
because it has a tautologous form? It certainly sounds mathe-
matical, or, at least, sounds as if it were about something in
mathematics. He would not admit this because in the absence of
a proof that the decimal expansion of Pi is identical with a
sequence containing 777 he has no idea of what it is for the
decimal expansion of Pi to be identical with such a sequence. To


Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitisi
131
be sure he can imagine himself computing Pi, and then imagine
himself at some point writing 777. But, although a strict finitistic
proof can be in the imagination, it must be surveyable; and this
means that one must see the result of an operation being the same
as the operating. So imagining oneself writing 777 while com-
puting Pi does not give mathematical meaning to the claim that
the decimal expansion of Pi contains 777, unless what one im-
agined enabled them to see their result of writing 777 as part of
the calculating of Pi. If you could see your writing down 777 as
part of calculating Pi, you would have calculated Pi to a point
where 777 is calculated and thus you would have proved that
777 is in Pi. Arguments to show that we don't understand the
affirmative member of the disjunction are sufficient to show that
we do not understand the entire disjunction. So a strict finitist
would agree with the Intuitionists that this disjunction about Pi
is not a meaningful mathematical sentence, because in the absence
of proof we do not know what it would be like for there to be
777 in the decimal expansion of Pi.
16. To settle that Wittgenstein's position on the law of the
excluded middle is strict finitistic, I must determine his position
on what the tautologousness of its prepositional form shows us
about the mathematical truth of sentences with this form. Next I
must determine his position on the meaningfulness of sentences
of the form (P v~P) where one of the disjuncts has been proved.
Finally, I must determine his position on the meaningfulness of
the disjunction about 777 in the decimal expansion of Pi.
Wittgenstein also held that a proof that the form (P v~P) is
tautologous does not make a sentence of that form into a meaning-
ful mathematical statement. In 111-14 he wrote:
"A tautology (e.g. 'P v~P') says nothing" is a proposition referring
to the language-game in which the proposition P has application. (E.g.
"It is raining or it is not raining" tells us nothing about the weather.
Russellian logic says nothing kinds of propositions — I don't mean
logical propositions - and their employment: and yet logic gets its
whole sense simply from its presumed application to propositions.
He meant that the metalinguistic claim that a sentence has a
tautologous form tells us about the truth of what that sentence


132
Wittgenstein
as a Strict
Finitist
could be used to say only in the areas in which the disjuncts
already have a use. Thus, unless the disjuncts already have a
mathematical use, knowledge of the fact that a disjunction of
them is tautologous will not show us that the disjunction is a
necessary mathematical truth. He added that a defect of formal
logic is that it does not tell us in which areas its propositions are
applicable. For instance, there is nothing in logic which tells us
that "A sentence with the form (P v~P) is always true" does or
does not apply to mathematics. In IV-12 he made the same point
that simply knowing that a sentence has a tautologous form does
not suffice for knowing that whatever it is used to assert will
always be true; we need also to know how the component propo-
sitions are used. He wrote in IV-12:
In the law of excluded middle we think that we have already got
something solid, something that at any rate cannot be called in doubt.
Whereas in truth this tautology has just as shaky a sense (if I may put
it like that) as the question whether P or ~ P is the case.
I interpret this passage as saying that it is a contingent matter, -
contingent on the use of the disjuncts -, whether or not possession
of the (P v~P) makes a sentence a necessary truth. So Wittgen-
stein agrees with strict finitism that mere possession of a tau-
tologous form does not make a sentence a necessary truth. In
particular, possession of (Pv~P) form does not guarantee that
mathematical sounding sentences with this form will be necessarily
true or even true.
17. We have seen that a strict finitist holds that sentences with
the (P v~P) form can never be used to assert a meaningful
mathematical statement. Did Wittgenstein also hold this? I believe
so. I believe that Wittgenstein held that a sentence with the form
(P v~P) cannot be used to state a meaningful mathematical
proposition if one of the disjuncts is proved. (You can read much
of IV-18 as investigating this problem. Or, as he asked: "isn't the
law of excluded middle valid for these inside mathematics?")
My reason for believing this is that he questioned, as we saw in
section 4, REDUCTIO AD ABSURDUM proofs because in them we
need to use the denial of a mathematical truth. Also in 1-106 to


Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitisi
133
1-112 he suggested that we cannot believe a false mathematical
proposition because it is unthinkable. Certainly, if one disjunct of
a sentence with the (P v~P) form were mathematically true, i.e.,
proved, the other would be a mathematical falsehood and hence
unthinkable. But for the whole disjunction to be meaningful that
falsehood would have to be thinkable. Wittgenstein also revealed
his position that sentences of the (P v~P) form are not meaning-
ful mathematical propositions if one of the disjuncts is proved by
making a verbal point in IV-18.
The opposite of "it must not occur" is "it can occur". For a finite
segment of the series, however, the opposite of "it must not occur in
it" seems to be: "it must occur in it".
In this context propositions about finite segments of a series can
be regarded as proved propositions. His point is that negation in
a sentence of the form (P v~P) where a disjunct is proved does
not have its normal interpretation if the disjunction is to make
sense. For instance, after we prove "The number of letters in
'Bismarck' is 8", we can assert it as necessarily true. So after this
proof we would have: "The number of letters in 'Bismarck' is 8
or the number of letters in 'Bismarck' need not be 8", if we wanted
to apply the principle of excluded middle to this proved proposi-
tion. But this disjunction just cited makes no sense because we
have no idea of what it is to say that "Bismarck" need not have
8 letters. However, if we write: "The number of letters in 'Bis-
marck' must be 8 or it must not be 8" we can have a slightly
better idea of what we are saying in the right disjunct. We are
saying that "Bismarck" cannot have 8 letters. But, of course, this
last disjunction cited is not an instance of the law of excluded
middle. More generally: a sentence with the (P v~P) form is not
an instance of the law of excluded middle if negation does not
receive its normal interpretation.
18. Wittgenstein would also agree that the disjunction about
777 occurring in the decimal expansion of Pi is not a meaningful
mathematical proposition because in the absence of proof neither
disjunct is mathematical. His discussion of the law of the excluded
middle runs from IV-9 through IV-28. But since I believe his


134
Wittgenstein as a Strict
Finitist
position is already brought out in IV-9, my discussion of this
issue will center on IV-9. In IV-9 he suggested that a question
about the truth of a non-empirical proposition which has no
proof, such as "777 occurs in the decimal expansion of Pi", is as
meaningless as asking a poet whether or not the hero of his poem
has a sister when the poet has not even thought about the matter.
Just as the poet can give no answer until he decides whether his
hero should have a sister, a mathematician cannot talk about
there being an answer until he decides what the answer is to be.
Of course, the mathematician's decision cannot be given as freely
as the poet's since the mathematician requires proof to make his
decision. Wittgenstein put it his way in IV-9:
The question - I want to say - changes its status, when it becomes
decidable. For a connexion is made then, which formerly was not
there.
This means that sentences such as "777 occurs in the decimal
expansion of Pi" change from having no truth value to having
one as a result of a proof, and he would say that with no truth
value they are not mathematical. As he admitted later in IV-9:
However, queer it sounds, the further expansion of an irrational num-
ber is a further expansion of mathematics.
And expansion of mathematics is augmenting the number of
mathematical propositions.
Now Wittgenstein not only agreed with strict finitism that the
disjunction about Pi is mathematically meaningless, he also agreed
that it is meaningless because in the absence of proof we do not
have an idea of what it is for the decimal expansion of Pi to be
one with or without 777. In IV-12 he remarked:
For how do I know what it means to say: the pattern . . . occurs in the
expansion? Surely by way of examples - which shew me what it is like
for. . . . But these examples do not shew me what it is like for this
pattern to occur in the expansion!
Might one not say: If I really had a right to say that these examples
tell me what it is like for the pattern to occur in the expansion, then
they would also have to shew me what the opposite means.


Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitist
135
The last part of the remark means that if a picture of 111 oc-
curring in a sequence of numerals showed us what it is like for
there to be 777 in the decimal expansion of Pi, then erasing these
three 7s should show us what it is like for there not to be 777 in
the decimal expansion of Pi. The embarrassing thing about ad-
mitting that we can have a clear idea of what it is like for 777
to be in the decimal expansion of Pi as well as have a clear idea
of what it is like for the decimal expansion of Pi not to have 777
in it, is that we admit to knowing what would falsify a necessary
truth. One way of making sense of holding that "777 occurs in
the decimal expansion of Pi" is necessarily true but that yet we
can think of falsifying conditions for it, is to hold also that it
describes correctly the infinite expansion already completed. The
proposition is necessarily true because the expansion is already
completed and is unalterable. But we can imagine how the com-
pleted infinite series would look without the 777. Wittgenstein,
however, had no use for such platonism. In V-34 he wrote;
Suppose that people go on and on calculating the expansion of Pi. So
God, who knows everything, knows whether they will have reached
'777' by the end of the world. But can his omniscience decide whether
they would have reached it after the end of the world? It cannot.
I want to say: Even God can determine something mathematical only
by mathematics.
So it seems that in regard to the law of excluded middle Wittgen-
stein can be interpreted as a strict finitist.
19. Let us now see how his objections to Cantor's theorem
that the real numbers are non-denumerable and Dedekind's
theorem or principle of continuity stem from the strict finitism in
his thought. I should warn the reader that I am not mathematically
sophisticated. So, if discussion of sophisticated mathematical
results by someone not particularly skilled in mathematics disgusts
and irritates him, he should turn to section 23.
20. A strict finitist would reject Cantor's theorem because
the proof itself is not strict finitistic, and the concept of 2X0
which we obtain from the proof has no empirical, - ordinary -,
application and hence is not usable for strict finitistic mathematics.


136
Wittgenstein
as a Strict
Finitist
Since it is proved by reductio, the set-theoretic proof of 2 n > n
is clearly not strict finitistic. The diagonal proof is not strict
finitistic despite the fact that in a way it is based on a picture. (If
the reader is not familiar with the diagonal proof of the non-
denumerability of the real numbers, he should see Appendix II-3
of RFM for what a fragment of its proof-pattern is like. Also,
most introductory set-theory texts contain it.) For one, the partial
list of decimal expansions of real numbers running downwards is
not perspicuous as such a list. We cannot tell at a glance that the
list is a list of most all of the real numbers between 0 and 1 in
their decimal expansion form. We have to be told that the list
runs infinitely far down; that is not perspicuous. Similarly none
of the finite segments of decimal expansions of the real numbers,
running from left to right, are perspicuous as such, since we have
to be told that they would be extended infinitely far to the right
to uniquely represent real numbers. But the greatest defect, from
the strict finitist point of view, in the diagonal proof is that it does
not entitle us to identify the operation of making the diagonal
numbers different with a result which is different for all the num-
bers in the list running downward. The reason, from a strict
finitistic point of view, that we cannot make such an identification
is because we cannot survey this picture to see that a picture of
the completed operation is a picture of the desired result. Because
the lists are allegedly infinite, there is nothing that we can call a
picture of the completed operation of changing each diagonal
number.
21. Wittgenstein's discussion of Cantor's diagonal argument
is in Appendix II of RFM. At first glance, he does not seem to
have the strict finitistic objections to the diagonal argument itself.
He admitted in Appendix II-2 that the proof gives good grounds
for no longer trying to put the irrational numbers in a series. In
Appendix II-3, he suggested that the diagonal procedure is a good
technique for constructing a real number different from every
member of a given series of real numbers. So he did think that
the diagonal argument shows something. He thought that it cer-
tainly showed a disanalogy between the concepts "cardinal num-
ber" and "real number". (By "cardinal number" he meant integer


Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitisi
137
or natural number.) Nonetheless he did object to the proof. In
Appendix II-3 he called it a puffed-up proof.
I submit that his objection to the proof is the interpretation of
it as a proof of the existence of transfinite infinite sets. At the
end of Appendix II-3 he wrote:
The difference in kind between the two conceptions is represented,
by a shew form of expression, as difference of extension.
What he meant is that we represent the disanalogy between the
concepts of cardinal (natural) number and real number as a
difference in the number of real numbers and cardinal numbers.
I want, however, to show that his objections to Cantor's diagonal
argument spring from the strict finitism in his philosophy of
mathematics. I can support my interpretation of Wittgenstein as
a strict finitist on this issue if I can show that his reasons for
rejecting the diagonal proof construed as an existence proof of
transfinite sets are strict finitistic. I shall try to show this. We
have already noted his claim from IV-41 that concepts which
occur in necessary truths must already have a use and meaning
in non-necessary, - unproved -, propositions. In V-35, he asked
the rhetorical question of what concept can we assert 2X0 em-
pirically. For instance, can it be applied to a concept of X number
of strokes or can it be applied to some technique as X0 can be
applied to the technique of counting for which there is no speci-
fied end. I believe that he asks this rhetorical question to suggest
that it is useless mathematically because it has no empirical use.
(In mathematics we develop rules for the concepts we use outside
of mathematics. If a concept has no non-mathematical use, we
do not know what rules for its use, developed in mathematics,
tell us to do.) Also back in Appendix II-2 Wittgenstein charged
that the mistake began when one talked about the cardinal num-
bers being ordered in a series. (The mistake is asserting the
existence of transfinite sets.) I submit that his reason for saying
that this is the basic mistake in interpreting the diagonal proof is
that it leads us to look at the cardinal (natural) numbers as objects
which can be put into a series. Once we have this misleading


138
Wittgenstein
as a Strict
Finitisi
platonistic picture, it is easy to interpret the difference between
the concepts of real and cardinal numbers as there being more
real numbers than cardinal numbers. If we would use only number
terms which applied to observable pluralities and if, when we
talked about all natural numbers, we would talk only of the tech-
nique of counting without end, we would not be inclined to think
of all the natural numbers as objects which can laid out in a
series. We would not be so inclined because our number terms
would be adjectives of things which a strict finitist would counte-
nance, viz., observable pluralities such as | | | | or observable
techniques, but never unobservable infinite pluralities. Wittgen-
stein pointed out in Appendix 11-17 that it is a mistake to talk
about infinities in mathematics if we try to use vague pictures of
the infinite, - the enormously big -, to give meaning to mathe-
matical propoistions as the picture
] 11 | j"
gave meaning to
2 + 3 = 5 in our strict finitistic proof. In Appendix II-5, 9, 13,
and 14, he suggested that "infinite" should be used in mathe-
matics only to classify techniques such as counting with no pro-
vision for a last number. If "infinite" is applied to such techniques
it gets its meaning from the language of mathematics but does
not give meaning to mathematical propositions. But my point is
that Wittgenstein wrote as if talking about infinite pluralities in
mathematics is objectionable because it leads us to poor strict
finitistic mathematics, in other words, to drift into platonism and,
worst of all, to determine conceptual connections on the basis of
unperspicuous and unsurveyable pictures. So I do not think it
rash to conclude that Wittgenstein objected to talk of actual in-
finities on strict finitistic grounds. Thus we can regard his objec-
tions to Cantor's theorem as stemming from the strict finitism in
his thought.
22. In RFM IV, from 28 through 40, Wittgenstein discussed
Dedekind's theorem. In my opinion this discussion is even more
difficult to understand than his discussion of Cantor's theorem. In
this essay, I shall not examine these Remarks on Dedekind's
theorem. I would like to suggest, however, that Wittgenstein's
objections to Dedekind's argument in Dedekind's "Continuity and


Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitisi
139
Irrational Numbers" stems from the strict finitism in Wittgenstein's
thought. I suggest that Wittgenstein objections can be read as focus-
ing on one step of Dedekind's proof of the continuity of the real
numbers, as Dedekind defined our intuitive concept of continuity.
This step is the proof that there are irrational numbers, which proof
is a generalization of the proof that the square root of two is irra-
tional. Wittgenstein would not quarrel with the proof of the irra-
tionality of the square root of two. He would quarrel with the
standard interpretation of what is proved when we show that the
square root of two is not rational. He objected to taking the proof
of the irrationality of the square root of two as showing the exist-
ence of an irrational number. I believe that he objected to taking
the proof of the irrationality of the square root of two as an exist-
ence proof because he thought that the reason for taking it as an
existence proof is that if we did not we would have to say that a
straight line has HOLES in it. I read IV-37 as a claim that we do not
have a strict finitistic proof that a straight line has no HOLES.
23. Wittgenstein's remarks on Cantor's and Dedekind's theo-
rems are not the most philosophically impressive sections of RFM
since they are about aspects of mathematics which one must be
mathematically sophisticated to appreciate. Hence, when they are
discussed in a mathematically unsophisticated way, as the way in
which Wittgenstein discussed them, one has the uneasy feeling
that they are not being properly dealt with. However, when we
come to his discussion of consistency no special mathematical
background is required since he does not talk about the mathe-
matical techniques of consistency proofs. So no one needs to feel
unqualified to philosophize along with him here. (His philoso-
phizing against worrying about consistency is primarily in: Ap-
pendix 1-17, 11-78 to U-89, 111-56 to 111-60, V-8 to V-13, V-21
to V-22, and V-28 to V-32.) At times his position on consistency
seems extremely eccentric, but I believe that it can be appreciated
if we read him as a strict finitist.
What would a strict finitist say about consistency and is it more
or less what Wittgenstein would say? First, to avoid suggesting
that anyone advocates mathematicians propounding contradic-
tions, I should note that strict finitists would agree with Wittgen-


140
Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitisi
stein in saying that they do not advocate contradictions. Strict
finitists would answer a question about using contradictions as
Wittgenstein answered it in V-12:
'Then are you in favour of contradiction?' Not at all; any more than
of soft rulers.
Recall from Chapter II, 29 that for a strict finitist a mathematical
truth provides a rule which enables him to determine a priori
what is the result of the application of an operation to some
observable structure. If he had contradictions in his mathematics,
he would have the inconvenience of possessing different and con-
flicting a priori determinations of the result of applying the opera-
tion just as a ruler which stretches and bends is inconvenient
because it gives different measurements of what looks to be the
same length. Such results are inconvenient because if we want an
unambiguous answer we have to resort to another technique. So
a strict finitist's discussion of consistency is academic in that he
does not advocate a different use for contradictions than that
which they have now. A strict finitist would say with Wittgenstein
in 11-83:
It is - 1 should like to say - for practical, not for theoretical purposes,
that the disorder is avoided.
But the disorder is avoided. A strict finitist wants only to change
the philosophy of contradictions and their place in nature. He
would say with Wittgenstein in II-82:
My aim is to alter the attitude to contradiction and to consistency
proofs. (Not to shew that this proof shews something unimportant.
How could that be so?)
(I shall say a word on the parenthetical portion of this passage in
section 27). Here the main point to make is that what a strict
finitist is trying to do when he talks about consistency is to try
to remove what Wittgenstein called in Appendix 1-17: "The super-
stitious fear and awe of mathematicians in the face of contra-
diction".


Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitist
141
24. A strict finitist could say that the belief that there is
something mathematically wrong with contradictions as opposed
to their practical inconvenience is a superstition. A strict finitist
holds that the occurrence of a contradiction does not necessarily
indicate that there is any mathematical error in the proofs of the
proposition and its denial. Le, The mathematical reasoning lead-
ing to both sides of a contradiction can be perfectly good strict
finitistic mathematical reasoning. (Bad strict finitistic reasoning
would be reasoning from unperspicuous, unmemorable, or un-
surveyable pictures to a proposition which we did not see as being
unfalsifiable from the picture.) Such reasoning could be perfectly
good strict finitistic reasoning since for strict finitists both a
mathematical truth, - proved proposition - , and its denial are
empirical propositions before they are made mathematical. There
are no a priori grounds for holding that a proposition and its
denial could not both describe perspicuous, etc. pictures which
lead us to make them into mathematical truths. After all, experi-
ments do not have to come out any one way. In 1-136,11-78, and
V-40 Wittgenstein presented pictures which may lead us to produce
mathematics inconsistent with that which we have now. But the
occurrence of these pictures does not destroy the pictures we now
have for proving the mathematics which we now have. For in-
stance, the picture in 1-136 which may lead some of us to assert
as necessarily true: 3 + 3 + 3 + 3 + 2 = 10, would not destroy
those pictures which provide strict finitistic proof-patterns for
3 + 3 + 3 + 3 + 2 = 14. So we could have good mathematics, -
no bad proofs -, in which the same numbers had different sums.
I think that Wittgenstein believed that reasoning that leads to
inconsistency can be perfectly good mathematical reasoning. He
claimed in V-27 that the purpose of the mathematical problem
of making a bit of mathematics consistent cannot be to create
mathematics where there was none before. So Wittgenstein thought
that inconsistent mathematics was mathematics. This together with
his belief that a philosopher was to understand and not reform
current mathematics suggests that he believed that an inconsistent
mathematics could be a perfectly good mathematics. He wrote
in V-28:


142
Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitist
I mean: if a contradiction were now actually found in arithmetic -
that would only prove that an arithmetic with such a contradiction in
it could render very good service; and it will be better for lis to
modify our concept of the certainty required than to say that it would
really not yet have been a proper arithmetic.
(V-8, V-9, and V-12 contain more remarks that an inconsistent
mathematics can be a perfectly good mathematics.) In 11-81,
Wittgenstein called our attention to the fact that someone could
produce by mathematical reasoning a contradiction in a system
and be quite proud of this bit of mathematical reasoning. And is
this not the way it is? If a man showed that our present arithmetic
is inconsistent, he would produce by mathematical reasoning a
contradiction.
25. A strict finitist could also say that it is a superstition to
believe that contradictions are mathematically wrong because at
least one of the contradictory propositions must be false. A strict
finitist would call this belief a superstition since it would be based
on a belief that one of a pair of contradictory propositions must
be false because both cannot correspond to the mathematical
facts. What I have just said is not inconsistent with what I wrote
in Chapter II, section 35 on strict finitism and mathematical
falsehoods. In Chapter II, section 35, I characterized mathe-
matical falsehoods as UNPROVED propositions which are incon-
sistent with proved ones. In this section we are considering what
to say if we have two PROVED propositions which are mutually
inconsistent. I think that Wittgenstein's rejection of any platonism
in mathematics shows that he would agree that it is mistaken to
say that one of a pair of contradictory propositions must be false
since they cannot both have mathematical truth conditions. Also
in remarks such as:
If the contradiction is so well hidden that no one notices it, why
shouldn't we call what we do now proper calculation?
from V-12 and:
The abysses in the calculus are not there if I do not see them!
Is no demon deceiving us at present? Well if he is, it doesn't matter.
What the eye doesn't see the heart doesn't grieve over.


Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitist
143
from 11-78, he showed that he was indifferent to unnoticed or
implicit contradictions. If he had thought that one of a pair of
contradictory propositions must be false, he would not have been
indifferent to implicit contradictions.
26. A strict finitist could also label as superstititous fear the
belief that if we have an explicit contradiction everything follows.
To be sure one can derive everything from an explicit contra-
diction. But for a strict finitist, derivations are not mathematical
reasoning. For a strict finitist one needs perspicuous, etc. pictures
to prove propositions. It is not at all clear that an explicit contra-
diction provides such a picture for any proposition. I am confi-
dent that Wittgenstein was in agreement with strict finitism on
this point. In V-9 he suggested that only if we try to do mathe-
matics in a mechanical fashion, viz., solely by derivations from
axioms, do we have to worry about concluding everything from
an explicit contradiction. He went on to suggest that the idea of
doing mathematics mechanically is only a fashion of the times,
where I interpret "fashion" as meaning "a foolish fad which will
and ought to pass away". As he noted in II-4: Logic is only frills
attached to real mathematical reasoning.
So strict finitists and Wittgenstein would agree that inconsistency
does not show a mathematical error in that it does not indicate
poor mathematical reasoning, it does not show that we have a
mathematical falsehood, and it does not yield every proposition
as a theorem of mathematics.
27. Wittgenstein and strict finitists would, of course, also
agree that consistency proofs are significant bits of mathematics
and that on occasion a contradiction can make sense. There is
nothing controversial in admitting that consistency proofs can be
significant mathematical proofs in that they show that the results
of applying the operations called inference rules to the axioms of
a system can be only formulae of a certain type.
28. But the claim that contradictions can make sense is con-
troversial. In V14 Wittgenstein wrote:
There is one mistake to avoid: one thinks that a contradiction must
be senseless: that is to say, if e.g. we use the signs 'P',
con-
sistently, then 'P-~P' cannot say anything.


144
Wittgenstein
as a Strict
Finitist
Could a strict finitist agree that contradictions can have a sense?
We have seen that a strict finitist would admit that both a
proposition P and its denial ~ P can be theorems at the same
time. But of course to admit this is not to admit that (P-~P)
could be a theorem. For a proposition of the form (P-~P) to
have a strict finitistic proof a proposition of the (P'~P) form
would have to have an empirical use to describe a proof-pattern.
It is far more disturbing to admit that a contradiction can be the
proper thing to say in an empirical situation than to say that it
can have a legitimate mathematical use. This much is clear. If a
strict finitist holds that an empirical situation cannot be described
by a contradiction, he holds this because he has a strict finitistic
proof, most likely from truth tables, that an empirical proposition
with the (P • ~P) form cannot be true. In his strict finitistic proof
he has taken the empirical truth describing truth tables: "The
assignment of truth values to (P • ~P) for all possible values of P
is false", and made this empirical truth a necessary truth. But he
has to admit that before he made this descriptive truth about
truth tables into a necessary truth its denial had a sense. So he
has to admit, at least, that if it were not for his proof contra-
dictions could have a sense. And to admit this is to admit very
much. A strict finitist always has to be prepared to admit that
someone could produce a picture showing what it would be like
for his proved proposition to be false. Such a picture would lead
him to put his proved proposition back amongst the mere em-
pirical truths, or even on occasion amongst the empirical false-
hoods. So a strict finitist has to admit that a new picture could
lead him to say that contradictions could be true despite the fact
that right now he has not the slightest idea of what such a picture
would be. So I think that a strict finitist would agree with Wittgen-
stein that explicit contradictions could have a sense both in
mathematics and in empirical uses although he would say that he
cannot now see the sense that they could have. I think that this
close agreement on contradictions is strong support for my recom-
mendation that at least much of Wittgenstein's RFM can be read
as strict finitism.
29. The last issue on which I shall compare Wittgenstein and


Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitist
145
strict finitism is on an issue which is discussed more in PI than
in RFM. This issue is: What justifies us in writing a particular
number K in the n-th place of a series S(n)? For instance, what
justifies us in writing 25 in the 5-th place of the sequence n2? (In
PI this is the primary issue discussed in 143 to 156 and in 185
to 241. The four opening Remarks of RFM are devoted to this
issue and he touched on it in III-9). In 1-3 Wittgenstein stated
the problem.
How do / know that in working out the series + 2 1 must write
"200004, 200006"
and not
"200004, 200008"?
- (The question: "How do I know that this colour is 'red'? is similar.)
"But you surely know for example that you must always write the
same sequence of numbers in the units 2,4,6,8,0,2,4, etc." - Quite true:
the problem must already appear in this sequence, and even in this
one: 2,2,2,2, etc. - For how do I know that I am to write "2" after
the five hundreth "2"? i.e. that 'the same figure' in that place is "2":
And if I know it in advance, what use is this knowledge to me later
on? I mean: how do I know what to do with this earlier knowledge
when the step actually has to be taken?
(If intuition is needed to continue the series + 1, then it is also
needed to continue the series + 0.)
"But do you mean to say that the expression '+ 2' leaves you in
doubt what you are to do e.g. after 2004?" - no; I answer "2006"
without hesitation. But just for that reason it is superfluous to suppose
that this was determined earlier on. My having no doubt in face of
the question does not mean that it has been answered in advance.
30. I shall support my interpretation of Wittgenstein as a strict
finitist by showing that the above passage from 1-3 is the natural
thing for a strict finitist to say. First, I should like to point out
why I believe that Wittgenstein discusses the issue so frequently.
He discusses it so frequently because it represents a fundamental
break with his position of the Tractatus 5.473 that logic takes
care of itself. In his later work Wittgenstein shifted to the strict
finitistic position that we have to decide what is a truth of logic
and mathematics for each and every logical and mathematical
truth. So I think that the problem is one which arises from the


146
Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitist
position that we must take care of logic. I do not believe that the
issue is one of how can we make sure a rule, e.g. + 2, is not
interpreted in some unusual way, say as add 4. Wittgenstein ad-
mitted he knew what the rule tells him to do; he was not worried
about the interpretation of the rule. His worry was about how we
know what we have done is in accordance with the rule, given
that we understand the rule.
But what about strict finitism and this problem? Once one
adopts the strict finitistic position that there are no antecedently
existing mathematical facts for our mathematics to correspond
with, we have to account for how mathematical propositions can
be said to be correct because they do correspond with, or are
determined by, a rule. For instance, we would say that writing 25
in the fifth place of the sequence n2 is the proper answer because
that is what we should do according to the rule: square n. So it
seems that in mathematics, as it is presently done, there is, in
addition to having a proof, another criterion for saying that a bit
of mathematics is properly done. This apparently different crite-
rion is that the mathematics, viz., the number computed, does or
does not correspond with what should be done according to a
rule. Consequently, since a strict finitist holds that provability is
the sole criterion for mathematical truth, he will try to explain
how the criterion of being correct because it is in accordance
with a rule is not a criterion different from that of being provable
by strict finitistic means. In particular, a strict finitist wants to
avoid the platonistic suggestion that when his calculations are
judged by reference to whether or not they accord with a rule, one
is checking his calculations by determining whether or not his
calculations correspond with a series of numbers already given
by the rule in some way. In 1-21 Wittgenstein characterized the
kind of picture a strict finitist wants to avoid.
In his fundamental law Russell seems to be saying of a proposition: "It
already follows - all I still have to do is, to infer it." Thus Frege
somewhere says that the straight line which connects any two points is
already there before we draw it; and it is the same when we say that
the transitions, say in the series + 2, have really already been made
before we make them orally or in writing - as it were tracing them.


Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitist
147
So if Wittgenstein were a strict finitist it would be appropriate
for his reflections on mathematics to begin as RFM does begin
with an attempt to understand the phrase: "The steps are deter-
mined by the formula."
We have now seen that a strict finitist would have the problem
of: How do I know that in working out the series + 2, I must
write 200004, 200006 rather than 200004, 200008? But the strict
finitist is asking how he can know this by strict finitistic means
and not have to say that he knows 200004, 200006 is the right
sequence because it corresponds with what is determined by the
rule +2. Now the question is: Are the other statements made in
the passage quoted from 1-3 the kind of statement which a strict
finitist would make? It shall be easier to understand the remaining
statements as strict finitistic statements if I first give the strict
finitist solution to he problem.
We know that 200006 follows 200004 in the sequence + 2 be-
cause by strict finitistic means we, and almost anyone else who
develops the sequence, will when we come to 200004 determine
that 200004 + 2 is 200006. There is no question that the rule
tells us to add 2 to 200004; that, as I said, is not the problem. But
the rule does not give the answer to 200004 + 2. The rule gives
only the operation to be applied. At each step we have to deter-
mine, - prove -, what is the result of the application of the
operation. So a strict finitist can grant that the ordinary language
expression: . "The steps are determined by the formula" is legiti-
mate in that the formula or rule tells us what operation to use.
If we understand our ordinary language, there is no difficulty in
knowing which operation the formula or rule tells us to apply.
But the expression is misleading insofar as it suggests that the
rule can be referred to in some way as a standard for what we
have determined to be the result of the operation at each step.
Thus the rule "add 2, starting from 0'' tells us what operation to
apply at the two hundreth and fifty-first step. But this rule cannot
be, or in any way provide, a criterion for saying that 504 does
not result when we add 2 to 500; only a proof can tell us that.
31. Now let us reconsider the quotation from 1-3. Why would
a strict finitist say that this problem is like: How do I know that


148
Wittgenstein
as a Strict
Finitisi
this color is red? We could say that there is a rule for selecting
red objects. This rule runs: The objects similar in color to this
paradigm are red. (Each person may have a different group of
paradigms, but he does have paradigms.) But this rule gives only
an operation; it tells us to compare objects with paradigms to see
whether or not they are similar in color. But just as in the case
of + 2, knowing what operation is to be performed does not tell
us the result of the performance of the operation. So, just as in
the case of + 2 we had to go through a proof to determine what
results from adding 2, in the case of color judgments we have to
use our eyes to determine the results of comparing objects with a
paradigm object to tell if they have similar colors. Similarly, the
rule: "Add 0 to 2 for each natural number", only gives us the oper-
ation "add 0 to 2"; it does not give us the result of any application
of this simple operation. We need intuition, viz., strict finitistic
proof, to determine the results of any application of 4- 0 as much
as we need it to determine the results of an application of any
other operation, e.g., + 1. (But sections 74, 75, and 76 show us
that "intuition" is not a good term to use here. The passage from
1-3 also suggests that "intuition" is not regarded too favorably.)
A strict finitist would also observe that if he were using corre-
spondence with a series already done in some way when he
computed a sequence, then he could genuinely doubt that 200006
follows 200004 in the series + 2 because he could always doubt
that he is correctly observing the standard or original series. But
if he has proved that 200006 follows 200004, there is no doubt.
(Indeed the epistemological problem raised in 1-3 does not arise
from doubts; it arises because there is a case of knowledge about
which there is not the slightest doubt. Indubitability is a phenom-
enon to evoke wonder.)
So I hope that I have shown that a strict finitistic interpretation
of the problem and the comments on it are NATURAL. If they are,
this supports my contention that it is helpful to read Wittgenstein
as strict finitist.
32. What has been accomplished in this Chapter? Certainly,
I have shown how several passages from RFM can be read if we
assume that Wittgenstein was a strict finitist. I hope that this has


Wittgenstein as a Strict Finitist
149
shown that it is helpful to read many passages in RFM as if Witt-
genstein were a strict finitist. Even if Wittgenstein were not simply
a strict finitist, if much of his philosophy of mathematics can be
construed as strict finitism, this simplifies the problem of finding
more exactly what his philosophy was. All that needs to be done
is to determine how he went beyond strict finitism. I do not
care whether or not I proved that he was nothing but a strict
finitist. My purpose has not been to place Wittgenstein into a
neat philosophical category and then refute him by refuting that
category of philosophic position.
Wittgenstein is what I would call an open as opposed to a
closed strict finitist. A closed strict finitist holds that the only
good mathematics is strict finitistic mathematics. (We shall see in
Wittgenstein's criticism of Intuitionism that he did not want a
closed view which would lead him to reforming, or to suggesting
mathematical reforms.) An open strict finitist holds that strict
finitism is a paradigm of mathematical philosophy. As much as
possible we should try to understand mathematics as strict finitists
understand the simple bits of mathematics they talk about. We
should try to philosophize about other areas of mathematics with
as much clarity and with no augmentation of ontology. I would
like to close this Chapter with the suggestion that strict finitism
plays the same role in RFM as behaviorism plays in PI. Wittgen-
stein was more than a strict finitist in RFM as he was more than
a behaviorist in PI. But they were his starting points, and he crept
only cautiously beyond them.


IV
WITTGENSTEIN'S OBJECTIONS TO OTHER POSITIONS
IN THE PHILOSOPHY OF MATHEMATICS
If in the midst of life we are in death, so in sanity
we are surrounded by madness. (IV-53)
1. Before we assess Wittgenstein's position on the philosophical
foundations of mathematics, we shall take a look at his assessment
of other positions on the philosophical foundations of mathematics
since defects in other positions are some support for his. Also we
may improve our understanding of his position, before criticizing
it, if we first see what he objected to in other philosophies of
mathematics. A man's enemies tell a lot about a man. Logicism
is the only position of which he made any sustained critique.
RFM II from 2 through 56 is devoted primarily to a critique of
Logicism. Scattered remarks contain criticisms of Formalism,
Intuitionism, and empirical interpretations of mathematical state-
ments as extremely well justified experimental statements or as
statements about human behavior. However, I suppose that his
remarks against philosophizing against contradiction can be taken
as a criticism of Formalism. I shall consider his criticisms of
other positions in the following order: Logicism, Formalism, In-
tuitionism, and empirical interpretations. In this Chapter, I shall
assume familiarity with the positions of Logicism, Formalism,
and Intuitionism. Max Black's The Nature of Mathematics and
Stephen Kleene's Introduction to Metamathematics contain short
accounts of these positions. Stephen Korner's The Philosophy of
Mathematics contains a longer exposition. And BP contains
several papers expounding Logicism, Formalism, and Intuitionism.
2. Wittgenstein was not concerned with a technical criticism


Wittgenstein's Objections to Other Positions
151
of Logicism. For instance, he was not interested in arguing that
a logicist must have in his axiomatization of mathematics axioms
which are not universally valid formulae. Nor was he interested
in arguing, as Godel did, that not all mathematical truths can be
represented as consequences of some set of axioms with which a
logicist would begin. As he wrote in V-16:
It is my task, not to attack Russell's logic from within, but from
without.
That is to say; not to attack it mathematically - otherwise I should
be doing mathematics - but its position, its office.
My task is, not to talk about (e.g.) Godel's proof, but to pass it by.
Indeed, Wittgenstein even granted in 11-46 and 11-53 that every
mathematical proof corresponds to a proof in a logicist's axio-
matization of mathematics. What, then, are Wittgenstein's objec-
tions to Logicism since he was not bothered by the fact that not
all axioms required by a logistic axiomatization are universally
valid, e.g., an axiom of infinity? Nor was he bothered by the
incompleteness and absence of a consistency proof for any logistic
axiomatization of all of mathematics. Wittgenstein's objections to
Logicism can be classified under two headings. The first is that
the logistic axiomatization of mathematics does not provide a
foundation for mathematics. (By "logistic axiomatization" I mean
an axiom system that a logicist would claim shows that all of
mathematics is symbolic logic, as Bertrand Russell said in section
4 of his Principles of Mathematics. Or better: An axiom system
which a logicist would allege shows that mathematics consists of
logical truths obtained by deduction from axioms which are logi-
cal truths, all the formulae of the axiom system are written in the
notation of symbolic logic, and the formulae contain no undefined
non-logical constants. Since any two axiom systems which seem
to show that mathematics is symbolic logic would be the same
for a logicist, I shall usually use "the logistic axiomatization"
rather than "a logistic axiomatization". Of course, I do not mean
that a logicist would deny any significant difference between an
axiomatic set-theory and the axiomatization in Principia Mathe-
matica. There is a difference between starting with properties and
starting with classes. I mean that a logicist would not pay any


152
Wittgenstein's Objections to Other Positions
attention to changes in notation and axioms as long as the axioms
could give the theorems he wanted. In this essay when I use "the
logistic axiomatization" I mean primarily the logistic axiomatiza-
tion in Principia Mathematica because that is what Wittgenstein
was talking about. The second is that the mere development of
the logistic thesis that mathematics is logic, in works such as
Principia Mathematica, has corrupted our thinking both in and
about mathematics.
3. In 11-43 Wittgenstein remarked:
logic as the foundation of all mathematics does not work and to shew
this it is enough that the cogency of logical proof stands and falls
with its geometric cogency.
It is obvious from this passage that Wittgenstein claimed that the
logistic axiomatization cannot be a foundation for mathematics.
It is also clear that he had a reason for this charge. But before
we evaluate this, and other, reasons for the charge we must try
to understand why he bothered to make the charge since, as he
claimed in V-13, mathematics needs no foundations. Indeed this
charge that the logistic axiomatization is not a foundation for
mathematics seems at odds with the passage we recently cited from
V-16 where he claimed that he was not interested in attacking
the axiomatization as a mathematical instrument, viz., attacking
it from within.
We have already noted in Chapter I that "foundations of
mathematics" is ambiguous. It can mean either mathematical
foundations of mathematics or philosophic foundations of mathe-
matics. A logistic axiomatization provides a mathematical foun-
dation for mathematics if developing it results in clear concepts
of such mathematical terms as "point", "limit", and "number";
if developing it uncovers fundamental principles of mathematics;
if developing it leads to greater rigor; if the developed axiom
system is complete with respect to the mathematical truths; and
if the axiom system is consistent. For an axiomatization of mathe-
matics to provide a mathematical foundation for mathematics, it
must be mathematics; but, of course, mathematics done better in
regard to rigor and clarity than it is actually done. If the axio-


Wittgenstein's Objections to Other Positions
153
matization were not mathematics, establishing its consistency
would not show the consistency of our actual mathematical
reasoning; nor would the clarity of its concepts and the rigor of
its proofs be any improvement in mathematics. A logistic axio-
matization provides a philosophic foundation for mathematics if
we can regard it as sufficiently like mathematics and if, when we
so regard it, we can give satisfactory answers to the three groups
of questions on the philosophic foundations of mathematics,
which were raised in Chapter I, section 1. As I discussed phi-
losophic foundations of mathematics in section 31 of the same
Chapter, it is not obvious that a logistic axiomatization must be
mathematics to provide a philosophic foundation for mathematics;
it need only be sufficiently like mathematics. Recall that in Chap-
ter I, section 27 we observed that Wittgenstein held only that
mathematics needs no mathematical foundations. So his remark
in 11-43 that logic cannot provide a foundation for mathematics
is inconsistent with the rest of his work only if he meant "mathe-
matical foundations of mathematics".
Incidentally, we can note here that if we accept that Wittgen-
stein was a strict finitist we can understand why he was indifferent
to mathematical foundations. (From here on, I shall write "mathe-
matical foundations" in place of "mathematical foundations of
mathematics" and "philosophic foundations" in place of "phil-
osophic foundations of mathematics".) Consistency, as we have
