seen, is obnoxious only for practical purposes and for practical
purposes we can ignore contradictions. Of course, since a strict
finitist does not prove theorems by deriving conclusions from
premisses, a strict finitist would be unconcerned with logical rigor.
Definitions of mathematical concepts are uninteresting since a
finitist is interested in how concepts in empirical use are connected
with other concepts in empirical use; and proofs, not definitions,
give him these connections. Since a strict finitist makes mathe-
matics as he proceeds, he would not want mathematics to be
complete, viz., completely finished, if such a notion would even
make sense to him.
In my opinion, much of RFM-II is devoted to showing that the
logistic axiomatization of Principia Mathematica cannot provide


154
Wittgenstein's Objections to Other Positions
a mathematical foundation because this axiomatization is not and
cannot be mathematics. So I do think that Wittgenstein's charge in
11-43 that the logistic axiomatization is not a foundation for
mathematics is inconsistent with claims such as those in V-13 that
he is not interested in mathematical foundations. Of course, this
inconsistency is trivial for Wittgenstein's philosophy of mathe-
matics. The interesting questions for interpreting RFM are as
follows. What are his arguments that the logistic axiomatization
is not mathematics? Are these arguments inconsistent with his
claim that he wanted to show by non-mathematical means that
the logistic axiomatization does not and cannot perform its office?
And of most importance: Do these arguments show that the
logistic axiomatization of mathematics is so much different from
actual mathematics that discussion of it cannot provide philosophic
foundations?
4. Wittgenstein's conclusion that the logistic axiomatization
is not mathematics rests on two claims about the logistic axio-
matization. The first claim is that when we work with the logistic
axiomatization, the mathematics that we actually do is only a
small part of mathematics and that small portion is in the meta-
language of the logistic axiomatization. The second claim is that
we could not actually do any mathematics within the axiomatiza-
tion itself. The interest of these claims that the logistic axiomatiza-
tion is not mathematics is that they establish that the logistic
axiomatization cannot perform the office of providing mathe-
matical foundations. They show that the logistic axiomatization
cannot provide mathematical foundations since, as we shall see,
only mathematics can provide such foundations. As we shall also
see, these two claims provide the basis for what can be inter-
preted as Wittgenstein's argument that the logistic axiomatization
cannot provide philosophic foundations.
The charge, which I just quoted from 11-43, that the cogency
of logical proof depends upon its geometrical cogency is part of an
argument to support the first claim. As I showed in our discus-
sion of the law of excluded middle in Chapter III, section 10, the
argument is that the mathematics we do when we deduce formula
F from the axioms is to establish the mathematical truth, - meta-


Wittgenstein's Objections to Other Positions
155
mathematical truth
that the sequence of formulae which we
construct is a formal proof of the formula F. As a matter of fact,
Wittgenstein also believed that the logistic axiomatization was not
even very good for this metamathematics. When the formal proofs
become extremely long we cannot establish with certainty that
they are formal proofs of the end formula. Wittgenstein pointed
this out in II-2, II-7, and 11-14. He pointed out that in very long
formal proofs we have only empirical certainty that the sequence
which we have constructed is a proof of the end formula. This is
not merely to say that we have only empirical certainty about
what is written down. It is to say that we cannot be certain that
what we are aware of is a proper formal proof, despite the fact
that we do not worry about seeing the formulae in the proof
sequence properly. He added that if we abbreviate the techniques
of proof in a formal axiom system so that, for instance we can
eliminate a hundred steps, then we may have a proof that a
certain formula is formally provable by means of the abbreviated
techniques although we could get no proof that the formula is
provable by means of the unabbreviated techniques. As Wittgen-
stein also wrote in 11-43:
logical proof, e.g. of the Russellian kind is cogent only so long as it
also possess geometrical cogency. And an abbreviation of such a
logical proof may have this cogency and so be a proof, when the
Russellian construction, completely carried out, is not.
The significance of this second charge from 11-43 that formal
proofs carried out by unabbreviated rules of proof are not even
good proof-patterns for metamathematical propositions that the
end formula of a sequence is formally proved by the sequence is
that it is a charge that completely rigorous formal proofs, - ones
with all the steps -, have no mathematical office. Or at best they
have an insignificant office. The rigor of formal proofs is only a
pretense of rigor. The formal proofs, as shall be argued, are not
mathematical proofs and as we have just seen the formal proofs
are not even part of good metamathematical proofs. So if they
have any rigor, it is not the rigor of anything mathematical. (A
metamathematical proposition is a mathematical proposition in a
metalanguage.)


156
Wittgenstein's Objections to Other Positions
5. In my opinion, Wittgenstein was correct in holding that
the cogency of logical proofs, i.e., proofs in a formalized axiom
system or uninterpreted calculus, are dependent upon their geo-
metric cogency. In an uninterpreted calculus a proof sequence, if
it shows anything, shows that the last formula in the sequence is
a theorem of the calculus. A formula's being a theorem of a
calculus is not what we would regard as a contingent matter.
Whether or not a certain formula can be derived from the axioms
is as fixed as whether or not REVO can be obtained as the
reverse of OVER. So showing that a formula is a theorem of a
calculus is a topic of genuine mathematical concern even if not
of great importance. We certainly would not be satisfied with
mere empirical certainty that a formula is a theorem. But it is a
fact that if a proof sequence in an uninterpreted calculus is so
long, viz., lacks geometrical cogency, that I cannot be certain
that it is a proof sequence, I cannot assert as a mathematical
truth that the end formula of the sequence is a theorem of the
calculus. However, on occasion I can abbreviate the rules
of proof and be certain that a sequence constructed according to
the abbreviated rules is a proof according to the abbreviated
rules.
6. Wittgenstein was also right in holding that logic cannot be
a mathematical foundation because the cogency of logical proof
depends upon geometric cogency. (Recall that the gist of this
charge that logical cogency depends upon geometric cogency is
that the mathematical office of the logistic axiomatization is only
to provide proof-patterns for metamathematical propositions of
the form: Formula F is a theorem of axiom system A.) He would
be right on this point even if every logical proof provided com-
plete geometric cogency that its end formula is a theorem. Even
if every formal proof provided geometric cogency that it was a
proper proof sequence, this would show only that the axiom sys-
tem was a very good device for providing proof-patterns for
metamathematical propositions about what is provable in the
axiom system. Now providing proof-patterns for the proofs of
such metamathematical propositions could not be providing
mathematical foundations. These metamathematical propositions


Wittgenstein's Objections to Other Positions
157
constitute only a very small part of actual mathematics. Proving
them well is not going to provide much support for the rest of
actual mathematics.
It should be noted that although much of Wittgenstein's criti-
cism of Logicism in II-2 to 11-56 can be read as a characterization
of how a strict finitist would do the metamathematics of Principia
Mathematica, and a complaint that Principia Mathematica is not
a good system for doing metamathematics in a strict finitistic
way, the charge that logic is not the mathematical foundation of
mathematics because logical cogency depends upon geometric
cogency is not based on a premiss that metamathematics should
be done in a strict finitistic way. It is based on a premiss that
proving propositions of the form: "Formula F is a theorem of
axiom system A", is insignificant in providing mathematical foun-
dations.
7. However, someone may make a case that proving proposi-
tions of the form: Formula F is a theorem of axiom system A, is
significant in providing mathematical foundations, especially if
that axiom system is that in Principia Mathematica. (Hereafter, I
shall call the axiom system of Principia Mathematica, PM) They
would point out that when PM is given its normal interpretation,
every mathematical proposition can be correlated with a distinct
interpreted formula of PM. I should point out that here "formula
of PM" refers to well-formed formulae and not just theorems of
PM. Also I should point out that "normal interpretation of PM"
means only interpreting the logical signs, viz., giving a reading
to the quantifiers, prepositional connectives, and the way of
representing that something has a property or that somethings
stand in a relation. So no interpreted formula of PM would natu-
rally read as a mathematical proposition. At least no interpreted
formula of PM would sound like the kind of proposition actually
used in mathematics. Wittgenstein himself granted in II-2,11-46,
and 11-53 that there could be such a correspondence between
propositions of actual mathematics and interpreted formulae of
PM. Indeed it seems that he not only admitted that every mathe-
matical proposition can be re-expressed in the language of PM
but, contrary to Godel's theorem, every proof in actual mathe-


158
Wittgenstein's Objections to Other Positions
matics can be rewritten as a formal proof in PM. But let us
return to my hypothetical defender of the logistic axiomatization.
This defender of Logicism would go on to point out that the mere
fact that mathematical propositions can be re-expressed as inter-
preted formulae of PM solves one problem of mathematical foun-
dations. This is the problem of providing clear definitions of
mathematical concepts. The very task of re-expressing proposi-
tions of actual mathematics requires that all mathematical con-
cepts be defined in terms of logical ones. So Logicists have
certainly given clear definitions of mathematical concepts. Also
once we have this correspondence between propositions of actual
mathematics and interpreted formulae of PM we can identify the
axioms and inference rules of PM as corresponding with, viz.,
being the re-expression of the axioms, possibly implicit, and in-
ference rules used in actual mathematics. Thus, defenders of PM
as providing mathematical foundations would allege that to know
that formula F is a theorem of interpreted PM provides knowl-
edge that the ordinary mathematical language correlate of formula
F is rigorously provable from the axioms and inference rules of
actual mathematical practice. Hence these defenders of the
mathematical office of PM could also go on to claim that a con-
sistency proof of PM would show the consistency of a large por-
tion of actual mathematics.
8. However, someone who gives the above type of defense of
PM as providing mathematical foundations is assuming that
mathematics re-expressed as interpreted formulae of PM is still
mathematics. If the interpreted formula F which re-expresses a
mathematical proposition M were not merely another version of
M, then knowing that F is formally provable from the formal
axioms would not establish that M can be rigorously proved from
the informal versions of the formal axioms. Hence, we can see
how the second claim presented at the beginning of section 4 of
this chapter supports the conclusion that the logistic axiomatiza-
tion cannot provide mathematical foundations for mathematics.
This is so because, in effect, this second claim is a claim that the
interpreted formulae of PM which re-express mathematical propo-
sitions are not a version of the mathematical propositions which


Wittgenstein's Objections to Other Positions
159
they re-express. Let us look at Wittgenstein's arguments for this
second claim, i.e., the claim that we cannot do actual mathe-
matics within PM. This will show why, and what it means to say
that, the logistic axiomatization is not another version of mathe-
matics.
9. Wittgenstein's claim that we cannot do much, if any, mathe-
matics withing the logistic axiomatization is supported by the
following three points. The first point is: If we originally possessed
the logistic axiomatization but had not yet done any of what we
now call mathematics, all of what we now call mathematics would
remain to be done. In 11-47 Wittgenstein wrote:
Can what I want to say be put like this: "If we had learnt from the
beginning to do all mathematics in Russell's system, the differential
calculus, for example, would not have been invented just by our having
Russell's calculus
In 11-46 he also remarked:
Now surely one could simply say: if a man had invented calculating
in the decimal system - that would have been a mathematical inven-
tion! - Even if he had already got Russell's PRINCIPIA MATHEMATICA.
(There are other comments like these in 11-46 and 11-56.)
In my opinion, Wittgenstein was absolutely correct in main-
taining that we would not even have begun to develop what we
now call mathematics if we had only deductions within PM. Of
course, if we had also some metalinguistic truths about PM we
would have a small fragment of what we now call mathematics.
But this metamathematics is not mathematics within PM even if
it can be re-expressed in PM. The way to convince oneself of
this point is to ask oneself how much mathematics one would
know if all that he were taught of mathematics was deductions
in interpreted PM, where "interpreted" means only that the logical
signs have been given a sense. Knowledge of such deductions
would not even give one knowledge that 2 + 2 = 4.
10. The second point is: Although once branches of actual
mathematics, such as computations in the decimal system and


160
Wittgenstein's Objections to Other Positions
the differential calculus, have been developed we can find the
correlates of this mathematics in PM, the formal proofs in PM
are not the proofs that established these results in actual mathe-
matics. In other words, the second point is what Wittgenstein
suggested in II-4. In II-4 he suggested that the logical calculus,
i.e., PM, is a frill tacked on to actual mathematics. The logical
calculus is a frill because the logical calculus is parasitic on actual
mathematics and does not improve it. Only after we have estab-
lished truths in actual mathematics can we reconstruct their proofs
and restate them in the logical calculus. Wittgenstein developed
this point in 11-46. In 11-46 Wittgenstein admitted that the work
in PM corresponding to a branch of mathematics could be called
a new aspect of that branch. "Aspect" is a vague term here. I do
not think that to say that proposition P is proposition Q under a
new aspect is to make the strong point that P and Q say the same
thing. However, in this context, it is not important if we give
"aspect" this strong interpretation. After this unclear admission
about PM being as aspect of actual mathematics, he considered
the following suggestion.
"But in that case the truth of true mathematical propositions can still
be proved from those general foundations."
(Of course, the general foundations are the proofs in PM.)
His reply to this suggestion was:
It seems to me there is a snag here. When do we say that a mathe-
matical proposition is true?
The snag is that we say that a mathematical proposition is true
when we have proved it. Thus, to prove true propositions in PM
is redundant since the fact that they are true shows that they have
already been proved.
I think that Wittgenstein was correct on this second point that
work in PM is redundant for the actual development of mathe-
matics. To convince ourselves of the redundancy of the logistic
axiomatization of mathematics, let us consider how we would
answer the following two questions in this passage from 11-53.


Wittgenstein's Objections to Other Positions
161
"But surely we can always go back to the primitive logical method!"
"Well assuming that we can - how is it that we don't have to? Or are
we hasty, reckless, if we do not?
Who would want to maintain that if mathematicians were really
doing their jobs, they would be doing nothing but deductions in
PM? And if we would not maintain this, is it not because mathe-
maticians are already doing their job properly?
11.
The third point is: The proofs in a logistic axiomatization
cannot replace those in actual mathematics because we still need
to use ordinary mathematics to determine whether or not the
logistic proofs give the proper result. Wittgenstein raised such a
point in 11-17 when he asked:
Let me ask the following question: Is it not conceivable that the 1-1
correlation could not be trustworthily carried out in the Russellian
proof, that when, for example, we try to use it for adding, we re-
gularly get a result contradicting the usual one, and that we blame this
on fatigue, which makes us leave out certain steps unawares? And
might we not then say: - if only we didn't get tired we should get the
same result - ? Because logic demands it? Does it demand it, then?
Aren't we here rectifying logic be means of another calculus?
In this passage from 11-17 he was saying that when we try to add
large sums by means of doing a deduction in PM, we may easily
get confused by the complicated formulae. Thus, we use ordinary
arithmetic techniques to decide whether or not an addition done
in PM is correctly done. So we cannot say logic, - deductions in
PM - , really justifies what we do in addition. In II-4, 11-13, and
11-14 he also makes this point that if we deduce results in PM,
or some other logistic axiomatization, inconsistent with results
obtained by ordinary mathematical techniques, we say that the
results obtained in the formal system are wrong.
Of course, a user of the formal axiom system would not say
that the formal axiom system yielded mathematical falsehoods.
He would say that he miscalculated in the formal system when-
ever his formal calculations conflicted with the results of ordinary
calculations. In regard to the opening quotation of Chapter I, we


162
Wittgenstein's Objections to Other Positions
can see here that there is a use of "I must have miscalculated"
which reveals something of importance about the mathematical
foundations of mathematics. In the context in which someone
rejects a formal deduction with "I must have miscalculated" be-
cause the formal deduction conflicts with an ordinary calculation,
his use of "I must have miscalculated" reveals that the logistic
axiomatization cannot be a mathematical foundation since actual
mathematics has a legislative control over formal mathematics.
To me, this third point seems correct. Who would accept that
1010 + 1 — 1010 simply because to the best of our knowledge the
PM version of 101" + 1 = 1010 was provable? It does not help
PM to say that the proofs in PM are still logically prior even if
they are not psychologically prior. If we use the PSYCHOLOGICALLY
prior proofs, - proofs actually used in mathematics -, as a stand-
ard for correctness of formal proofs, the psychologically prior
proofs are logically prior to the formal proofs.
12. The above three points establish that working within a
logistic axiomatization is not doing any mathematics. The first
and third points show that such work is not sufficient for doing
actual mathematics while the second point shows that work in a
logistic axiomatization is not necessary for doing any actual
mathematics. If working in a logistic axiomatization is neither
necessary nor sufficient for doing actual mathematics it follows
that not much if any actual mathematics can be done in the
logistic axiomatization. Actually the stronger conclusion that no
mathematics can be done in the logistic axiomatization is yielded
by the three points. But I use the weaker "not much if any"
because Wittgenstein granted in II-4 that very small computations
such as 2 + 3 can be done within PM. Still, I think that what he
really meant was that a PM formula expressing 2 + 3 = 5 could
be used as a strict finitistic proof-pattern for 2 + 3 = 5, and the
proved proposition would be in the metalanguage. So I submit
that Wittgenstein held, in effect, that no mathematics could be
done within a logistic axiomatization of mathematics. And it
seems to me that this is true. However, as I pointed out in Chapter
I, section 7 we cannot prevent people in the future calling work
within PM mathematics. If in the future work within PM is called


Wittgenstein's Objections to Other Positions
163
mathematics and treated^ as mathematics, then a philosophy of
mathematics will have to cover it.
13. We have seen that Wittgenstein has shown that a logistic
axiomatization cannot have the office of providing mathematical
foundations because it is not mathematics. Our next question is
whether or not he has done this by attacking it from without or
within. Recall from section 2 of this chapter the quotation from
V-16 in which he claimed that he wanted to attack PM from
without, i.e., non-mathematically. In my opinion the attack was
from without. He did not argue mathematically that a logistic
axiomatization such as PM is mathematically inadequate to pro-
vide mathematical foundations. For instance, he did not bother
to try to prove the inconsistency or incompleteness of PM. He
showed in a non-mathematical way that PM is inadequate to be
mathematics. What was this non-mathematical way? Wittgen-
stein's arguments that PM is not mathematics are of the type that
they should be for a philosopher who held that a philosopher
should describe and not explain. (Mathematics is not describing.)
He described, or more accurately, he recommended that we de-
scribe PM and actual mathematics, - get clear about what they
are -, and the descriptions will remind us that they cannot be
the same. If it is a philosophic task to show that PM cannot be a
mathematical foundation for mathematics, we can again appre-
ciate Wittgenstein's claim in P/-127 that a philosopher should
assemble reminders for a particular purpose. The descriptions we
give ourselves of PM and actual mathematics remind us that they
are not the same, and hence that PM is not a mathematical foun-
dation.
14. Book II of RFM was written around 1940. In the March
1958 issue of The Review of Metaphysics, Alan Ross Anderson
published his "Mathematics and the Language Game", which is
an unfavorable review of RFM. (It is reprinted on pp. 481-490
of BP.) Anderson charges that Wittgenstein's remarks on logicism
are out of data since for the past twenty-five years no one of any
ability has seriously maintained that mathematics can be reduced
to logic. Anderson points out on pp. 484-485 of BP that the
contemporary position is " 'mathematical logic' is logic studied


164
Wittgenstein's Objections to Other Positions
mathematically, and that using mathematical techniques to study
the syntax of artificial languages does not somehow count as
'reducing' mathematics to logic." Of course, Wittgenstein was not
quite as many years behind the times as Anderson charges. But
even if Wittgenstein's attack on the position that mathematics can
be reduced to logic were an attack on a view no longer widely
held, I think that his attack has value. (My personal opinion is
that the view that mathematics can be reduced to logic is very
widely held although maybe it is not held by the best logicians.)
Wittgenstein's attack is valuable because it shows that we do not
need mathematical results to conclude that mathematics is not
and cannot be logic, viz., a logistic axiomatization. At least at the
present time, we cannot identify logic with mathematics. But
despite the value of establishing by non-mathematical means that
mathematics cannot be reduced to logic, and hence that a logistic
axiomatization cannot be a mathematical foundation, the entire
issue of finding mathematical foundations may seem of little im-
portance. May it not still be the case that a logistic axiomatization,
such as PM, can provide a philosophic foundation for mathe-
matics? For instance, could not the fact that no descriptive con-
stants need be used in PM be grounds for the ontological claim
that mathematical propositions are not truths about some objects?
Or could not the fact that almost all of the re-expressions of actual
mathematical propositions are universally valid formulae be
grounds for the epistemological claim that mathematical necessity
is logical necessity? Indeed, it is not immediately obvious that
PM's failure to provide a mathematical foundation shows that it
cannot provide a philosophic foundation. Of course, PM would
not be the philosophy of mathematics; our answers to the three
groups of questions from section 1 answered by reference to PM
would be the philosophy of mathematics. However, we can find
in RFM remarks that establish that a logistic axiomatization can-
not provide philosophic foundations either.
15. In RFM the remarks that establish that a logistic axio-
matization is not mathematics are those which establish that a
logistic axiomatization does not provide a philosophic foundation
for mathematics. It would be misleading for me to suggest that


Wittgenstein's Objections to Other Positions
165
Wittgenstein made the distinction between mathematical and
philosophic foundations. I submit that when he claimed in V-16
that he was going to show by external means, i.e., non-mathe-
matical means, that a logistic axiomatization could not fulfill its
office he meant that he was going to show that such an axio-
matization could provide neither a mathematical nor a philosophic
foundation for mathematics. So now we have to look at his non-
mathematical arguments that PM is not mathematics as showing
that PM is not sufficiently like mathematics so that it can provide
philosophic foundations for mathematics. This phrase "sufficiently
like" is annoyingly vague; but I use it so that we will not restrict
a philosopher of mathematics to talking only about the sentences
actually used in mathematics expressed exactly as they are used.
Some alterations may help us appreciate philosophic points. But
to discuss Wittgenstein's arguments that PM cannot provide phil-
osophic foundations for current mathematics because PM is not
sufficiently like current mathematics, I must at least give a neces-
sary condition for "sufficiently like". If we re-express an actual
mathematical proposition M as a proposition M-l where M-l is
not a proposition actually used in mathematics, M-l is sufficiently
like M only if the mathematics done with M could have been
done if we would have originally started out with M-l. For in-
stance, if M is 2 + 3 = 5, candidates for a suitable M-l are the
strict finitistic "The counting together of two and three is counting
five" and the PM language re-expression of 2 + 3 = 5. Certainly
this condition for applying "sufficiently like" is necessary. You
could not find out anything about mathematics by talking about
forms of expression which could not even be used to do mathe-
matics. Of course, once we recognize this necessary condition for
applying "sufficiently like", we use Wittgenstein's arguments that
logic is not mathematics to conclude that logic is not enough like
mathematics to provide a philosophic foundation for mathematics.
This is especially true of the arguments from section 9 of this
chapter, where we noted that the PM correlates of theorems in
the differential calculus could not be used to do calculus.
We should note that to establish the philosophic irrelevance of
logic, e.g., PM, it was very important to use non-mathematical ar-


166
Wittgenstein's Objections to Other Positions
guments. If Wittgenstein had shown only that PM was mathe-
matically inadequate, he would not have shown that PM could
not be used to do a portion of mathematics. For instance, if he
had shown that it was incomplete or inconsistent, he would not
have shown that PM could not be used to do some mathematics,
or when slightly altered to make it consistent, to do some or all
mathematics. If PM could be used to do a portion of mathematics,
it could still have the office of providing a philosophic foundation
for that portion. As we shall see in the next chapter, Wittgenstein
believed that providing a philosophy of a portion of mathematics
is significant. So before he would dismiss PM, he would want to
be sure that PM is philosophically irrelevant for anything that we
now call mathematics.
16. In RFM we can find two other kinds of reasons for
holding that a logistic axiomatization should not be used for
developing a philosophy of mathematics. The first is: The belief
that mathematics can be properly expressed as logical truths in a
logistic axiomatization is based on an incorrect metaphysics. The
second is: If we believe that mathematics can be properly ex-
pressed as logical truths in a logistic axiomatization, we get a
distorted idea of what mathematics actually is.
17. In IV-48 Wittgenstein wrote:
'Mathematical logic' has completely deformed the thinking of mathe-
maticians and of philosophers, by setting up a superficial interpreta-
tion of the forms of our everyday language as an analysis of the struc-
ture of facts. Of course in this it has only continued to build on the
Aristotelian logic.
This passage is relevant to the following kind of defense of a
logistic axiomatization as providing philosophic foundations. A
philosopher may hold the view that to get the correct ontology,
viz., to determine what there must be for there to be the world
that we experience, we must rewrite the sentences of our ordinary
language in their logical form. A sentence S is written in logical
form if it is written as LS, LS has the same truth conditions as S,
but as long as S can be either true or false it is impossible that


Wittgenstein's Objections to Other Positions
167
any of the referring terms of LS fail to have referents although
the referring terms of S may fail to have referents. For instance,
if S is in Russell's famous "The present King of France is bald",
his analysis of it as "There is something X is bald and a King of
France and anything which is a bald King of France is X" has
the logical form of S. Or at least it is closer to the logical form
of S. (We cannot definitely say that Russell's analysis gives the
logical form because we cannot be sure that the predicate "King
of France" has a single quality as a referent.)
A re-expression of the sentences of our ordinary language in
logical form can be called an Ideal Language. Once we have an
Ideal Language, a correct ontology can be developed simply by
listing what kinds of things must be referred to in order to use
the Ideal Language. (Since I am here presenting a criticism of
philosophizing by means of an Ideal Language, it is not necessary
to specify what devices in a sentence of an Ideal Language would
be used to refer if the Ideal Language were used.) A philosopher
who philosophizes by means of an Ideal Language may be in-
clined to say that the Ideal Language shows him the structure of
facts since the Ideal Language shows him what there must be for
there to be the kinds of facts he experiences; it shows him the
simples composing the facts and how these simples are connected
to form facts. (Crudely put: An Ideal Language philosopher be-
lieves that God made the world in the image and likeness of the
Predicate Calculus.)
An Ideal Language philosopher may claim that he does not
absolutely need the notation of symbolic logic to state sentences
of his Ideal Language. However, the multiplicity of ways of
making assertions in natural languages makes it seem very un-
likely that any single natural language or way of speaking within
a natural language would show the structure of facts, if there is
such a structure to be shown. I do not think it is unjust to accuse
the rigid notations of mathematical logic, even Aristotelian logic,
of giving birth to the idea of an Ideal Language. It may be unjust
to accuse logic of deforming the thinking of mathematicians and
philosophers since the notation of logic does not represent itself
as showing the structure of facts. By itself, mathematical logic is


168
Wittgenstein's Objections to Other Positions
simply an extension of Aristotelian logic and Aristotelian logic
is merely a tool for helping us assess deductive validity.
In his Tractatus Wittgenstein was an Ideal Language philoso-
pher. In his Philosophical Investigations, especially in 37 to 65,
he rejected the metaphysics underlying Ideal Language philoso-
phy. This underlying metaphysics is the belief that there are
objects which must exist if there are to be the kinds of facts we
experience. These objects which must exist could be called basic
objects or simples. This belief in simples implies that there is one
correct description of the world. The one correct description
would, of course, be a description of the order and connection of
the simples, or any description whose logical form was a correct
description of the order and connection of the simples. (As we
shall see in the last chapter, strict finitism is inconsistent with the
metaphysics underlying Ideal Language philosophy.)
But what has the rejection of Ideal Language philosophy to do
with rejection of PM as providing philosophic foundations of
mathematics? Philosophizing by talking about a logistic axio-
matization, or at least mathematics re-expressed as logical for-
mulae, would be how an Ideal Language philosopher philoso-
phizes about mathematics. An Ideal Language philosopher would
try to define as many referring terms as he could since if a term
used to refer can be defined that means that a complex of con-
ditions must be present for its referent to be present. In other
words, the referent of a definable referring term is not a simple.
I think that Frege and Russell have shown that if we define all
mathematical terms which seem to refer we will re-express all
mathematical truths in formulae which for the most part are
logical truths, and in which no referring term need occur unbound
by a quantifier. So PM is how mathematics would look in an
Ideal Language. Hence rejection of Ideal Language philosophy
is a rejection of one of the best reasons for philosophizing about
mathematics by means of talking about a logistic axiomatization.
18. We shall now turn to an investigation of the charge that
believing that mathematical propositions can be properly ex-
pressed as logical truths in a logistic axiomatization gives a dis-
torted view of mathematics. In IV-24 Wittgenstein wrote:


Wittgenstein's Objections to Other Positions
169
rhe harmful thing about logical technique is that it makes us forget
the special mathematical technique. Whereas logical technique is only
an auxiliary technique in mathematics. For example it sets up certain
connexions between different techniques.
It is almost as if one tried to say that cabinet-making consisted in
glueing.
For instance, a mathematician may establish several Lemmas by
his mathematical techniques and then use logic to deduce his
Theorem from his Lemmas. It would be odd to say that all his
reasoning was like the deduction of the Theorem from the Lem-
mas. I do believe that Wittgenstein was correct in claiming that
accepting PM as providing a philosophic foundation for mathe-
matics can lead to the distorted epistemological view that if
mathematicians did their job properly they would simply derive
conclusions from premisses. Even if one is a non-mathematician,
as I am, a brief look at what good mathematicians do in their
lectures and papers makes it seem preposterous that anyone could
believe that the real justification of a theorem lies in the deduction
of a logistic re-expression of it from some axioms. Yet, I fear
that such a belief is still widely held because it is still believed
that PM provides a philosophic foundation for mathematics.
Wittgenstein also claimed that the belief that mathematics can
be expressed as logical truths in a logistic axiomatization gives us
a distorted idea of the conditions which make a proposition a
meaningul mathematical proposition. In IV-46 he wrote:
The curse of the invasion of mathematics by mathematical logic is
that now any proposition can be represented in a mathematical sym-
bolism, and this makes us feel obliged to understand it. Although of
course this method of writing is nothing but the translation of vague
ordinary prose
The kind of proposition Wittgenstein was particularly concerned
about was assertions of the existence of a number which we do
not know how to compute. An assertion that 777 occurs in the
decimal expansion of Pi is an example of the kind of proposition
that Wittgenstein had in mind. Let us call "777 occurs in the
decimal expansion of Pi" B. In the notation of mathematical logic


170
Wittgenstein's Objections to Other Positions
B could be represented by a well formed formula. Let us call the
well formed formula representing B: FB. Since FB is a well
formed formula which can be interpreted we believe that it can
be understood when interpreted. But what should we understand
when we read the interpreted FB? The formula representing B,
when read in ordinary language, may not sound at all like B. So
at first glance, it may seem inaccurate to claim that the formulae
of mathematical logic only translate our vague ordinary prose.
However, FB will begin with an existential quantifier, or its equi-
valent, and this quantifier only translates the vague ordinary
"there is" to "exists". Consequently, when we read FB it will tell
us in a complicated way that there is a 777 in the decimal ex-
pansion of Pi. But now because B is expressed so impressively
as FB we feel that it must say something; it looks just like all the
other formulae. The only thing we can think of having FB say is
that 777 exists in the decimal expansion of Pi, and because
"exists" has its ordinary sense we are saying that 777 is some-
where in the decimal expansion of Pi. But as we have seen in the
discussion of the law of excluded middle in Chapter III, section
15, we do not know what it is like for there to be 777 in the
decimal expansion of Pi unless we know how to compute 777 as
part of the decimal expansion of Pi. If we do not know what it
would be like for B and FB to be true, we do not understand
them. A reductio ad absurdum proof of FB would be a perfectly
good formal proof of FB. Still it would not give us understanding
of FB since it would not show us what it is like for it to be true.
But if we had a formal proof of FB we would think we had
something that had to make sense.
I think that the preceding discussion of B and FB show the
justice of the following complaint from 11-25.
A psychological disadvantage of proofs that construct propositions is
that they easily make us forget that the sense of the result is not to be
read off from this by itself, but from the proof. In this respect the
intrusion of the Russellian symbolism into the proofs has done a great
deal of harm.
Of course, he meant that proofs in the Russellian symbolism do


Wittgenstein's Objections to Other Positions
171
not give a sense to the proved proposition and thus the proofs do
not provide understanding of the propostion. And if no one
thought that PM had a philosophic office or mathematical office
there would be no intrusion of the Russellian symbolism into
mathematics.
19. I have now completed my examination of Wittgenstein's
devastating critique of Logicism. I submit that he has reminded
us, by getting us to take a closer look at what is done in actual
mathematics and what is done in a logistic axiomatization, that a
logistic axiomatization is so unlike actual mathematics that not
only can it not provide mathematical and philosophic foundations
for mathematics but also that the serious attempt to reduce mathe-
matics to logic has produced a distorted view of mathematics.
We shall now turn to his critique of Formalism and Intuitionism.
20. RFM does not contain any long critique of Formalism as
it does of Logicism. Although, of course, as we noted in section
1 of this chapter, the recurring attacks on the position that mathe-
matics needs a consistency proof to be certified as being proper
mathematics can certainly be regarded as criticisms of the goal
of Formalism. In his paper "On the Infinite", translated and
printed on pp. 134 to 151 of BP, David Hilbert stated the goal
of Formalism as:
The goal of my theory is to establish once and for all the certitude of
mathematical methods.
This certitude would be established by showing the consistency
of the principles used in mathematics. However, Formalists
planned to establish the consistency of mathematics by establish-
ing the consistency of a logistic axiomatization of mathematics
such as PM. They hoped to establish the consistency of a logistic
axiomatization by finitistic means. Here "finitistic" means that
all the statements made in establishing the consistency of the
axiom system would be justified by reference to uninterpreted
formulae or finite sets of uninterpreted formulae of the axiom
system. (If it were not for the fact that Formalists would use
mathematical induction in their consistency proofs, I believe that
their finitism is close to what I have called strict finitism.) But


172
Wittgenstein's Objections to Other Positions
the nature of Formalistic metamathematics is not important for
this critique of Formalism. What is of interest here is the fact that
Formalists regard a logistic axiomatization of mathematics as
mathematics. If they did not, they would not have tried to estab-
lish the consistency of mathematics by establishing the consistency
of a logistic axiomatization. But in regard to mathematics being
a logistic axiomatization of mathematics Formalists differ from
Logicists. Formalists hold that an uninterpreted logistic axio-
matization is mathematics while Logicists identify the interpreted
axiom system with mathematics. (I should note however that
Formalists such as Haskell B. Curry include the meaningful meta-
linguistic statements about a logistic axiomatization as part of
mathematics. See Curry's "On the Definition and Nature of
Mathematics" reprinted on pp. 152 to 156 of BP.) The identifi-
cation of actual mathematics with an uninterpreted axiom system
compounds the logicists' error of identifying mathematics with a
logistic axiomatization. Once one accepted that mathematics
could be properly presented as an uninterpreted axiom system,
it is easy to drift into the position that mathematics is a game
with symbols.
Of course, we can now take all of Wittgenstein's arguments
against identifying mathematics with a logistic axiomatization and
treat them as objections to Formalism. However, in IV-4 we find
two objections which seem directed against the Formalists' ad-
ditional error, - additional to the erors which they share with
Logicism
that mathematics is a game with symbols. In IV-4
Wittgenstein first asked how a Formalist distinguishes the game
he calls mathematics from other games. The argumentive force
of this question is to show that a Formalist will have to say that
his game of deducing formulae is mathematics because these
formulae represent mathematical propositions which are not un-
interpreted formulae. Secondly, he pointed out that a philosopher
who claims that mathematics is a game with symbols will not be
able to talk properly about what mathematicians actually do. A
Formalist would speak absurdly if he said that mathematicians
pass out of mathematics when they cease to do formal deductions
in an uninterpreted calculus. But a Formalist would have to hold


Wittgenstein's Objections to Other Positions
173
this absurdity if he held that doing mathematics is really playing
the game of constructing formal deductions. In 111-35 Wittgen-
stein also observed that we do not regard mathematics as dealing
with signs and he took this observation as showing that mathe-
matics does not deal with signs. And he was right in so taking it
as long as he wanted to restrict himself to philosophizing about
what is now called mathematics. Or better: He overlooked only
that small portion of current mathematics called metamathe-
matics.
21. Because of its eccentricity, Wittgenstein's argument against
a concern for consistency may not convince many that Formalism
is in error. However, his arguments that a formal axiom system
for mathematics is not mathematics seem to me to show that even
if Formalists could establish the consistency of such axiom sys-
tems they would not have done much towards establishing the
consistency of actual mathematics. When we pass from Wittgen-
stein's critique of Formalism to his critique of Intuitionism we
have even less material. I read the following remark from Ap-
pendix 11-18 as a mild criticism of Intuitionism.
Finitism and behaviorism are quite similar trends. Both say, but surely,
all we have here is ... Both deny the existence of something, both with
a view to escaping from a confusion.
I submit that here "finitism" refers to the Intuitionism of Brou-
wer and Heyting. A behaviorist says that surely we have only
bodies, bodily processes, and bodily properties while an Intui-
tionist says that if we do talk about some objects in mathematics
they surely do not exist independently of human thought. To
avoid the confusion of thinking that there are minds and mental
processes a behaviorist denies the existence of meaning to sen-
tences which can be construed as indicating the existence of minds.
Such a sentence would be: "Tom thought that Joan had something
serious on her mind." An Intuitionist would deny the existence
of meaning to sentences which suggest that there are mathematical
objects existing independently of human thought. (I write the awk-
ward "deny the existence of meaning to sentences" as an interpre-
tation of Wittgenstein's "deny the existence of something".) For


174
Wittgenstein's Objections to Other Positions
instance, the sentence "111 occurs in the decimal expansion of Pi"
suggests that this expansion is laid out in some way outside of hu-
man thought and that it is the job of mathematicians to discover
whether or not 777 is in this expansion. Hence, the Intuitionist
denies the existence of meaning for this sentence about 777 in Pi.
Wittgenstein differed from behaviorism and Intuitionism because
his view was that we should try to understand these ways of
speaking that suggest confusion. We should not try to eliminate
them when we do psychology, mathematics, or speak in our
ordinary language. So although Wittgenstein may not have been
in disagreement with the mathematical ontology and epistemology
of Intuitionism, I think that we can interpret this remark as
showing that he disagreed with their way of doing philosophy.
Intuitionists are Marxists in that they want to change language
rather than understand it while Wittgenstein was Hegelian in that
he wanted merely to understand language.
22. Of course, all the remarks in RFM which state or suggest
strict finitism can be interpreted as criticism of a view that mathe-
matical propositions are simply experimentally justified proposi-
tions. Strict finitism is an account of how experimental proposi-
tions are converted into mathematical ones. So if Wittgenstein
was a strict finitist, we can conclude that he rejected a view that
all mathematical propositions are simply well justified empirical
truths. Also if strict finitism is an adequate account of even a
small part of mathematics, we can conclude that not all mathe-
matical propositions are simply well justified empirical proposi-
tions.
Wittgenstein also gave other arguments that mathematical
propositions are not simply well justified empirical truths. In 1-156
he cited as evidence of the non-empirical character of mathe-
matics the fact that if we add 2 apples to 2 apples but somehow
only have 3 apples altogether, we do not reject 2 + 2 = 4 but
say that somehow one must have disappeared. But he did not
ignore the fact that empirical conditions are necessary for there
to be mathematical truths although as we have seen he did deny
that any empirical conditions were truth conditions for mathe-
matical propositions. In 11-64 to 11-76 and also in V-14 and


Wittgenstein's Objections to Other Positions
175
V-15 he stressed that for there to be mathematics people must
agree on calculations and each person must agree on his own
calculations almost each time he does them. He also stressed that
there must be at least enough order in nature so that we can carry
out strict finitistic proofs. He went so far as to write in 11-73:
But on the other hand I say again: 'Calculating is right - as it is done'.
There can be no mistake of calculation in '12 X 12 = 144'. Why? This
proposition has assumed a place among the rules.
But is '12 x 12 = 144' the assertion that it is natural to all men to
work out 12 X 12 in such a way that the answer is 144?
23. Wittgenstein gave a negative answer to the question at the
close of the passage just quoted. He did not conclude that mathe-
matical propositions are anthropological propositions on how men
infer and calculate. Nor did he conclude that mathematical propo-
sitions assert confusion in other people's calculations and our
own proofs will not reign. The gist of his argument is in 11-75.
He pointed out that the conditions for speaking in a certain way,
- here doing mathematics
are not what is asserted by the
propositions of that way of speaking. If we did hold that proposi-
tions assert the existence of conditions necessary for their use,
then all propositions assert at least that there is not too much
confusion for people to have language. But if all propositions
assert that there is not too much confusion for people to have
language then the proposition that there is not too much confu-
sion to have language becomes a proposition which can be asserted
by any proposition whatsoever. But since it is absurd to conclude
that "There is not too much confusion for people to have lan-
guage" can be asserted by any sentence we pick, it is absurd to
conclude that propositions assert the existence of the conditions
necessary for their use. (In this case, I think you could say that
Wittgenstein gave what could be called a fairly standard type of
philosophical argument.)
24. I do not think that Wittgenstein's criticisms of empirical
interpretations are particularly original. However, I do believe
that they are correct. The interesting aspect of Wittgenstein's
thought on mathematical and empirical propositions is how closely


176
Wittgenstein's Objections to Other Positions
he connects them in his strict finitistic account of the creation of
mathematical propositions.
25. We now come to an end of our examination of Wittgen-
stein's critique of other positions on both the philosophic and
mathematical foundations of mathematics. Although it may be
harsh to call these other positions madness, he has shown them
to be defective. Our next task is to determine whether or not
Wittgenstein's position is sanity in the midst of this madness.


V
ASSESSMENT OF RFM
If I wished to find out what sort of thing arithmetic
is, I should be very content to have investigated the
case of finite cardinal arithmetic. For
(a) this would lead me on to all the more compli-
cated cases,
(b) a finite cardinal arithmetic is not incomplete,
it has no gaps which are then filled in by the
rest of arithmetic.
CBlue Book, p. 20 [Blackwell, 1958])
1. This opening quotation, although not from Wittgenstein's
RFM, is, I believe, the kind of defense which Wittgenstein would
give of the philosophy of mathematics developed in RFM against
the most obvious objection to it. I believe that the most obvious
objection to the strict finitistic philosophy developed in RFM is
that it is at best a philosophy of elementary Euclidean plane
geometry and what Wittgenstein called finite cardinal arithmetic,
viz., addition, subtraction, multiplication, and division with finite
numbers. In this chapter, we shall consider this and other objec-
tions to Wittgenstein's position and see whether or not he can
meet some of these objections.
When I talk about criticizing Wittgenstein's philosophy in
RFM, I should stress that I am talking about criticizing that
philosophy of mathematics which I have called strict finitism.
When I defend Wittgenstein against these criticisms, I will really
be defending what I called at the end of Chapter III "open-ended
strict finitism". On what basis shall I criticize strict finitism? Since
Wittgenstein was not interested in providing mathematical foun-
dations for mathematics and since a strict finitist would hold that


178
Assessment of RFM
each proof is sufficient foundation for the proposition it proves,
there is no point in appraising strict finitism as providing mathe-
matical foundations. However, for those who are concerned with
mathematical foundations, strict finitism can be recommended as
the best justified metamathematics to be used in consistency
proofs, etc. But for my part, I shall appraise strict finitism on
whether or not it is an adequate philosophy of mathematics or
can be slightly altered so that it becomes one.
2. The criticisms of strict finitism as a philosophy of mathe-
matics fall under two headings. The first criticism is that the strict
finitists' paradigm of necessary truth for understanding mathe-
matical necessity, viz., "The number of letters in 'Bismarck' is 8"
is unacceptable. It is alleged to be unacceptable because it is said
that mathematical truths cannot be construed as being this kind
of proposition. Hence, the objection goes, even if strict finitists
develop an appealing ontology and epistemology for necessary
truths like their paradigm they still would have provided no phi-
losophy of mathematics. (From now on I shall replace "construed
as being of the same kind as the strict finitists' paradigm of
mathematical necessity" with "falling under the strict finitists'
paradigm".) The second criticism is that although strict finitists
may be able to develop an appealing ontology and epistemology
of a portion of mathematics by construing mathematical proposi-
tions as being of the same kind as "The number of letters in
'Bismarck' is 8", acceptance of "The number of letters in 'Bis-
marck' is 8" as a necessary truth requires an undesirable onto-
logy and epistemology in general.
3. The criticism that mathematical propositions do not fall
under the strict finitists' paradigm can have a strong and a weak
sense. In the strong sense, it is the claim that no mathematical
proposition falls under the strict finitists' paradigm. In the weak
sense, it is the claim that some, actually many, mathematical
propositions do not fall under the strict finitists' paradigm. In
Chapter I, section 30 I pointed out that if a mathematical propo-
sition fell under the strict finitists' paradigm, this meant that the
paradigm proposition was sufficiently like the mathematical


Assessment of RFM
179
proposition. From what was said in section 15 of Chapter IV it
follows, then, that if a mathematical proposition M falls under a
strict finitists' paradigm the mathematical truth expressed through
M could just as well have been originally proved as a strict
finitistic proposition. Hence, the strong sense of the above criti-
cism is that if we had started out by doing strict finitistic mathe-
matics we would not yet have done anything which we now call
mathematics. The proof in Chapter II, section 26 of the strict
finitistic version of 2 4- 3 = 5, viz., "The counting together of 2
and 3 is counting 5", shows the absurdity of the strong sense.
Indeed I suspect that the first mathematics that many people do
is done in a strict finitistic way. (Here by "do mathematics" I
mean "prove". I do not mean merely compute by means of rules.)
So at least a portion of arithmetic can be done, i.e., rules of
computation justified, by talking only about observable patterns
and manipulations of these patterns to which arithmetic terms
apply.
4. Simple geometric propositions such as "The shortest dis-
tance between two points is a straight line" can be established by
strict finitistic means. To see this, consider Picture 4 below.
PICTURE 4
Á
B
Á
B
(Taking shortest route from A to B.)
Á
B (Recognizing that the picture of taking the
shortest route is a straight line.)
In Stage 1, A and B are perspicuous as points. If we would not
call tiny dots points, there is nothing observable that we would
call points. But it is proper to talk about observable points; we
ask for the distance from one point to another. Stage 2 is a
memorable picture of the operation of going from A to B by the
shortest route. Stage 3, of course, looks the same as Stage 2 but
Stage 1:
Stage 2:
Stage 3:


180
Assessment of RFM
it is a different stage because we are looking at it in a different
way. In Stage 3 we judge the quality rather than the quantity of
the line as we did in Stage 2. We can identify the line in Stage 3
as a straight line if it lies along a straight edge or if it is what
people call straight. Once we identify the line as a straight line,
Stage 3 can be surveyed so that we see that drawing a picture of
the shortest distance between A and B is drawing a straight line
between A and B. Note that this truth about points and lines was
established without any reference to extensionless points or
breathless lines. This is why I speak of strict finitism having an
appealing ontology and epistemology. Another example of a
geometric truth established by strict finitistic means is the proof
of: "The square constructed on the diagonal of a given square is
a square with twice the area of the given square" given by
Socrates in the Meno. But, of course, the doctrine of recollection
is not part of strict finitistic philosophy.
5. Hence, strict finitism as a philosophy of mathematics is
superior to Logicism because strict finitists philosophize about
at least a portion of mathematics since they philosophize about
that portion which can be done by strict finitistic means. But the
Logicists philosophize by talking about what is not and cannot
be mathematics, viz., a logistic axiomatization.
6. We must now consider the weak sense of the claim that
mathematical propositions do not fall under the strict finitists'
paradigm. The weak sense is that MANY mathematical proposi-
tions do not fall under the strict finitists' paradigm. In the weak
sense, the claim is true; and this, in my opinion is the only serious
defect in the philosophy of mathematics developed in RFM. We
have seen in Chapter I, section 20, 21, and 22, while examining his
remarks on Cantor's and Dedekind's theorems, that as presently
interpreted these theorems would not fall under the strict finitists'
paradigm. But unfortunately we do not have to go to the mathe-
matics of the real numbers to find mathematical propositions
which do not fall under the strict finitists' paradigm. Mathematical
induction cannot be accepted by a strict finitist. For a strict finitist
a principle of induction would have to read as follows. (I shall
write "provable by strict finitistic means" as SF provable.) For


Assessment of RFM
181
any operations f and g, if it is SF provable that f (1) = g (1) and
if it is SF provable that f (n-1) = g (n-1) then it is SF provable
that f (n) = g (n); then for all natural numbers n, it is SF provable
that f (n) = g (n). A strict finitist could not accept this principle
of induction because he could not find a memorable picture of
applying one operation which would also be a picture of applying
any operation. In other words, the quantification over operations
removes it from the realm of strict finitistic propositions. Also a
strict finitist would never assume that it is SF provable that
f (n-1) -- g (n-1) for n-1 picked at random. He would not make
such as assumption because he realizes that if n-1 is too large to get
a perspicuous set of objects for applying n-1, he may not be able
to have a strict finitistic proof that f (n-1) = g (n-1). So, since a
strict finitist would not make an induction assumption, he could
not use induction even if something led him to accept the prin-
ciple.
7. However, propositions even more elementary than prin-
ciples of mathematical induction fail to fall under the strict
finitists' paradigm. Addition, multiplication, and division with
many numbers or with very large numbers cannot be justified by
strict finitistic means; such calculations can be done only in the
decimal. Actually, I suspect that when computations get very
large they can only be done by electronic computers without any
possibility of justification by strict finitistic means or by pencil
and paper computations in the decimal notation. To be sure, a
strict finitist could use abacuses to get proof-patterns. But sooner
or later the abacuses would get so huge that they could not justify
computations done in the decimal notation. Of course, Wittgen-
stein was aware of the fact that strict finitistic proofs, i.e., proofs
in the stroke system, cannot justify most computations in the
decimal notation. He pointed this out in 11-51, 11-54, and 11-57.
But I do not find in RFM any account of how mathematical
truths are justified when they are not justified by strict finitistic
means. Maybe I have not found such an account because I have
blinded myself to any conventionalism in Wittgenstein's philoso-
phy of mathematics done within a system.
8. So we must conclude that strict finitism, and hence Witt-


182
Assessment of RFM
genstein's philosophy of mathematics as well, are philosophies of
only a little bit of what is now called mathematics. I suppose that
strict finitism would be a philosophy of all of mathematics if the
average IQ were forty points lower. But such a conjecture hardly
puts strict finitism in a favorable light.
9. Is there any defense of strict finitism? There is no defense
for strict finitists who hold that all mathematics should be done
in a strict finitistic way since this cannot be done. Nor can there
be any defense of a strict finitist who claims that we should
eschew all mathematics which cannot be done by strict finitistic
means. A philosopher ought not urge a return to barbarism.
Similarly, philosophers of mind or philosophers of perception
ought not to urge a return to the barbaric stage of talking only of
bodies or sensations. Part of the point of Wittgenstein's claim in
P/-124 that philosophy leaves everything as it is, is a rebuke of
such primitivism.
10. However, the opening quotation of this chapter shows
two mutually compatible ways of defending strict finitism as a
philosophy of mathematics. Point (a) of that quotation suggests
defending strict finitism by adopting what I called, at the end of
Chapter III, open-ended strict finitism. An open-ended strict
finitist accepts strict finitism as an adequate philosophy of only
as much mathematics as can be done by strict finitistic means.
However, he resolves to understand the remainder of mathematics
by deviating as little as possible from strict finitistic philosophy.
In particular an open-ended strict finitist will resolve not to
abandon the following beliefs (and I believe that I will be listing
positions which Wittgenstein would be reluctant to abandon). We
do not know what a proposition means as a mathematical propo-
sition unless it is proved. A mathematical proof must show us
what it is like for the proved proposition to be necessarily true.
Mathematical truths must be proved but they do not have truth
conditions. Mathematical truths are not determined as true by
conventions. Without certain psychological and physical facts
there would not be the mathematical truths which we now have
because we would not have the proofs which we now have although
these empirical facts are not truth conditions for mathematical


Assessment of RFM
183
propositions. Extant mathematics is sufficiently justified as long
as each proposition is backed by a convincing proof; there is no
need for a mathematical foundation for mathematics. Mathe-
matical foundations are only more mathematics. Of course, an
open-ended strict finitist will have to abandon the belief that all
terms which appear in mathematical propositions have applica-
tion to some observable paradigm for applying that term. In other
words, an open-ended strict finitist cannot demand that all proofs
be pictorial. Unfortunately, it is not at all clear how the analysis
given of pictorial proofs will lead us on to give an analysis of the
more complicated cases where the analysis of the more com-
plicated cases must be such that it preserves the above strict
finitistic beliefs. So a strict finitistic philosopher of mathematics
is not refuted by the severe limitations of strict finitistic mathe-
matics if he shifts to open-ended strict finitism. However, if he
wants to develop a philosophy of all extant mathematics he has
only a pious anti-platonistic wish without any clear program of
how to fulfill it.
11. Still a strict finitist's wish is not completely unfulfilled
since his philosophy does cover the arithmetic of (small) finite
cardinal numbers. And it covers it well. Point (b) of the opening
quotation suggests defending strict finitism by pointing out that
it does well what it does do. I have, in effect, been arguing
throughout this essay that strict finitism is a good philosophy of
(small) finite cardinal arithmetic. So I shall not here defend it on
that point. Here, I shall consider whether or not strict finitism is
worthy of attention because it does a good job in a small area
although it fails elsewhere. In his Blue Book, pp. 17 to 20, Witt-
genstein claimed that we should be satisfied with understanding
particular cases and that we should not try to attain a general
account. On p. 44 of his Blue Book he pointed out that putting a
few books in order is progress when we organize a library, even
if we do not know how these books fit in with the other books.
According to Wittgenstein, a similar situation exists in philosophy.
Attaining philosophic clarity about one small area is philosophic
progress even if we do not know how this philosophic solution
will fit in with others. So clearly Wittgenstein would not doubt


184
Assessment
of RFM
that strict finitism is a satisfactory philosophy of the mathematics
which it does cover simply because it covers only a small amount
of mathematics. However, Wittgenstein's satisfaction with a par-
tial account of mathematics is based on a premiss which is un-
justified unless he has a philosophy of all of mathematics. He
needs a premiss that there are different kind of mathematics. I.e.,
he needs a premiss that there is not one body of interdependent
mathematical truths nor is there one ontology and epistemology
of mathematics. The clause: "a finite cardinal arithmetic is not
incomplete", from point (b) of the opening quotation shows that
he held such a premiss. But such a premiss cannot be justified
until one has a satisfactory philosophy of all of mathematics.
Until we have philosophized about all of mathematics we cannot
be certain that we will not have to revise some of what we have
done before. So although strict finitism may be defended by
pointing out that it does well what it does do, this defense is
meager since a bit more philosophy of mathematics may reveal
that we were mistaken in thinking that strict finitism was a satis-
factory philosophy of (small) finite cardinal arithmetic.
12. As we have just seen, strict finitism as a philosophy is far
from being well justified because of the severe limitations of strict
finitistic mathematics. We now come to our final question. Is
strict finitism unacceptable as a tentatively held philosophy of a
part of mathematics because it has unacceptable philosophic im-
plications outside the philosophy of mathematics? It would be
dogmatic to reject strict finitism simply because acceptance of
strict finitism requires acceptance of synthetic a priori truths. Ex-
hibition of such truths as "The reverse of the letters of OVER
is REVO" refute the dogma that there is only logical necessity.
If one wants to reject strict finitism because it accepts non-logical
necessity, he must attack the ontology and epistemology which
form a strict finitist's explication of this kind of necessity.
13. I shall appraise only one aspect of the ontology and
epistemology underlying strict finitism's explication of non-logical
necessity. This is the ontological claim that observable objects
have essences conjoined with the epistemological claim that we
invent rather than discover what these essences are.


Assessment of RFM
185
Let me expand a bit on strict finitism's doctrine of essence.
Individual observable objects, and not kinds of objects, have
essences. But individuals have essences as being one of a kind.
Thus, if I write the Bismarck-token, Bismarck, it is of the essence
of this token that it have eight letters. But this token must have
eight letters only because if it did not it would not be a Bismarck-
token. So before we can determine that a property P of an object
is essential to it we must first identify it as one of a kind. Once
we have identified it as one of a kind, then if we cannot conceive
of the object as being of the kind without having property P we
decide that P is essential to the individual object. We saw in
Chapter II, section 10 that we do not determine that an object
must have a property P to be of kind K by logical deduction from
the concept of being of kind K. Often when we determine that
object X of kind K must have property P to be of kind K, we
are just then including having P as part of our concept of being
of kind K. For instance, only after we determine that a particular
OVER must have a REVO as its reverse to be an OVER, would
we include having REVO as its reverse as part of our concept of
being an OVER.
What if anything is wrong with this doctrine of essence? It
certainly avoids the difficult epistemological problem of account-
ing for how we discover which properties of a member of a kind
are essential to it so that it can be of that kind. But the claim that
we invent the essence leads to the following kind of objection.
Someone may object that this doctrine of essence is based on the
belief that objects are what we take them to be. This objection
correctly calls attention to the fact that if a strict finitistic type
proof does not lead us to decide that an object X must have a
property P to be of kind K, then P would not be essential for
being a K. Thus if we did not prove that an OVER must have
REVO as its reverse, having REVO as its reverse would not be
essential for any figure to be an OVER. A person, in this case,
could admit the possibility of there being OVERs whose reverse
was not REVO. (In RFM V-42 to V-44, Wittgenstein gave ex-
amples of how people could have different concepts of what it
is to be of a certain kind from those which we normally have.)


186
Assessment of RFM
So a strict finitist is forced to admit that he believes that two
groups of people could identify an object as being of a particular
kind and yet correctly go on to develop mutually inconsistent
concepts of what it is to be of that kind. For instance, a strict
finitist would admit that two groups of people could identify a
figure as an OVER. One group could then go on to say correctly
that it must have REVO as its reverse to be an OVER. But the
other group could say correctly that it need not have REVO as
its reverse to be an OVER. Of course, we have a hard time seeing
how the second group could say this since we belong to the first
group.
What is wrong with saying that inconsistent concepts of OVER
can both be correct? It may seem to entail that in and by them-
selves OVER-configurations are nothing definite; they are an
inchoate something which can be taken as anything. However,
all that a strict finitist means when he says that mutually incon-
sistent concepts of a kind of object may both be correct is that
both are correctly formed, viz., based on proof. Since he claims
that essences are invented, he can place the inconsistencies in
language; not in things. And he -will remind us that there is
nothing odd about there being inconsistencies in peoples' concept
of a thing because people do see things differently. So I do not
think that strict finitism's doctrine of essence leads to the absurd
metaphysics that things are nothing definite before we look at
them in a certain way. In Chapter III of my dissertation I in-
correctly attributed such a metaphysics to strict finitism and Witt-
genstein.
Of course, I have not defended strict finitism against attacks
on all of its philosophic implications; that task is beyond the
scope of this essay. In regard to strict finitism, the task of this
essay has been to bring it out of RFM so that it can be examined
philosophically.
14. In conclusion, I submit that in RFM Wittgenstein was
what I have called an open-ended strict finitist. We have seen the
inadequacies of such a philosophy of mathematics. It is not con-
clusively refuted but it is not clear that it can be fully developed.
So I cannot recommend Wittgenstein's philosophy of mathematics


Assessment of RFM
187
as the correct one. Nor can I recommend is as one likely to be
correct. The only reason I can give for trying to extend strict
finitism to all of mathematics is that it is such a pleasing account
of small finite cardinal aritmethic and simple geometry that if
one tries to work out the belief that it can be extended he will
come to understand how it can be extended.
